January evening, early seventies. The air inside the Academy of Music was thick with the scent of lilies and the hush of a thousand held breaths. Up on the stage, Christine Nilsson sang, her voice a crystalline instrument cutting through the silence. She was performing *Faust*, though the words were a fluid thing, shifting from German to Italian to suit the ears of the American crowd. *"M'ama... non m'ama..."* she cooed, sprinkling falling daisies with notes as clear as morning dew, before pressing the flower to her lips and lifting her eyes to the man playing the lover—a figure in purple velvet who looked far too serious for his artless role.

Newland Archer leaned against the velvet wall of the club box, watching her. He had arrived late, a deliberate choice born of both metropolitan etiquette and a personal desire to savor the anticipation. He had dined alone with his mother and sister, lingering over a cigar in the gloomy library where smoking was permitted, letting the moment build until it was exquisite. Now, as the prima donna sang her triumphant *"M'ama!"*, his gaze drifted from the stage to the opposite side of the house.

There, in the box of the Mingotts, sat the object of his thoughts. May Welland was there, a young girl in white with eyes fixed ecstatically on the lovers. A warm pink flush climbed her cheeks, mantling her brow and suffusing the slope of her breast, where a single gardenia pinned a modest tucker. Her white-gloved fingers touched the immense bouquet of lilies-of-the-valley on her lap. Newland felt a surge of satisfied vanity, a thrill of possession mingled with a tender reverence for her purity. He imagined them later, reading *Faust* together by the Italian lakes, heaping romance upon her abysmal innocence. He did not wish her to be a simpleton; he intended to refine her, to teach her the social tact necessary to navigate the world, to make her as worldly-wise as the married women he admired, yet without their frailties.

Beside him, Lawrence Lefferts shifted, his opera-glass snapping shut with a sharp click. Lefferts was the arbiter of "form" in New York, a man whose elegance was as innate as his knowledge of when to wear a black tie. "My God!" he murmured, handing the glass to the elderly gentleman beside him.

Newland followed the direction of Lefferts's stare. In the Mingott box, a new figure had appeared. It was a slim young woman, slightly shorter than May, with brown hair curled close to her temples and held by a narrow band of diamonds. She wore a dark blue velvet gown, caught theatrically under her bosom, giving her a distinct "Josephine" look. She stood for a moment, consulting with Mrs. Welland about seating arrangements, then smiled faintly and took her place in the row, settling herself between Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Welland.

Old Mr. Sillerton Jackson, the keeper of New York's family trees and its buried scandals, returned the glass. The entire box turned instinctively, waiting for his verdict. Jackson was a man of immense memory, knowing the tangled connections of the Thorleys and the Chiverses, the stinginess of the Leffertses, and the madness that plagued certain generations. He carried the weight of fifty years of secrets in his hollow temples. He looked at the newcomer, his filmy blue eyes scanning the group over his veined lids. He gave his moustache a thoughtful twist, a gesture that spoke volumes to the men around him.

"I didn't think the Mingotts would have tried it on," Jackson said simply.

The words hung in the air, heavy with implication. To the men in the box, it was a comment on propriety, on the breaking of unwritten rules. To Newland, it was a sudden, chilling disruption of the perfect picture he had held in his mind. The pristine world of the Mingotts, the fortress of their reputation, had just been breached by a stranger whose presence felt entirely out of place. The spell of the evening, the romantic haze of the opera, seemed to fracture instantly, replaced by the rigid, terrifying clarity of social consequence.

Newland Archer found himself trapped in a peculiar state of social vertigo. It was mortifying that the box commanding the undivided attention of the male half of New York should be occupied by his betrothed, seated between her mother and aunt. For a fleeting second, he failed to recognize the lady in the Empire gown, unable to fathom why her presence sparked such a tempest among the initiated. Then, clarity struck, accompanied by a sudden surge of indignation. Indeed, no one would have suspected the Mingotts of attempting such a maneuver.

Yet, they had. The hushed whispers drifting from the rear seats confirmed it beyond doubt: the young woman was May Welland's cousin, the relative universally whispered about as "poor Ellen Olenska." Newland knew she had arrived from Europe only days prior; he had even received a note from Miss Welland—delivered without disapproval—confirming that she had visited Ellen, who was currently residing with the elderly Mrs. Mingott. Newland possessed a generous heart; he admired the Mingott family's fierce loyalty to their few "black sheep" and believed that true nobility lay in kindness, even in private. However, receiving the Countess in the domestic sphere was one thing, but parading her publicly at the Opera, specifically in the very box where his own engagement to May was to be announced within weeks, was a different matter entirely. He felt exactly as Old Sillerton Jackson did: he could not believe the Mingotts would dare.

Of course, Newland knew that whatever a man dared within the bounds of Fifth Avenue, Old Mrs. Manson Mingott, the matriarch, would dare. He had always held her in high regard. Despite her origins as Catherine Spicer of Staten Island—a name marred by a father mysteriously discredited and lacking the wealth or status to erase the stigma—she had allied herself with the head of the Mingott fortune. She had married two daughters to "foreigners," an Italian marquis and an English banker, and capped her audacity by constructing a vast house of pale cream-colored stone in an inaccessible wilderness near Central Park, defying the era's preference for brown sandstone.

Her foreign daughters had become legends, never returning to visit their mother. Sedentary and corpulent in her habits, the old lady had philosophically remained at home, ruling from her cream-colored palace—a structure rumored to be modeled after Parisian aristocratic hotels—as if living above Thirty-fourth Street or possessing French doors that swung open rather than sliding up were entirely normal. Everyone, including Mr. Sillerton Jackson, agreed that Old Catherine lacked beauty, a gift that in New York justified every triumph and excused every failure. Uncharitable observers claimed she had risen through sheer will and a hardness of heart, a haughty effrontery somehow sanctified by the extreme decency of her private life. Her husband, Mr. Manson Mingott, had died young, leaving her with a fortune tied up with the caution of the Spicers, but his bold widow had gone forth fearlessly. She mingled in foreign circles, married her daughters into questionable fashion, dined with Dukes and Ambassadors, and befriended Papists and opera singers alike. Yet, as Sillerton Jackson was the first to proclaim, her reputation remained untarnished, the only distinction being her lack of the earlier Catherine's beauty.

Mrs. Mingott had successfully untied her husband's fortune and lived in affluence for half a century, yet memories of her early struggles rendered her excessively thrifty. While she insisted on the finest fabrics and furniture, she refused to spend lavishly on transient pleasures. Consequently, her table was as meager as Mrs. Archer's, and her wines offered no redemption. Her relatives deemed this penury a discredit to the Mingott name, traditionally synonymous with fine living, yet guests continued to flock to her despite the "made dishes" and flat champagne. In response to her son Lovell's attempts to restore family credit by hiring the city's best chef, she would laugh, "What's the use of two good cooks in one family, now that I've married the girls and can't eat sauces?"

As Newland mused on these complexities, he turned his gaze back to the Mingott box. He observed that Mrs. Welland and her sister-in-law faced their semicircle of critics with the Mingottian *aplomb* instilled by Old Catherine, though May Welland betrayed a heightened flush—perhaps because she knew he was watching her—with a sense of the situation's gravity. As for the cause of the commotion, she sat gracefully in her corner, eyes fixed on the stage, revealing, as she leaned forward, a glimpse of shoulder and bosom that New York was not accustomed to seeing in ladies who wished to remain unnoticed.

To Newland, nothing seemed more abhorrent than an offense against "Taste," that distant divinity whose mere visible representative was "Form." Madame Olenska's pale, serious face seemed appropriate to her tragic situation, but the way her dress, devoid of a tucker, sloped away from her thin shoulders shocked and troubled him. He dreaded the thought of May Welland being influenced by a young woman so careless of the dictates of Taste.

"After all," he heard a younger man begin behind him as the Mephistopheles-and-Martha scenes unfolded, "just *what* happened?"

"Well—she left him; nobody attempts to deny that."

"He's an awful brute, isn't he?" continued the young inquirer, a candid Thorley, clearly preparing to champion the lady's cause.

"The very worst; I knew him at Nice," said Lawrence Lefferts with authority. "A half-paralysed white sneering fellow—rather handsome head, but eyes with a lot of lashes. Well, I'll tell you the sort: when he wasn't with women he was collecting china. Paying any price for both, I understand."

A general laugh rippled through the box, and the young champion pressed, "Well, then----?"

"Well, then; she bolted with his secretary."

"Oh, I see." The champion's face fell.

"It didn't last long, though: I heard of her a few months later living alone in Venice. I believe Lovell Mingott went out to get her. He said she was desperately unhappy. That's all right—but this parading her at the Opera's another thing."

"Perhaps," young Thorley hazarded, "she's too unhappy to be left at home."

This was greeted with an irreverent laugh, and the youth blushed deeply, trying to feign an air of having intended a "_double entendre_."

"Well—it's queer to have brought Miss Welland, anyhow," someone murmured in a low tone, casting a sidelong glance at Archer.

"Oh, that's part of the campaign: Granny's orders, no doubt," Lefferts laughed. "When the old lady does a thing, she does it thoroughly."

The act was concluding, and a general stir erupted in the box. Suddenly, Newland Archer felt compelled to decisive action. The desire to be the first to enter Mrs. Mingott's box, to proclaim to the waiting world his engagement to May Welland, and to stand by her through whatever difficulties her cousin's anomalous situation might entail, had abruptly overruled all scruples and hesitations. He hurried through the red corridors to the farther side of the house.

Upon entering the box, his eyes met Miss Welland's. She had instantly understood his motive, though the family dignity both considered a supreme virtue prevented her from voicing it. The inhabitants of their world lived in an atmosphere of faint implications and pale delicacies, and the fact that they understood each other without a word seemed to draw them closer than any explanation could. Her eyes said, "You see why Mamma brought me," and his answered, "I would not for the world have had you stay away."

"You know my niece Countess Olenska?" Mrs. Welland enquired as she shook hands with her future son-in-law. Archer bowed without extending his hand, adhering to the custom of introduction to a lady; Ellen Olenska bent her head slightly, keeping her pale-gloved hands clasped on her huge fan of eagle feathers. After greeting Mrs. Lovell Mingott, a large blonde lady in creaking satin, Newland sat beside his betrothed. In a low tone, he said, "I hope you've told Madame Olenska that we're engaged? I want everybody to know—I want you to let me announce it this evening at the ball."

Miss Welland's face flushed like the dawn, and she looked at him with radiant eyes. "If you can persuade Mamma," she said. "But why should we change what is already settled?" He offered no verbal answer, only the silent affirmation of his gaze, and she added, smiling more confidently, "Tell my cousin yourself: I give you leave. She says she used to play with you when you were children."

She made way for him by pushing back her chair, and promptly, with a touch of ostentation designed for the whole house to witness his action, Newland seated himself at the Countess Olenska's side.

"We *did* use to play together, didn't we?" she asked, turning her grave eyes to his. "You were a horrid boy, and kissed me once behind a door; but it was your cousin Vandie Newland, who never looked at me, that I was in love with." Her glance swept the horseshoe curve of boxes. "Ah, how this brings it all back to me—I see everybody here in knickerbockers and pantalettes," she said, her slightly foreign accent trailing softly, her eyes returning to his face.

Though agreeable, the young man was shocked that they should reflect such an unseemly picture of the august tribunal before which, at that very moment, her case was being tried. Nothing could be in worse taste than misplaced flippancy. He answered somewhat stiffly, "Yes, you have been away a very long time."

"Oh, centuries and centuries; so long," she replied, "that I'm sure I'm dead and buried, and this dear old place is heaven;" which, for reasons he could not define, struck Newland Archer as an even more disrespectful way of describing New York society.

It invariably happened in the same way.

On the night of her annual ball, Mrs. Julius Beaufort never failed to appear at the Opera. In fact, she deliberately scheduled her grandest evening event on an Opera night, a strategic move designed to signal her total detachment from domestic drudgery and her command of a staff capable of orchestrating such a spectacle in her absence.

The Beaufort residence was one of the few houses in New York boasting a true ballroom—a luxury that predated even the mansions of the Mingotts and the Headly Chiverses. While the city was beginning to view the practice of covering a drawing-room floor with a "crash" and moving furniture upstairs as somewhat provincial, the Beauforts maintained a dedicated space for dancing. For three hundred and sixty-four days of the year, this room remained shut away in darkness, its gilt chairs stacked in corners and its chandelier covered in a protective bag. This undeniable architectural superiority was deemed sufficient compensation for any regrets regarding the Beaufort past.

Mrs. Archer, who loved to distill her social observations into axioms, once remarked, "We all have our pet common people." Though the phrase was bold, its truth was quietly acknowledged in many exclusive hearts. Yet, the Beauforts were not merely common; some whispered they were worse. Mrs. Beaufort hailed from one of America's most revered families; she was the lovely Regina Dallas of the South Carolina branch, a penniless beauty introduced to New York society by her cousin, the imprudent Medora Manson, who was always doing the wrong thing for the right reasons. Being related to the Mansons and the Rushworths granted one a *droit de cité* in New York society, as Mr. Sillerton Jackson, a frequent visitor to the Tuileries, termed it. However, did one not forfeit that privilege by marrying Julius Beaufort?

The question remained: who was Beaufort? He passed for an Englishman; he was agreeable, handsome, ill-tempered, hospitable, and witty. He arrived in America with letters of recommendation from Old Mrs. Manson Mingott's English son-in-law, a banker, and quickly secured an influential position in the world of affairs. Yet, his habits were dissipated, his tongue bitter, and his origins mysterious. When Medora Manson announced her cousin's engagement to him, it was viewed as another act of folly in her long history of imprudence.

But folly is often justified as surely as wisdom. Two years after young Mrs. Beaufort's marriage, it was admitted that she presided over the most distinguished house in New York. No one knew exactly how this miracle was achieved. She was indolent, passive, and even the caustic called her dull. Yet, dressed like an idol and draped in pearls, she grew younger, blonder, and more beautiful with each passing year. She reigned from her heavy brownstone palace, drawing the entire world within its walls without lifting a jeweled finger. The knowing ones whispered that it was Beaufort himself who trained the servants, instructed the chef on new dishes, directed the gardeners on which hot-house flowers to cultivate for the dinner table, selected the guests, brewed the after-dinner punch, and dictated the intimate notes his wife sent to her friends. If he did these things, they were performed in secret, presenting to the public the image of a careless, hospitable millionaire strolling into his own drawing-room with the detached air of an invited guest, remarking, "My wife's gloxinias are a marvel, aren't they? I believe she gets them out from Kew."

New York was agreed that Mr. Beaufort's secret lay in his ability to carry things off. It was perfectly acceptable to whisper that he had been "helped" to leave England by the international banking house where he had worked; he dismissed that rumor as easily as the rest. Though New York's business conscience was as sensitive as its moral standards, he swept everything before him, dragging the entire city into his drawing rooms. For over twenty years, people had said they were "going to the Beauforts'" with the same sense of security as if they were visiting Mrs. Manson Mingott's, with the added satisfaction of knowing they would receive hot canvas-back ducks and vintage wines rather than tepid Veuve Clicquot without a year and warmed-up croquettes from Philadelphia.

Thus, as usual, Mrs. Beaufort appeared in her box just before the Jewel Song. And when, as was customary, she rose at the end of the third act, wrapped her opera cloak around her lovely shoulders, and vanished, New York knew that the ball would begin within the hour.

The Beaufort house was one that New Yorkers were proud to display to foreigners, particularly on the night of the annual ball. The Beauforts had been among the first in New York to own their own red velvet carpet, having it rolled down the steps by their own footmen under their own awning, rather than renting it along with the supper and ballroom chairs. They also pioneered the custom of allowing ladies to remove their cloaks in the hall, instead of shuffling up to the hostess's bedroom to recurl their hair with the aid of a gas burner; Beaufort was understood to have remarked that he supposed all his wife's friends had maids who ensured they were properly *coiffées* before leaving home.

Furthermore, the house was boldly planned with a ballroom, so that instead of squeezing through a narrow passage to reach it—as at the Chiverses—one marched solemnly down a vista of enfiladed drawing rooms (the sea-green, the crimson, and the *bouton d'or*), seeing from afar the many-candled lustres reflected in the polished parquetry, and beyond that, the depths of a conservatory where camellias and tree-ferns arched their costly foliage over seats of black and gold bamboo.

Newland Archer, fitting for a young man of his station, strolled in somewhat late. He had left his overcoat with the silk-stockinged footmen—the stockings being one of Beaufort's few eccentricities—and dawdled for a while in the library, hung with Spanish leather and furnished with Buhl and malachite, where a few men were chatting and putting on their dancing gloves. Finally, he joined the line of guests whom Mrs. Beaufort was receiving on the threshold of the crimson drawing room.

Archer was distinctly nervous. He had not returned to his club after the Opera, as the young bloods usually did, but, the night being fine, had walked a considerable distance up Fifth Avenue before turning back toward the Beauforts' house. He was deeply afraid that the Mingotts might be going too far; that, in fact, Granny Mingott's orders might have summoned the Countess Olenska to the ball.

From the tone of the conversation in the club box, he had perceived how grave a mistake that would be. Although he was more than ever determined to "see the thing through," he felt less chivalrously eager to champion his betrothed's cousin than during their brief talk at the Opera.

Wandering into the *bouton d'or* drawing room—where Beaufort had had the audacity to hang "Love Victorious," the much-discussed nude by Bouguereau—Archer found Mrs. Welland and her daughter standing near the ballroom door. Couples were already gliding over the floor beyond: the light of the wax candles fell upon revolving tulle skirts, upon girlish heads wreathed with modest blossoms, upon the dashing aigrettes and ornaments of the young married women's coiffures, and upon the glitter of highly glazed shirt-fronts and fresh *glacé* gloves.

Miss Welland, evidently about to join the dancers, hung on the threshold, holding her lilies-of-the-valley (she carried no other bouquet), her face a little pale, her eyes burning with candid excitement. A group of young men and girls were gathered about her, engaging in much hand-clasping, laughter, and pleasantry, while Mrs. Welland, standing slightly apart, shed the beam of a qualified approval. It was evident that Miss Welland was in the act of announcing her engagement, while her mother affected the air of parental reluctance deemed suitable for the occasion.

Archer paused for a moment. It was at his express wish that the announcement had been made, yet it was not how he would have wished his happiness to be known. To proclaim it in the heat and noise of a crowded ballroom was to rob it of the fine bloom of privacy which should belong to things nearest the heart. His joy was so deep that this blurring of the surface left its essence untouched; but he would have liked to keep the surface pure as well. It was a certain satisfaction to find that May Welland shared this feeling. Her eyes fled to his beseechingly, and their look said: "Remember, we're doing this because it's right."

No appeal could have found a more immediate response in Archer's breast; yet he wished that the necessity of their action had been represented by some ideal reason, and not simply by poor Ellen Olenska. The group surrounding Miss Welland made way for him with significant smiles, and after taking his share of the felicitations, he drew his betrothed into the middle of the ballroom floor and put his arm about her waist.

"Now we shan't have to talk," he said, smiling into her candid eyes as they floated away on the soft waves of the Blue Danube.

She made no answer. Her lips trembled into a smile, but the eyes remained distant and serious, as if bent on some ineffable vision. "Dear," Archer whispered, pressing her closer; it dawned on him that the first hours of being engaged, even if spent in a ballroom, held something grave and sacramental. What a new life it was going to be, with this whiteness, radiance, and goodness at one's side!

Once the dance was over, the two, as became an affianced couple, wandered into the conservatory. Sitting behind a tall screen of tree-ferns and camellias, Newland pressed her gloved hand to his lips.

"You see I did as you asked me to," she said.

"Yes; I couldn't wait," he answered, smiling. After a moment, he added, "Only I wish it hadn't had to be at a ball."

"Yes, I know." She met his glance comprehendingly. "But after all—even here we're alone together, aren't we?"

"Oh, dearest—always!" Archer cried.

Evidently, she was always going to understand; she was always going to say the right thing. The discovery made the cup of his bliss overflow, and he went on gaily, "The worst of it is that I want to kiss you and I can't." As he spoke, he took a swift glance about the conservatory, assured himself of their momentary privacy, and catching her to him, laid a fugitive pressure on her lips. To counteract the audacity of this proceeding, he led her to a bamboo sofa in a less secluded part of the conservatory. Sitting down beside her, he broke a lily-of-the-valley from her bouquet. She sat silent, and the world lay like a sunlit valley at their feet.

"Did you tell my cousin Ellen?" she asked presently, as if she spoke through a dream.

He roused himself and remembered that he had not done so. Some invincible repugnance to speak of such things to the strange foreign woman had checked the words on his lips.

"No—I hadn't the chance after all," he said, fibbing hastily.

"Ah." She looked disappointed but gently resolved on gaining her point. "You must, then, for I didn't either; and I shouldn't like her to think—"

"Of course not. But aren't you, after all, the person to do it?"

She pondered on this. "If I'd done it at the right time, yes; but now that there's been a delay, I think you must explain that I'd asked you to tell her at the Opera, before our speaking about it to everybody here. Otherwise she might think I had forgotten her. You see, she's one of the family, and she's been away so long that she's rather—sensitive."

Archer looked at her glowingly. "Dear and great angel! Of course I'll tell her." He glanced a trifle apprehensively toward the crowded ballroom. "But I haven't seen her yet. Has she come?"

"No; at the last minute she decided not to."

"At the last minute?" he echoed, betraying his surprise that she should ever have considered the alternative possible.

"Yes. She's awfully fond of dancing," the young girl answered simply. "But suddenly she made up her mind that her dress wasn't smart enough for a ball, though we thought it so lovely; and so my aunt had to take her home."

"Oh, well—" said Archer with happy indifference. Nothing about his betrothed pleased him more than her resolute determination to carry to its utmost limit that ritual of ignoring the "unpleasant" in which they had both been brought up.

"She knows as well as I do," he reflected, "the real reason of her cousin's staying away; but I shall never let her see by the least sign that I am conscious of there being a shadow of a shade on poor Ellen Olenska's reputation."

The following day, the rigid rituals of New York betrothal took hold with their customary precision. Newland Archer, accompanied first by his mother and sister to pay respects to Mrs. Welland, then joined her and May for a carriage ride to the ancestral home of Mrs. Manson Mingott, where the matriarch's blessing was the final seal on their union.

For a young man of Archer's standing, a visit to Mrs. Mingott was invariably a spectacle of amusement. Her residence, though historically significant, lacked the austere, grim elegance of the older Fifth Avenue mansions with their rosewood consoles and mahogany bookcases. Instead, Mrs. Mingott had embraced the frivolous opulence of the Second Empire, mixing her family's heirlooms with lighter upholstery. She preferred to sit in a ground-floor window, observing the world with a calm, unshakeable confidence. She believed the chaotic sprawl of the city—the quarries, the one-story saloons, the ragged gardens—would inevitably yield to grander residences like hers, and the clatter of omnibuses would be silenced by the smooth asphalt of Paris. Since everyone she wished to see came to her, she felt no isolation despite her location.

Time had transformed Mrs. Mingott into a natural phenomenon. What was once a plump, active woman had become a vast, august figure, accepting her physical expansion with the same philosophy she applied to life's other trials. Now, in extreme old age, she presented a mirror image of firm, pink flesh, save for the faint traces of a small face waiting to be excavated. Smooth double chins led down to a snowy bosom draped in muslins pinned by a miniature portrait of the late Mr. Mingott, while waves of black silk cascaded over her armchair, framing two tiny white hands that rested like gulls upon the billows.

Her unique arrangement of living quarters defied every New York propriety. Unable to climb stairs, she had moved her reception rooms to the ground floor, leaving the bedrooms upstairs. To a guest seated with her, the open door revealed a startling vista: a bedroom with a low, sofa-like bed and a toilet table adorned with lace, creating an atmosphere reminiscent of French fiction and architectural invitations to immorality. It amused Archer to imagine Mrs. Mingott leading a blameless life within a stage-set designed for adultery; he privately conceded that if a lover had been what she desired, the intrepid woman would certainly have secured him.

Fortunately, the Countess Olenska was absent during the betrothed couple's visit. Mrs. Mingott claimed she had gone out—a seemingly indiscreet move for a woman of her compromised past on a bright, busy shopping day—but her absence spared the young couple the embarrassment of her shadow falling upon their radiant future. The visit proceeded successfully. Mrs. Mingott delighted in the engagement, which had been carefully vetted by family councils, and admired the engagement ring: a large, thick sapphire set in invisible claws.

"It is the new setting," Mrs. Welland had explained, glancing conciliatorily at her future son-in-law. "It shows the stone beautifully, though it looks a bit bare to old-fashioned eyes."

"Old-fashioned eyes? I hope you do not mean mine, my dear? I adore all novelties," the ancestress replied, lifting the stone to her bright, unassisted eyes. "Very handsome. Very liberal. In my time, a cameo set in pearls sufficed. But it is the hand that sets off the ring, is it not, Mr. Archer?" She waved a tiny hand, its fingers tipped with pointed nails and rolls of aged fat encircling the wrist like ivory bracelets. "Mine was modeled in Rome by the great Ferrigiani. You should have May's done; no doubt he will. Her hand is large—these modern sports spread the joints—but the skin is white. And when is the wedding to be?" she asked, fixing her gaze on Archer.

"As soon as possible, if only you will back me up, Mrs. Mingott," Archer smiled at his betrothed.

"We must give them time to know each other better, mamma," Mrs. Welland interjected with practiced reluctance.

"Know each other? Fiddlesticks! Everyone in New York knows everyone. Let the young man have his way; do not wait until the bubble bursts on the wine. Marry them before Lent; I may catch pneumonia this winter, and I wish to host the wedding breakfast," the ancestress declared.

The room filled with expressions of amusement and gratitude as the visit wound down in mild pleasantry. Then, the door opened to admit the Countess Olenska, wearing bonnet and mantle, followed unexpectedly by Julius Beaufort.

A cousinly murmur of pleasure rippled through the ladies. Mrs. Mingott extended Ferrigiani's model to the banker. "Ha! Beaufort, this is a rare favor!" she exclaimed, using her odd foreign custom of addressing men by surname.

"Thanks. I wish it might happen oftener," the visitor replied in his easy arrogance. "I am generally so tied down, but I met the Countess Ellen in Madison Square, and she was kind enough to let me walk home with her."

"Ah—I hope the house will be gayer now that Ellen is here!" Mrs. Mingott cried with glorious effrontery. "Sit down, Beaufort; push up the yellow armchair. Now that I have you, I require a good gossip. I heard your ball was magnificent, and I understand you invited Mrs. Lemuel Struthers? Well, I have a curiosity to see the woman myself."

She had forgotten her relatives, who drifted out into the hall under Ellen's guidance. Mrs. Mingott, who always professed admiration for Julius Beaufort due to their shared cool dominance and shortcuts through convention, was eager to learn why the Beauforts had invited Mrs. Struthers for the first time. The widow of Struthers's Shoe-polish had returned from a European sojourn to besiege New York's tight little citadel. "Of course, if you and Regina invite her, the matter is settled. We need new blood and new money—and I hear she is still very good-looking," the carnivorous old lady declared.

In the hall, as Mrs. Welland and May donned their furs, Archer noticed the Countess Olenska looking at him with a faintly questioning smile.

"Of course you know already—about May and me," he said, answering her look with a shy laugh. "She scolded me for not giving you the news last night at the Opera; I had her orders to tell you we were engaged, but the crowd was too great."

The smile traveled from the Countess's eyes to her lips; she looked younger, more like the bold brown Ellen Mingott of his boyhood. "Of course I know; yes. And I am so glad. But one does not announce such things first in a crowd." The ladies stood on the threshold, and she held out her hand.

"Good-bye; come and see me some day," she said, her gaze lingering on Archer.

In the carriage, descending Fifth Avenue, they spoke pointedly of Mrs. Mingott—her age, her spirit, her wonderful attributes. No one mentioned the Countess Olenska. Yet Archer knew Mrs. Welland was thinking: *It is a mistake for Ellen to be seen, the very day after her arrival, parading up Fifth Avenue at the crowded hour with Julius Beaufort.* And the young man himself mentally added: *And she ought to know that a man who is just engaged does not spend his time calling on married women. But I daresay in the set she has lived in, they do—they never do anything else.* Despite his pride in his cosmopolitan views, he thanked heaven that he was a New Yorker, about to ally himself with one of his own kind.

The following evening, Old Mr. Sillerton Jackson arrived to dine with the Archers, bringing with him the air of a man who had spent his life cataloging the secrets of society. Mrs. Archer, a woman of quiet reserve who shrank from the clamor of social gatherings yet craved their latest whispers, was his perfect hostess. She possessed a rare talent for gathering intelligence, and her daughter, Janey, served as an excellent audience, filling in the gaps of her father's meticulous observations with minor gossip. Whenever Mrs. Archer desired to know what was happening in the world, she extended her invitation to Mr. Jackson; since she honored so few with such requests, and since the two women made such delightful listeners, he invariably came himself rather than sending his sister.

If Mr. Jackson could dictate the terms of his own existence, he would have chosen evenings when Newland was absent. Not because the young man was uncongenial—the two men were fast friends at their club—but because the old anecdotist sometimes sensed a tendency in Newland to weigh his evidence too heavily before sharing it with the ladies. Furthermore, he would have preferred a dinner where the food was less austere. Yet, New York, as far back as memory could trace, was divided into two great camps: the Mingotts and Mansons, who prized fine eating, fashionable clothes, and money, and the Archer-Newland-van der Luyden tribe, who were devoted to travel, horticulture, and high literature, regarding the grosser pleasures of the table with disdain. One could not have everything. At the Lovell Mingotts', one enjoyed canvas-back ducks and vintage wines; at Adeline Archer's, one could discuss Alpine scenery and *The Marble Faun*, the Madeira having long ago circled the Cape. Thus, when summoned by Mrs. Archer, Mr. Jackson, a true eclectic, would tell his sister, "I've been a little gouty since my last dinner at the Lovell Mingotts'; it will do me good to diet at Adeline's."

Mrs. Archer, a widow for many years, lived with her son and daughter in West Twenty-eighth Street. An upper floor was dedicated to Newland, while the two women squeezed into narrower quarters below. In an unclouded harmony of tastes, they cultivated ferns in Wardian cases, crafted macramé lace and wool embroidery, collected American Revolutionary glazed ware, subscribed to *Good Words*, and read Ouida's novels for their Italian atmosphere—preferring tales of peasant life for their scenic descriptions and gentle sentiments, though they generally favored stories of society where motives were comprehensible. They spoke severely of Dickens for failing to draw a gentleman and considered Thackeray less at home in the great world than Bulwer, who was already beginning to seem old-fashioned.

Both Mrs. and Miss Archer were great lovers of scenery, the principal object of their travels abroad, regarding architecture and painting as subjects for men and learned scholars. Mrs. Archer had been born a Newland, and mother and daughter, who were as alike as sisters, were both, as people said, "true Newlands." They were tall, pale, and slightly round-shouldered, with long noses, sweet smiles, and a kind of drooping distinction reminiscent of faded Reynolds portraits. Their physical resemblance would have been complete had not an elderly embonpoint stretched Mrs. Archer's black brocade, while Miss Archer's brown and purple poplins hung increasingly slackly on her virgin frame.

Mentally, however, the likeness was less complete than their identical mannerisms suggested. The long habit of living in mutually dependent intimacy had given them the same vocabulary and the same habit of prefacing opinions with "Mother thinks" or "Janey thinks," depending on who wished to advance the view. In reality, while Mrs. Archer's serene unimaginativeness rested easily in the accepted and familiar, Janey was subject to sudden aberrations of fancy welling up from springs of suppressed romance.

Mother and daughter adored each other and revered their son and brother. Archer loved them with a tenderness made compunctious and uncritical by the sense of their exaggerated admiration, and by his secret satisfaction in it. He believed it a good thing for a man to have his authority respected in his own house, even if his sense of humor sometimes led him to question the force of his mandate.

On this occasion, the young man was very sure that Mr. Jackson would have preferred to dine elsewhere, yet he had his own reasons for staying home. Of course, old Jackson wanted to talk about Ellen Olenska, and of course Mrs. Archer and Janey wanted to hear what he had to say. All three would be slightly embarrassed by Newland's presence now that his prospective relation to the Mingott clan had been made known. The young man waited with amused curiosity to see how they would navigate this difficulty.

They began, obliquely, by discussing Mrs. Lemuel Struthers.

"It's a pity the Beauforts asked her," Mrs. Archer said gently. "But then Regina always does what he tells her; and *Beaufort*..."

"Certain *nuances* escape Beaufort," said Mr. Jackson, cautiously inspecting the broiled shad and wondering for the thousandth time why Mrs. Archer's cook always burnt the roe to a cinder—a wonder Newland had long shared, always detecting it in the older man's expression of melancholy disapproval.

"Oh, necessarily; Beaufort is a vulgar man," said Mrs. Archer. "My grandfather Newland always used to say to my mother: 'Whatever you do, don't let that fellow Beaufort be introduced to the girls.' But at least he's had the advantage of associating with gentlemen; in England too, they say. It's all very mysterious..." She glanced at Janey and paused. She and Janey knew every fold of the Beaufort mystery, but in public, Mrs. Archer continued to assume the subject was not one for the unmarried.

"But this Mrs. Struthers," Mrs. Archer continued, "what did you say *she* was, Sillerton?"

"Out of a mine: or rather out of the saloon at the head of the pit. Then with Living Wax-Works, touring New England. After the police broke *that* up, they say she lived..." Mr. Jackson glanced at Janey, whose eyes began to bulge from under her prominent lids. There were still hiatuses for her in Mrs. Struthers's past.

"Then," Mr. Jackson continued—and Archer saw he was wondering why no one had told the butler never to slice cucumbers with a steel knife—"then Lemuel Struthers came along. They say his advertiser used the girl's head for the shoe-polish posters; her hair's intensely black, you know—the Egyptian style. Anyhow, he... eventually... married her." There were volumes of innuendo in the way the "eventually" was spaced, and each syllable given its due stress.

"Oh, well—at the pass we've come to nowadays, it doesn't matter," said Mrs. Archer indifferently. The ladies were not really interested in Mrs. Struthers just then; the subject of Ellen Olenska was too fresh and too absorbing. Indeed, Mrs. Struthers's name had been introduced only so that Mrs. Archer might presently be able to say: "And Newland's new cousin—Countess Olenska? Was *she* at the ball too?"

There was a faint touch of sarcasm in the reference to her son, and Archer knew it and had expected it. Even Mrs. Archer, who was seldom unduly pleased with human events, had been altogether glad of her son's engagement. "Especially after that silly business with Mrs. Rushworth," as she had remarked to Janey, alluding to what had once seemed to Newland a tragedy of which his soul would always bear the scar.

There was no better match in New York than May Welland; look at the question from whatever point you chose. Of course such a marriage was only what Newland was entitled to, but young men were so foolish and incalculable—and some women so ensnaring and unscrupulous—that it was nothing short of a miracle to see one's only son safe past the Siren Isle and in the haven of a blameless domesticity.

All this Mrs. Archer felt, and her son knew she felt; but he knew also that she had been perturbed by the premature announcement of his engagement, or rather by its cause. It was for that reason—because on the whole he was a tender and indulgent master—that he had stayed home that evening. "It's not that I don't approve of the Mingotts' *esprit de corps*; but why Newland's engagement should be mixed up with that Olenska woman's comings and goings I don't see," Mrs. Archer grumbled to Janey, the only witness of her slight lapses from perfect sweetness.

She had behaved beautifully—and in beautiful behavior she was unsurpassed—during the call on Mrs. Welland; but Newland knew (and his betrothed doubtless guessed) that all through the visit she and Janey were nervously on the watch for Madame Olenska's possible intrusion. And when they left the house together, she had permitted herself to say to her son: "I'm thankful that Augusta Welland received us alone."

These indications of inward disturbance moved Archer the more that he too felt the Mingotts had gone a little too far. But, as it was against all the rules of their code that the mother and son should ever allude to what was uppermost in their thoughts, he simply replied: "Oh, well, there's always a phase of family parties to be gone through when one gets engaged, and the sooner it's over the better." At which his mother merely pursed her lips under the lace veil that hung down from her grey velvet bonnet trimmed with frosted grapes.

Her revenge, he felt—her lawful revenge—would be to "draw" Mr. Jackson that evening on the Countess Olenska. Having publicly done his duty as a future member of the Mingott clan, the young man had no objection to hearing the lady discussed in private, except that the subject was already beginning to bore him.

Mr. Jackson had helped himself to a slice of the tepid filet which the mournful butler had handed him with a look as skeptical as his own, and had rejected the mushroom sauce after a scarcely perceptible sniff. He looked baffled and hungry, and Archer reflected that he would probably finish his meal on Ellen Olenska.

Mr. Jackson leaned back in his chair and glanced up at the candlelit Archers, Newlands, and van der Luydens hanging in dark frames on the dark walls.

"Ah, how your grandfather Archer loved a good dinner, my dear Newland!" he said, his eyes on the portrait of a plump, full-chested young man in a stock and a blue coat, with a view of a white-columned country house behind him. "Well—well—well... I wonder what he would have said to all these foreign marriages!"

Mrs. Archer ignored the allusion to the ancestral cuisine, and Mr. Jackson continued with deliberation: "No, she was *not* at the ball."

"Ah—" Mrs. Archer murmured, in a tone that implied: "She had that decency."

"Perhaps the Beauforts don't know her," Janey suggested, with her artless malice.

Mr. Jackson gave a faint sip, as if he had been tasting invisible Madeira. "Mrs. Beaufort may not—but Beaufort certainly does, for she was seen walking up Fifth Avenue this afternoon with him by the whole of New York."

"Mercy—" moaned Mrs. Archer, evidently perceiving the uselessness of trying to ascribe the actions of foreigners to a sense of delicacy.

"I wonder if she wears a round hat or a bonnet in the afternoon," Janey speculated. "At the Opera I know she had on dark blue velvet, perfectly plain and flat—like a night-gown."

"Janey!" said her mother; and Miss Archer blushed and tried to look audacious.

"It was, at any rate, in better taste not to go to the ball," Mrs. Archer continued.

A spirit of perversity moved her son to rejoin: "I don't think it was a question of taste with her. May said she meant to go, and then decided that the dress in question wasn't smart enough."

Mrs. Archer smiled at this confirmation of her inference. "Poor Ellen," she simply remarked, adding compassionately: "We must always bear in mind what an eccentric bringing-up Medora Manson gave her. What can you expect of a girl who was allowed to wear black satin at her coming-out ball?"

"Ah—don't I remember her in it!" said Mr. Jackson; adding: "Poor girl!" in the tone of one who, while enjoying the memory, had fully understood at the time what the sight portended.

"It's odd," Janey remarked, "that she should have kept such an ugly name as Ellen. I should have changed it to Elaine." She glanced about the table to see the effect of this.

Her brother laughed. "Why Elaine?"

"I don't know; it sounds more—more Polish," said Janey, blushing.

"It sounds more conspicuous; and that can hardly be what she wishes," said Mrs. Archer distantly.

"Why not?" broke in her son, growing suddenly argumentative. "Why shouldn't she be conspicuous if she chooses? Why should she slink about as if it were she who had disgraced herself? She's 'poor Ellen' certainly, because she had the bad luck to make a wretched marriage; but I don't see that that's a reason for hiding her head as if she were the culprit."

"That, I suppose," said Mr. Jackson, speculatively, "is the line the Mingotts mean to take."

The young man reddened. "I didn't have to wait for their cue, if that's what you mean, sir. Madame Olenska has had an unhappy life: that doesn't make her an outcast."

"There are rumours," began Mr. Jackson, glancing at Janey.

"Oh, I know: the secretary," the young man took him up. "Nonsense, mother; Janey's grown-up. They say, don't they," he went on, "that the secretary helped her to get away from her brute of a husband,

That evening, once Mr. Jackson had retreated and the ladies had withdrawn to their chintz-draped sanctuary, Newland Archer climbed the stairs to his study with a thoughtful heaviness. A faithful hand had tended the fire and trimmed the lamp, transforming the room—lined with books, adorned with bronze fencers, and filled with reproductions of masterpieces—into a haven of singular comfort.

As he sank into his armchair before the blaze, his gaze settled upon a large portrait of May Welland. It was a gift from her early in their courtship, now the sole occupant of the table's center. With a sudden, reverent awe, he studied the frankness of her brow, the seriousness of her eyes, and the cheerful innocence of her mouth. The girl whose soul he was destined to guard stared back at him, yet through May's familiar face, he saw something terrifying: a stranger born of the very social system he revered. This young creature, knowing nothing and expecting everything, made him realize that marriage was not the safe harbor his upbringing had promised, but a voyage into uncharted waters.

The shadow of the Countess Olenska had already begun to unsettle his convictions. His own declaration—"Women should be free, as free as we are"—echoed in his mind, challenging a truth his world insisted did not exist. In polite society, "nice" women, no matter how wronged, never demanded such liberty; thus, men of his generation could feign chivalry without consequence. Yet here he stood, pledged to defend the conduct of his betrothed's cousin, behavior that, if committed by his own wife, would invite the full wrath of Church and State. While the dilemma seemed purely hypothetical, Newland knew his imagination well enough to fear that the bond between him and May might tighten painfully. What did they truly know of one another? His duty as a "decent" man required him to hide his past, and hers, as a marriageable girl, left her with no past to reveal. He worried that beneath the surface, they might tire of each other, misunderstand, or irritate. Reviewing the marriages of his peers, he found none that mirrored the passionate comradeship he envisioned. Instead, he saw dull alliances of material interest, held together by ignorance on one side and hypocrisy on the other. Lawrence Lefferts epitomized this ideal; a high priest of form who had molded his wife so perfectly to his convenience that she remained blissfully unaware of his affairs with other men's wives, blushing indignantly only when reminded of Julius Beaufort's "other establishment."

Archer tried to comfort himself with the notion that he was not quite so foolish as Larry Lefferts, nor May so simple as Gertrude, but the distinction lay merely in intelligence, not in moral standards. They all inhabited a world of hieroglyphs, where reality was never spoken or acted upon, but only represented by arbitrary signs. Just as Mrs. Welland, fully aware of why Archer had pushed for the engagement announcement, felt compelled to simulate reluctance and pretend she had been forced into it, so too did the young girl at the center of this elaborate charade remain inscrutable. She was frank because she had nothing to hide, and assured because she knew nothing to fear. With no preparation for the harsh realities of life, she was about to be plunged into them.

He was sincerely, placidly in love. He adored her radiant beauty, her health, her horsemanship, and the shy curiosity she was developing under his guidance. She was straightforward, loyal, and brave, possessing a sense of humor that manifested in her laughter at his jokes. He sensed, deep within her innocent gaze, a warmth waiting to be awakened. Yet, as he surveyed her, he felt discouraged. This frankness and innocence were artificial constructs, manufactured by a conspiracy of mothers, aunts, and grandmothers because society believed it was what he desired—a purity he could then shatter with the lordly pleasure of a conqueror.

These reflections were common to young men approaching their wedding day, usually accompanied by a sense of guilt. Newland felt none. He could not lament, as Thackeray's heroes often did, that he lacked a blank page to offer his bride. He knew that if he had been raised as she had, neither of them would have been capable of navigating the world. He could find no honest reason, aside from his own vanity and desire for pleasure, why she should not have enjoyed the same freedom of experience as he.

Yet, at this hour, these thoughts were driven by the inopportune arrival of the Countess Olenska. Here he was, at the precipice of his betrothal, thrust into a scandal that complicated every hope he held. "Hang Ellen Olenska!" he muttered as he extinguished the fire and began to undress. He could not understand why her fate should concern him, yet he sensed he had only just begun to measure the risks his engagement now imposed upon him.

A few days later, the storm broke.

The Lovell Mingotts issued cards for a "formal dinner"—complete with extra footmen, double portions, and Roman punch—headlined with the words "To meet the Countess Olenska," treating the stranger with the hospitality reserved for royalty. The guest list was curated with the boldness of a tsar: the Selfridge Merrys, the Beauforts, Mr. Sillerton Jackson and his sister Sophy, the Lawrence Leffertses, Mrs. Lefferts Rushworth, the Harry Thorleys, the Reggie Chiverses, and young Morris Dagonet and his wife, a van der Luyden. It was a gathering of the inner circle, united in their daily and nightly amusements.

Forty-eight hours later, the unthinkable occurred. Every single guest refused the invitation, save for the Beauforts and the Jacksons. The slight was sharpened by the absence of the Reggie Chiverses, despite their Mingott lineage, and by the uniform phrasing of the regrets, devoid of the customary excuse of a prior engagement. In a city as small and interconnected as New York, where everyone knew everyone's schedule, the refusal was a deliberate, cruel statement.

The Mingotts met the blow with characteristic gallantry. Mrs. Lovell Mingott confided the matter to Mrs. Welland, who passed it to Newland Archer. Blazing with righteous anger, he appealed passionately to his mother. After a period of painful internal resistance and external delay, she succumbed. Embracing his cause with renewed vigor, she donned her grey velvet bonnet and declared, "I'll go and see Louisa van der Luyden."

New York in Newland Archer's time was a slippery pyramid. At its base lay the "plain people," an honorable but obscure majority elevated by marriage into the ruling clans. Above them rose the compact, dominant group represented by the Mingotts, Newlands, Chiverses, and Mansons. While others assumed they were the apex, the older generation knew that true aristocracy belonged to a select few.

"Don't tell me," Mrs. Archer would chide her children, dismissing modern notions of New York aristocracy. "If there is one, neither the Mingotts nor the Mansons belong to it—not the Newlands or the Chiverses. Our ancestors were respectable merchants who stayed because they prospered. One signed the Declaration; another was a general under Washington. These are points of pride, but they mean nothing of rank. New York is a commercial community, and only three families claim true aristocratic origin."

Everyone knew these three: the Dagonets of Washington Square, allied with the Pitts and Foxes; the Lannings, intermarried with the descendants of Count de Grasse; and the van der Luydens, direct heirs of the first Dutch governor of Manhattan and linked by pre-revolutionary marriages to French and British nobility. The Lannings survived only in two elderly, lively spinsters; the Dagonets were a significant clan; but the van der Luydens stood above all, fading into a super-terrestrial twilight from which emerged only Mr. and Mrs. Henry van der Luyden.

Mrs. Henry van der Luyden was formerly Louisa Dagonet. Her mother was the granddaughter of Colonel du Lac, a Channel Islander who fought under Cornwallis and settled in Maryland with his bride, Lady Angelica Trevenna. The ties between the Dagonets, the du Lacs, and their Cornish kinsfolk, the Trevennas, remained unbroken. The van der Luydens maintained long visits to the Duke of St. Austrey in Cornwall and Gloucestershire, though the Duchess feared the Atlantic. They divided their time between Trevenna, their Maryland estate, and Skuytercliff, the great Hudson estate granted to the first Governor, where Mr. van der Luyden still held the title of Patroon. Their Madison Avenue mansion was rarely opened, reserved for their most intimate friends.

"I wish you would go with me, Newland," his mother said suddenly, pausing at the door of the carriage. "Louisa is fond of you; and of course, it is for May's sake that I take this step. Besides, if we do not all stand together, there will be no Society left."

Mrs. Henry van der Luyden sat in silence, her attention fixed on her cousin Mrs. Archer's words. It was easy enough to convince oneself beforehand that Mrs. van der Luyden was a creature of quiet reserve, naturally non-committal yet deeply kind to those she truly cared for. Yet, even knowing this, the chill that settled in the high-ceilinged, white-walled drawing room of Madison Avenue was undeniable. The pale brocaded armchairs stood uncovered, waiting for guests who had not yet arrived, while gauze curtains still draped the ormolu ornaments and the magnificent carved frame holding Gainsborough's *Lady Angelica du Lac*.

Mrs. van der Luyden's own portrait, painted by Huntington in black velvet and Venetian point, hung opposite that of her lovely ancestress. It was widely regarded as "as fine as a Cabanel," and despite twenty years having passed since its creation, it remained a perfect likeness. The woman sitting beneath it, listening intently, might have been the twin sister of the fair, still-youngish figure drooping against a gilt armchair before a green rep curtain. Mrs. van der Luyden still dressed in black velvet and Venetian point for society—or rather, since she never dined out, when she opened her own doors to receive them. Her fair hair, faded without turning gray, was parted in flat, overlapping points across her forehead, and the straight nose dividing her pale blue eyes was only slightly more pinched at the nostrils than in the painting. To Newland Archer, she seemed gruesomely preserved in the airless atmosphere of a perfectly irreproachable existence, much like a body trapped in a glacier, retaining a rosy life-in-death for years.

Like all his family, Archer esteemed and admired Mrs. van der Luyden, though he found her gentle sweetness less approachable than the grim determination of some of his mother's old aunts—fierce spinsters who said "No" on principle before they even knew what was being asked. Mrs. van der Luyden's attitude offered neither a clear yes nor a no; it inclined toward clemency until her thin lips wavered into the shadow of a smile, delivering the almost invariable reply: "I shall first have to talk this over with my husband."

She and Mr. van der Luyden were so exactly alike that Archer often wondered how, after forty years of closest conjugality, two such merged identities could ever separate enough to deliberate on anything as controversial as a social slight. Yet, since neither had ever reached a decision without prefacing it with this mysterious conclave, Mrs. Archer and her son had set forth their case and waited resignedly for the familiar phrase.

However, Mrs. van der Luyden, who had seldom surprised anyone, now did so by reaching her long hand toward the bell-rope.

"I think," she said, "I should like Henry to hear what you have told me."

A footman appeared, to whom she gravely added, "If Mr. van der Luyden has finished reading the newspaper, please ask him to be kind enough to come." She spoke of "reading the newspaper" in the tone a Minister's wife might use to describe "presiding at a Cabinet meeting"—not from arrogance, but because a lifetime of habit and the attitudes of her circle had led her to consider Mr. van der Luyden's least gesture as possessing almost sacerdotal importance.

Her promptness indicated she viewed the matter as pressing as Mrs. Archer did, yet, lest she be thought to have committed herself prematurely, she added with her sweetest look: "Henry always enjoys seeing you, dear Adeline; and he will wish to congratulate Newland."

The double doors solemnly reopened, and between them appeared Mr. Henry van der Luyden: tall, spare, and frock-coated, with faded fair hair, a straight nose identical to his wife's, and the same look of frozen gentleness in eyes that were merely pale gray instead of pale blue.

Mr. van der Luyden greeted Mrs. Archer with cousinly affability, offered Newland low-voiced congratulations couched in the same language as his wife's, and seated himself in one of the brocade armchairs with the simplicity of a reigning sovereign.

"I had just finished reading the *Times*," he said, laying his long fingertips together. "In town, my mornings are so occupied that I find it more convenient to read the newspapers after luncheon."

"Ah, there is much to be said for that plan—in fact, I believe my uncle Egmont used to say he found it less agitating not to read the morning papers until after dinner," Mrs. Archer responded.

"Yes: my good father abhorred hurry. But now we live in a constant rush," Mr. van der Luyden said in measured tones, looking with pleasant deliberation around the large, shrouded room, which to Archer was a complete image of its owners.

"But I hope you *had* finished your reading, Henry?" his wife interposed.

"Quite—quite," he reassured her.

"Then I should like Adeline to tell you—"

"Oh, it is really Newland's story," his mother smiled, and proceeded to rehearse once more the monstrous tale of the affront inflicted upon Mrs. Lovell Mingott.

"Of course," she concluded, "Augusta Welland and Mary Mingott both felt that, especially in view of Newland's engagement, you and Henry *ought to know*."

"Ah—" said Mr. van der Luyden, drawing a deep breath.

A silence fell, during which the tick of the monumental ormolu clock on the white marble mantelpiece grew as loud as the boom of a minute-gun. Archer contemplated with awe the two slender, faded figures seated side by side in a kind of viceregal rigidity, mouthpieces of some remote ancestral authority that fate compelled them to wield, when they would so much rather have lived in simplicity and seclusion, digging invisible weeds from the perfect lawns of Skuytercliff and playing Patience together in the evenings.

Mr. van der Luyden was the first to speak.

"You really think this is due to some... some intentional interference of Lawrence Lefferts's?" he enquired, turning to Archer.

"I am certain of it, sir. Larry has been going it rather harder than usual lately—if cousin Louisa won't mind my mentioning it—having rather a stiff affair with the postmaster's wife in their village, or someone of that sort; and whenever poor Gertrude Lefferts begins to suspect anything, and he fears trouble, he gets up a fuss of this kind to show how awfully moral he is, talking at the top of his voice about the impertinence of inviting his wife to meet people he does not wish her to know. He is simply using Madame Olenska as a lightning-rod; I have seen him try the same thing often before."

"The *Leffertses*!" said Mrs. van der Luyden.

"The *Leffertses*!" echoed Mrs. Archer. "What would uncle Egmont have said of Lawrence Lefferts pronouncing on anybody's social position? It shows what Society has come to."

"We will hope it has not quite come to that," said Mr. van der Luyden firmly.

"Ah, if only you and Louisa went out more!" sighed Mrs. Archer.

Instantly, she realized her mistake. The van der Luydens were morbidly sensitive to any criticism of their secluded existence. They were the arbiters of fashion, the Court of Last Appeal, and they knew it, bowing to their fate. Yet, being shy and retiring persons with no natural inclination for their part, they lived as much as possible in the sylvan solitude of Skuytercliff, and when they came to town, declined all invitations on the plea of Mrs. van der Luyden's health.

Newland Archer came to his mother's rescue. "Everybody in New York knows what you and cousin Louisa represent. That is why Mrs. Mingott felt she ought not to allow this slight on Countess Olenska to pass without consulting you."

Mrs. van der Luyden glanced at her husband, who glanced back at her.

"It is the principle that I dislike," said Mr. van der Luyden. "As long as a member of a well-known family is backed up by that family, it should be considered—final."

"It seems so to me," said his wife, as if she were producing a new thought.

"I had no idea," Mr. van der Luyden continued, "that things had come to such a pass." He paused, looking at his wife again. "It occurs to me, my dear, that the Countess Olenska is already a sort of relation—through Medora Manson's first husband. At any rate, she will be when Newland marries." He turned toward the young man. "Have you read this morning's *Times*, Newland?"

"Why, yes, sir," said Archer, who usually tossed off half a dozen papers with his morning coffee.

Husband and wife looked at each other again. Their pale eyes clung together in prolonged and serious consultation; then a faint smile fluttered over Mrs. van der Luyden's face. She had evidently guessed and approved.

Mr. van der Luyden turned to Mrs. Archer. "If Louisa's health allowed her to dine out—I wish you would say to Mrs. Lovell Mingott—that she and I would have been happy to—er—fill the places of the Lawrence Leffertses at her dinner." He paused to let the irony of this sink in. "As you know, this is impossible." Mrs. Archer sounded a sympathetic assent. "But Newland tells me he has read this morning's *Times*; therefore, he has probably seen that Louisa's relative, the Duke of St. Austrey, arrives next week on the *Russia*. He is coming to enter his new sloop, the *Guinevere*, in next summer's International Cup Race; and also to have a little canvasback shooting at Trevenna." Mr. van der Luyden paused again and continued with increasing benevolence: "Before taking him down to Maryland, we are inviting a few friends to meet him here—only a little dinner—with a reception afterward. I am sure Louisa will be as glad as I am if Countess Olenska will let us include her among our guests." He got up, bent his long body with a stiff friendliness toward his cousin, and added: "I think I have Louisa's authority for saying that she will herself leave the invitation to dine when she drives out presently: with our cards—of course, with our cards."

Mrs. Archer, who knew this to be a hint that the seventeen-hand chestnuts which were never kept waiting were at the door, rose with a hurried murmur of thanks. Mrs. van der Luyden beamed on her with the smile of Esther interceding with Ahasuerus; but her husband raised a protesting hand.

"There is nothing to thank me for, dear Adeline; nothing whatever. This kind of thing must not happen in New York; it shall not, as long as I can help it," he pronounced with sovereign gentleness as he steered his cousins to the door.

Two hours later, everyone knew that the great C-spring barouche in which Mrs. van der Luyden took the air at all seasons had been seen at old Mrs. Mingott's door, where a large square envelope was handed in; and that evening at the Opera, Mr. Sillerton Jackson was able to state that the envelope contained a card inviting the Countess Olenska to the dinner which the van der Luydens were giving the following week for their cousin, the Duke of St. Austrey.

Some of the younger men in the club box exchanged a smile at this announcement and glanced sideways at Lawrence Lefferts, who sat carelessly in the front of the box, pulling his long fair moustache. As the soprano paused, he remarked with authority: "No one but Patti ought to attempt the *Sonnambula*."

It was the universal consensus in New York that the Countess Olenska had lost her looks.

She had first arrived in the city during Newland Archer's boyhood, a brilliantly pretty girl of nine or ten whom everyone whispered ought to be painted. Her parents were continental wanderers, and after a childhood spent chasing horizons, she had lost them both and fallen into the care of her aunt, Medora Manson. Medora was a wanderer herself, perpetually returning to New York to "settle down," only to bring with her a new husband or an adopted child. Yet, within months, she would invariably part ways with her spouse or quarrel with her ward, sell her home at a loss, and vanish once more into her itinerant life. Because her mother was a Rushworth and her final, unhappy marriage tied her to the eccentric Chivers family, New York tolerated Medora's oddities with indulgent shrugs. But when she returned with her little orphaned niece—whose parents had been beloved despite their unfortunate penchant for travel—the city felt a pang of pity that such a pretty child should be in such hands.

Everyone was inclined to be kind to little Ellen Mingott, though her dusky red cheeks and tight curls gave her an air of gaiety that seemed inappropriate for a child who should still be in black for her parents. It was one of Medora's many misguided peculiarities to flout the unalterable rules of American mourning. When she stepped off the steamer, the family was scandalized to see that the crape veil she wore for her own brother was seven inches shorter than those of her sisters-in-law, while little Ellen wore crimson merino and amber beads, looking like a gypsy foundling.

But New York had long resigned itself to Medora's chaos; only a few old ladies shook their heads over Ellen's gaudy attire, while her other relations fell under the spell of her high color and high spirits. She was a fearless, familiar little thing who asked disconcerting questions, made precocious comments, and possessed outlandish arts, such as dancing a Spanish shawl dance and singing Neapolitan love-songs to a guitar. Under the direction of her aunt—whose real name was Mrs. Thorley Chivers, but who, having received a Papal title, had resumed her first husband's patronymic to call herself the Marchioness Manson, since in Italy she could turn it into Manzoni—the little girl received an expensive but incoherent education. This included "drawing from the model," a concept never dreamed of before, and playing the piano in quintets with professional musicians.

Of course, no good could come of this. A few years later, when poor Chivers finally died in a madhouse, his widow (draped in strange weeds) again pulled up stakes and departed with Ellen, who had grown into a tall, bony girl with conspicuous eyes. For some time, no more was heard of them. Then news arrived of Ellen's marriage to an immensely rich Polish nobleman of legendary fame, whom she had met at a ball at the Tuileries. He was said to possess princely establishments in Paris, Nice, and Florence, a yacht at Cowes, and many square miles of shooting grounds in Transylvania. She disappeared in a kind of sulphurous apotheosis. When a few years later Medora returned to New York, subdued, impoverished, and mourning a third husband while seeking a still smaller house, people wondered why her rich niece had not been able to do something for her. Then came the news that Ellen's own marriage had ended in disaster, and that she was returning home to seek rest and oblivion among her kinsfolk.

These thoughts passed through Newland Archer's mind a week later as he watched the Countess Olenska enter the van der Luyden drawing-room on the evening of the momentous dinner. The occasion was solemn, and he wondered nervously how she would carry it off. She arrived late, one hand still ungloved, fastening a bracelet about her wrist. Yet she entered the drawing-room, where New York's most chosen company was assembled with some awe, without any appearance of haste or embarrassment.

In the middle of the room, she paused, looking about with a grave mouth and smiling eyes. In that instant, Newland Archer rejected the general verdict on her looks. It was true that her early radiance was gone. The red cheeks had paled; she was thin, worn, and perhaps a little older than her age, which must have been nearly thirty. But there was about her the mysterious authority of beauty—a sureness in the carriage of the head, the movement of the eyes—which, without being theatrical, struck him as highly trained and full of conscious power. At the same time, she was simpler in manner than most of the ladies present. Many people, as he heard afterward from Janey, were disappointed that her appearance was not more "stylish," for stylishness was what New York most valued. Perhaps, Archer reflected, it was because her early vivacity had disappeared; because she was so quiet—quiet in her movements, her voice, and the low, resonant tones of her speech. New York had expected something far more resonant from a young woman with such a history.

The dinner was a formidable business. Dining with the van der Luydens was at best no light matter, and dining there with a Duke who was their cousin was almost a religious solemnity. It pleased Archer to think that only an old New Yorker could perceive the subtle difference (to New York) between being merely a Duke and being the van der Luydens' Duke. New York took stray noblemen calmly, and even (except in the Struthers set) with a certain distrustful hauteur; but when they presented such credentials as these, they were received with an old-fashioned cordiality that they would have been greatly mistaken in ascribing solely to their standing in Debrett. It was for just such distinctions that the young man cherished his old New York, even while he smiled at its pretensions.

The van der Luydens had done their best to emphasize the importance of the occasion. The du Lac Sevres and the Trevenna George II plate were out; so was the van der Luyden "Lowestoft" (East India Company) and the Dagonet Crown Derby. Mrs. van der Luyden looked more than ever like a Cabanel, and Mrs. Archer, in her grandmother's seed-pearls and emeralds, reminded her son of an Isabey miniature. All the ladies wore their handsomest jewels, but it was characteristic of the house and the occasion that these were mostly set in rather heavy, old-fashioned styles. Old Miss Lanning, who had been persuaded to come, actually wore her mother's cameos and a Spanish blonde shawl.

The Countess Olenska was the only young woman at the dinner. Yet, as Archer scanned the smooth, plump, elderly faces between their diamond necklaces and towering ostrich feathers, they struck him as curiously immature compared with hers. It frightened him to think what must have gone to the making of her eyes.

The Duke of St. Austrey, who sat at his hostess's right, was naturally the chief figure of the evening. But if the Countess Olenska was less conspicuous than had been hoped, the Duke was almost invisible. Being a well-bred man, he had not (like another recent ducal visitor) come in a shooting-jacket; but his evening clothes were so shabby and baggy, and he wore them with such an air of their being homespun, that (with his stooping way of sitting and the vast beard spreading over his shirt-front) he hardly gave the appearance of being in dinner attire. He was short, round-shouldered, sunburnt, with a thick nose, small eyes, and a sociable smile. But he seldom spoke, and when he did, it was in such low tones that, despite the frequent silences of expectation around the table, his remarks were lost to all but his neighbors.

When the men joined the ladies after dinner, the Duke went straight up to the Countess Olenska, and they sat down in a corner, plunging into animated talk. Neither seemed aware that the Duke should first have paid his respects to Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Headly Chivers, or that the Countess should have conversed with that amiable hypochondriac, Mr. Urban Dagonet of Washington Square, who, in order to have the pleasure of meeting her, had broken through his fixed rule of not dining out between January and April. The two chatted for nearly twenty minutes. Then the Countess rose and, walking alone across the wide drawing-room, sat down at Newland Archer's side.

It was not the custom in New York drawing-rooms for a lady to get up and walk away from one gentleman in order to seek the company of another. Etiquette required that she wait, immovable as an idol, while the men who wished to converse with her succeeded each other at her side. But the Countess was apparently unaware of having broken any rule. She sat at perfect ease in a corner of the sofa beside Archer and looked at him with the kindest eyes.

"I want you to talk to me about May," she said.

Instead of answering her, he asked, "You knew the Duke before?"

"Oh, yes—we used to see him every winter at Nice. He's very fond of gambling; he used to come to the house a great deal." She said it in the simplest manner, as if she had said, "He's fond of wild-flowers." After a moment, she added candidly, "I think he's the dullest man I ever met."

This pleased her companion so much that he forgot the slight shock her previous remark had caused him. It was undeniably exciting to meet a lady who found the van der Luydens' Duke dull and dared to utter the opinion. He longed to question her, to hear more about the life of which her careless words had given him such an illuminating glimpse; but he feared to touch on distressing memories. Before he could think of anything to say, she had strayed back to her original subject.

"May is a darling; I've seen no young girl in New York so handsome and so intelligent. Are you very much in love with her?"

Newland Archer reddened and laughed. "As much as a man can be."

She continued to consider him thoughtfully, as if not to miss any shade of meaning in what he said. "Do you think, then, there is a limit?"

"To being in love? If there is, I haven't found it!"

She glowed with sympathy. "Ah—it's really and truly a romance?"

"The most romantic of romances!"

"How delightful! And you found it all out for yourselves—it was not in the least arranged for you?"

Archer looked at her incredulously. "Have you forgotten," he asked with a smile, "that in our country we don't allow our marriages to be arranged for us?"

A dusky blush rose to her cheek, and he instantly regretted his words.

"Yes," she answered, "I'd forgotten. You must forgive me if I sometimes make these mistakes. I don't always remember that everything here is good that was—was bad where I've come from." She looked down at her Viennese fan of eagle feathers, and he saw that her lips trembled.

"I'm so sorry," he said impulsively, "but you *are* among friends here, you know."

"Yes—I know. Wherever I go I have that feeling. That's why I came home. I want to forget everything else, to become a complete American again, like the Mingotts and Wellands, and you and your delightful mother, and all the other good people here tonight." Ah, here's May arriving, and you will want to hurry away to her," she added, but without moving; and her eyes turned back from the door to rest on the young man's face.

The drawing-rooms were beginning to fill with after-dinner guests. Following Madame Olenska's glance, Archer saw May Welland entering with her mother. In her dress of white and silver, with a wreath of silver blossoms in her hair, the tall girl looked like a Diana just alight from the chase.

"Oh," said Archer, "I have so many rivals; you see she's already surrounded. There's the Duke being introduced."

"Then stay with me a little longer," Madame Olenska said in a low tone, just touching his knee with her plumed fan. It was the lightest touch, but it thrilled him like a caress.

"Yes, let me stay," he answered in the same tone, hardly knowing what he said. But just then Mr. van der Luyden came up, followed by old Mr. Urban Dagonet. The Countess greeted them with her grave smile, and Archer, feeling his host's admonitory glance on him, rose and surrendered his seat.

Madame Olenska held out her hand as if to bid him goodbye.
