"Tomorrow, then, after five—I shall expect you," she said, and then turned back to make room for Mr. Dagonet.

"Tomorrow—" Archer heard himself repeating, though there had been no engagement, and during their talk she had given him no hint that she wished to see him again.

As he moved away, he saw Lawrence Lefferts, tall and resplendent, leading his wife up to be introduced. He heard Gertrude Lefferts say, as she beamed on the Countess with her large, unperceiving smile, "But I think we used to go to dancing-school together when we were children..." Behind her, waiting their turn to name themselves to the Countess, Archer noticed a number of the recalcitrant couples who had declined to meet her at Mrs. Lovell Mingott's. As Mrs. Archer remarked: when the van der Luydens chose, they knew how to give a lesson. The wonder was that they chose so seldom.

The young man felt a touch on his arm and saw Mrs. van der Luyden looking down on him from the pure eminence of black velvet and the family diamonds. "It was good of you, dear Newland, to devote yourself so unselfishly to Madame Olenska. I told your cousin Henry he must really come to the rescue."

He was aware of smiling at her vaguely, and she added, as if condescending to his natural shyness, "I've never seen May

The Countess Olenska had said "after five"; and at half-past the hour, Newland Archer rang the bell of the peeling stucco house. A giant wisteria choked the feeble cast-iron balcony above, a verdant strangler around the entrance of the home she had rented from the vagabond Medora, far down West Twenty-third Street.

It was a peculiar neighborhood for a woman of her station. Her neighbors were small dressmakers, bird-stuffers, and "people who wrote." Further down the disheveled street, Archer recognized a dilapidated wooden house at the end of a paved path, where a journalist named Winsett resided—a man Archer had occasionally encountered. Winsett did not invite guests, yet he had once pointed out his home during a late-night stroll, prompting Archer to shiver and wonder if the humanities were so poorly housed in other capitals.

Madame Olenska's dwelling was saved from this squalid appearance only by a fresh coat of paint on the window frames. As Archer approached the modest front, a dark thought crossed his mind: the Polish Count must have stripped her of her fortune as thoroughly as he had of her illusions.

The young man had endured an unsatisfactory day. He had lunched with the Wellands, hoping to whisk May away for a walk in the Park afterward. He craved the solitude of her company, wanting to tell her how enchanting she had looked the previous night, how proud he felt, and to urge her to hasten their marriage. But Mrs. Welland had firmly reminded him that the round of family visits was far from over. When he hinted at advancing the wedding date, she raised reproachful eyebrows and sighed, "Twelve dozen of everything—hand-embroidered."

Packed into the family landau, they rolled from one tribal doorstep to another. When the afternoon's circuit finally ended, Archer parted from his betrothed with the sensation of being paraded like a wild animal cunningly trapped. He supposed his anthropological readings colored his view, making him see this natural display of family affection as coarse. Yet, remembering that the Wellands did not expect the wedding until the following autumn, and picturing the years of confinement that lay ahead, a damp chill settled in his spirit.

"Tomorrow," Mrs. Welland called after him, "we'll do the Chiverses and the Dallases." He realized she was methodically alphabetizing through their two families, and they were only in the first quarter.

He had intended to mention the Countess Olenska's summons—the command, rather—to May. But in the brief moments they shared, he had more pressing matters to discuss. Furthermore, it struck him as absurd to bring it up. He knew May particularly desired him to be kind to her cousin; hadn't that wish hastened their engagement? It gave him an odd sensation to reflect that, but for the Countess's arrival, he might have been, if not still a free man, at least less irrevocably pledged. But May had willed it so, and he felt strangely relieved of further responsibility—and therefore at liberty, if he chose, to visit her cousin without telling her.

Standing on Madame Olenska's threshold, curiosity seized him. He was puzzled by the tone of her summons; he concluded she was less simple than she appeared.

The door opened to reveal a swarthy, foreign-looking maid with a prominent bosom beneath a gay neckerchief, whom he vaguely imagined to be Sicilian. She welcomed him with a flash of white teeth. Answering his inquiries with a head-shake of incomprehension, she led him through the narrow hall into a low, firelit drawing-room. The room was empty, and she left him for an appreciable time to wonder if she had gone to find her mistress or if she hadn't understood his purpose, perhaps thinking he was there to wind the clock—the only visible specimen of which had stopped. He knew the southern races communicated in the language of pantomime, and he was mortified to find her shrugs and smiles so unintelligible. Finally, she returned with a lamp. Archer, meanwhile, had constructed a phrase from Dante and Petrarch, evoking the answer: *"La signora è fuori; ma verrà subito,"* which he interpreted as, "She's out—but you'll soon see."

What he saw, illuminated by the lamp, was the faded, shadowy charm of a room unlike any he had known. He knew the Countess had brought some of her possessions with her—"bits of wreckage," she called them—and these were represented by a few small, slender tables of dark wood, a delicate Greek bronze on the mantelpiece, and a stretch of red damask nailed over the discolored wallpaper behind a couple of Italian-looking pictures in old frames.

Newland Archer prided himself on his knowledge of Italian art. His boyhood had been saturated with Ruskin, and he had devoured the latest books: John Addington Symonds, Vernon Lee's *Euphorion*, the essays of P. G. Hamerton, and the wonderful new volume *The Renaissance* by Walter Pater. He spoke easily of Botticelli and Fra Angelico with a faint condescension. But these pictures bewildered him; they were unlike anything he was accustomed to seeing when traveling in Italy. Perhaps, too, his powers of observation were impaired by the strangeness of finding himself in this empty house, where apparently no one expected him. He regretted not telling May Welland of the Countess's request and felt a disturbance at the thought that his betrothed might arrive to find him sitting there, waiting alone in the dusk at a lady's fireside with an air of intimacy.

But since he had come, he meant to wait. He sank into a chair and stretched his feet toward the logs.

It was odd to be summoned in that manner and then forgotten, yet Archer felt more curious than mortified. The atmosphere of the room was so distinct from anything he had ever breathed that self-consciousness vanished in the sense of adventure. He had been in drawing-rooms hung with red damask and pictures "of the Italian school" before; what struck him was how Medora Manson's shabby hired house, with its blighted background of pampas grass and Rogers statuettes, had been transformed by a turn of the hand and the skillful use of a few props into something intimate, "foreign," subtly suggestive of old romantic scenes and sentiments. He tried to analyze the trick, searching for clues in the grouping of chairs and tables, in the fact that only two Jacqueminot roses—of which nobody ever bought less than a dozen—sat in the slender vase at his elbow, and in the vague, pervading perfume that was not what one put on handkerchiefs, but rather the scent of a far-off bazaar, composed of Turkish coffee, ambergris, and dried roses.

His mind wandered to the question of what May's drawing-room would look like. He knew Mr. Welland, who was behaving "very handsomely," already had his eye on a newly built house in East Thirty-ninth Street. The neighborhood was considered remote, and the house was constructed of a ghastly greenish-yellow stone that the younger architects were beginning to employ as a protest against the brownstone that coated New York like a cold chocolate sauce; but the plumbing was perfect. Archer would have liked to travel, to postpone the housing question; but though the Wellands approved of an extended European honeymoon—perhaps even a winter in Egypt—they were firm on the need for a house for the returning couple. The young man felt his fate was sealed: for the rest of his life, he would go up every evening between the cast-iron railings of that greenish-yellow doorstep, passing through a Pompeian vestibule into a hall with wainscoting of varnished yellow wood. Beyond that, his imagination could not travel. He knew the drawing-room above had a bay window, but he could not imagine how May would deal with it. She submitted cheerfully to the purple satin and yellow tuftings of the Welland drawing-room, to its sham Buhl tables and gilt vitrines full of modern Saxe. He saw no reason to suppose she would want anything different in her own house; his only comfort was the reflection that she would probably let him arrange his library as he pleased—which would, of course, be with "sincere" Eastlake furniture and plain new bookcases without glass doors.

The round-bosomed maid came in, drew the curtains, pushed back a log, and said consolingly, "Verra—verra." When she had gone, Archer stood up and began to wander about. Should he wait any longer? His position was becoming rather foolish. Perhaps he had misunderstood Madame Olenska—perhaps she had not invited him after all.

Down the cobblestones of the quiet street came the ring of a stepper's hoofs; they stopped before the house, and he caught the opening of a carriage door. Parting the curtains, he looked out into the early dusk. A street-lamp faced him, and in its light he saw Julius Beaufort's compact English brougham, drawn by a big roan, and the banker descending to help out Madame Olenska.

Beaufort stood, hat in hand, saying something which his companion seemed to negate; then they shook hands, and he jumped into his carriage while she mounted the steps.

When she entered the room, she showed no surprise at seeing Archer there; surprise seemed the emotion she was least addicted to.

"How do you like my funny house?" she asked. "To me, it's like heaven."

As she spoke, she untied her little velvet bonnet and tossed it away with her long cloak, standing before him with meditative eyes.

"You've arranged it delightfully," he rejoined, alive to the flatness of his words but imprisoned by convention in his consuming desire to be simple and striking.

"Oh, it's a poor little place. My relations despise it. But at any rate, it's less gloomy than the van der Luydens'."

The words gave him an electric shock. Few rebellious spirits would dare to call the stately home of the van der Luydens gloomy. Those privileged to enter it shivered there and spoke of it as "handsome." But suddenly, he was glad she had given voice to the general shiver.

"It's delicious—what you've done here," he repeated.

"I like the little house," she admitted. "But I suppose what I like is the blessedness of its being here, in my own country and my own town; and then, of being alone in it." She spoke so low he hardly heard the last phrase, but in his awkwardness, he took it up.

"You like so much to be alone?"

"Yes; as long as my friends keep me from feeling lonely." She sat down near the fire, said, "Nastasia will bring the tea presently," and signed to him to return to his armchair, adding, "I see you've already chosen your corner."

Leaning back, she folded her arms behind her head and looked at the fire under drooping lids.

"This is the hour I like best—don't you?"

A proper sense of his dignity caused him to answer, "I was afraid you'd forgotten the hour. Beaufort must have been very engrossing."

She looked amused. "Why—have you waited long? Mr. Beaufort took me to see a number of houses—since it seems I'm not to be allowed to stay in this one." She appeared to dismiss both Beaufort and himself from her mind and went on, "I've never been in a city where there seems to be such a feeling against living in *des quartiers excentriques*. What does it matter where one lives? I'm told this street is respectable."

"It's not fashionable."

"Fashionable! Do you all think so much of that? Why not make one's own fashions? But I suppose I've lived too independently; at any rate, I want to do what you all do—I want to feel cared for and safe."

He was touched, as he had been the evening before when she spoke of her need of guidance.

"That's what your friends want you to feel. New York's an awfully safe place," he added with a flash of sarcasm.

"Yes, isn't it? One feels that," she cried, missing the mockery. "Being here is like—like—being taken on a holiday when one has been a good little girl and done all one's lessons."

The analogy was well meant but did not altogether please him. He did not mind being flippant about New York but disliked hearing anyone else take the same tone. He wondered if she did not begin to see what a powerful engine it was, and how nearly it had crushed her. The Lovell Mingotts' dinner, patched up in extremis out of all sorts of social odds and ends, ought to have taught her the narrowness of her escape; but either she had been all along unaware of having skirted disaster, or else she had lost sight of it in the triumph of the van der Luyden evening. Archer inclined to the former theory; he fancied that her New York was still completely undifferentiated, and the conjecture nettled him.

"Last night," he said, "New York laid itself out for you. The van der Luydens do nothing by halves."

"No: how kind they are! It was such a nice party. Every one seems to have such an esteem for them."

The terms were hardly adequate; she might have spoken in that way of a tea-party at the dear old Miss Lannings'.

"The van der Luydens," said Archer, feeling himself pompous as he spoke, "are the most powerful influence in New York society. Unfortunately—owing to her health—they receive very seldom."

She unclasped her hands from behind her head and looked at him meditatively.

"Isn't that perhaps the reason?"

"The reason—?"

"For their great influence; that they make themselves so rare."

He colored a little, stared at her—and suddenly felt the penetration of the remark. At a stroke, she had pricked the van der Luydens, and they collapsed. He laughed and sacrificed them.

Nastasia brought the tea, with handleless Japanese cups and little covered dishes, placing the tray on a low table.

"But you'll explain these things to me—you'll tell me all I ought to know," Madame Olenska continued

The next day, Newland Archer managed to persuade May to slip away for a stroll in the Park immediately following luncheon. It was the custom in old-fashioned Episcopalian New York for a young woman to accompany her parents to church on Sunday afternoons; yet Mrs. Welland had granted her permission to skip this duty, having been convinced that morning that a lengthy engagement was necessary to allow time for the meticulous preparation of a hand-embroidered trousseau, complete with the proper number of dozens.

The day was exquisite. The bare branches of the trees lining the Mall were draped in a canopy of lapis lazuli, arching over snow that glittered like shattered crystal. It was the kind of weather designed to ignite May's radiance, and she burned with the vibrant intensity of a young maple leaf in the frost. Archer felt a swell of pride watching the glances turned toward her, and the simple joy of possession seemed to clear away the lingering perplexities that often clouded his mind.

"It's so delicious—waking every morning to smell lilies-of-the-valley in one's room!" she exclaimed.

"Yesterday they arrived late. I didn't have time in the morning—"

"But your remembering to send them each day makes me love them so much more than if you'd given a standing order, with them arriving punctually every morning like a music teacher. I know Gertrude Lefferts's did, for instance, when she and Lawrence were engaged."

"Ah—they certainly would!" Archer laughed, amused by her keen observation. He glanced sideways at her fruit-like cheek and felt rich and secure enough to add, "When I sent your lilies yesterday afternoon, I noticed some rather gorgeous yellow roses and packed them off to Madame Olenska. Was that right?"

"How dear of you! Anything of that kind delights her. It's odd she didn't mention it: she lunched with us today and spoke of Mr. Beaufort sending her wonderful orchids, and of cousin Henry van der Luyden sending a whole hamper of carnations from Skuytercliff. She seems so surprised to receive flowers. Don't people send them in Europe? She thinks it such a pretty custom."

"Oh, well, no wonder mine were overshadowed by Beaufort's," Archer said irritably. Then he remembered that he had not included a card with the roses and was vexed at having mentioned them. He wanted to say, "I called on your cousin yesterday," but hesitated. If Madame Olenska had not mentioned his visit, it might seem awkward for him to bring it up now. Yet, failing to do so gave the affair an air of mystery that he disliked. To shake off the question, he began to talk of their own plans, their future, and Mrs. Welland's insistence on a long engagement.

"If you call it long! Isabel Chivers and Reggie were engaged for two years; Grace and Thorley for nearly a year and a half. Why aren't we very well off as we are?"

It was the traditional maidenly interrogation, and he felt ashamed of himself for finding it singularly childish. No doubt she simply echoed what was said around her; but she was nearing her twenty-second birthday, and he wondered at what age "nice" women began to speak for themselves.

"Never, if we won't let them, I suppose," he mused, recalling his mad outburst to Mr. Sillerton Jackson: "Women ought to be as free as we are..."

It would soon be his task to remove the bandage from this young woman's eyes and bid her look forth upon the world. But how many generations of the women who had shaped her had descended, blindfolded, to the family vault? He shivered slightly, remembering some of the new ideas in his scientific books and the much-cited instance of the Kentucky cave-fish, which had ceased to develop eyes because they had no use for them. What if, when he bade May Welland to open hers, they could only look out blankly at blankness?

"We might be much better off. We might be altogether together—we might travel."

Her face lit up. "That would be lovely," she admitted; she would love to travel. But her mother would not understand their desire to do things so differently.

"As if the mere 'differently' didn't account for it!" the wooer insisted.

"Newland! You're so original!" she exulted.

His heart sank, for he realized he was saying all the things young men in his situation were expected to say, and that she was making the answers that instinct and tradition taught her to make—even to the point of calling him original.

"Original! We're all as like each other as those dolls cut out of the same folded paper. We're like patterns stencilled on a wall. Can't you and I strike out for ourselves, May?"

He had stopped and faced her in the excitement of their discussion, and her eyes rested on him with a bright, unclouded admiration.

"Mercy—shall we elope?" she laughed.

"If you would—"

"You *do* love me, Newland! I'm so happy."

"But then—why not be happier?"

"We can't behave like people in novels, though, can we?"

"Why not—why not—why not?"

She looked a little bored by his insistence. She knew very well that they couldn't, but it was troublesome to have to produce a reason. "I'm not clever enough to argue with you. But that kind of thing is rather—vulgar, isn't it?" she suggested, relieved to have hit upon a word that would assuredly extinguish the whole subject.

"Are you so much afraid, then, of being vulgar?"

She was evidently staggered by this. "Of course I should hate it—so would you," she rejoined, a trifle irritably.

He stood silent, beating his stick nervously against his boot-top; and feeling that she had indeed found the right way to close the discussion, she went on light-heartedly: "Oh, did I tell you that I showed Ellen my ring? She thinks it the most beautiful setting she ever saw. There's nothing like it in the rue de la Paix, she said. I do love you, Newland, for being so artistic!"

The next afternoon, as Archer sat smoking sullenly in his study before dinner, Janey wandered in on him. He had failed to stop at his club on the way up from the office, where he exercised the profession of law in the leisurely manner common to well-to-do New Yorkers of his class. He was out of spirits and slightly out of temper, haunted by a horror of doing the same thing every day at the same hour.

"Sameness—sameness!" he muttered, the word running through his head like a persecuting tune as he saw the familiar tall-hatted figures lounging behind the plate-glass; and because he usually dropped in at the club at that hour, he had gone home instead. He knew not only what they were likely to be talking about, but the part each one would take in the discussion. The Duke, of course, would be their principal theme; though the appearance in Fifth Avenue of a golden-haired lady in a small canary-coloured brougham with a pair of black cobs (for which Beaufort was generally thought responsible) would also doubtless be thoroughly gone into. Such "women" (as they were called) were few in New York; those driving their own carriages still fewer. The appearance of Miss Fanny Ring in Fifth Avenue at the fashionable hour had profoundly agitated society. Only the day before, her carriage had passed Mrs. Lovell Mingott's, and the latter had instantly rung the little bell at her elbow and ordered the coachman to drive her home. "What if it had happened to Mrs. van der Luyden?" people asked each other with a shudder. Archer could hear Lawrence Lefferts, at that very hour, holding forth on the disintegration of society.

He raised his head irritably when his sister Janey entered, and then quickly bent over his book (Swinburne's "Chastelard"—just out) as if he had not seen her. She glanced at the writing-table heaped with books, opened a volume of the "Contes Drolatiques," made a wry face over the archaic French, and sighed: "What learned things you read!"

"Well—?" he asked, as she hovered Cassandra-like before him.

"Mother's very angry."

"Angry? With whom? About what?"

"Miss Sophy Jackson has just been here. She brought word that her brother would come in after dinner: she couldn't say very much, because he forbade her to; he wishes to give all the details himself. He's with cousin Louisa van der Luyden now."

"For heaven's sake, my dear girl, try a fresh start. It would take an omniscient Deity to know what you're talking about."

"It's not a time to be profane, Newland.... Mother feels badly enough about your not going to church..."

With a groan, he plunged back into his book.

"_Newland_! Do listen. Your friend Madame Olenska was at Mrs. Lemuel Struthers's party last night: she went there with the Duke and Mr. Beaufort."

At the last clause of this announcement, a senseless anger swelled the young man's breast. To smother it, he laughed. "Well, what of it? I knew she meant to."

Janey paled and her eyes began to project. "You knew she meant to—and you didn't try to stop her? To warn her?"

"Stop her? Warn her?" He laughed again. "I'm not engaged to be married to the Countess Olenska!" The words had a fantastic sound in his own ears.

"You're marrying into her family."

"Oh, family—family!" he jeered.

"Newland—don't you care about Family?"

"Not a brass farthing."

"Nor about what cousin Louisa van der Luyden will think?"

"Not the half of one—if she thinks such old maid's rubbish."

"Mother is not an old maid," said his virgin sister with pinched lips.

He felt like shouting back: "Yes, she is, and so are the van der Luydens, and so we all are, when it comes to being so much as brushed by the wing-tip of Reality." But he saw her long, gentle face puckering into tears, and felt ashamed of the useless pain he was inflicting.

"Hang Countess Olenska! Don't be a goose, Janey—I'm not her keeper."

"No; but you *did* ask the Wellands to announce your engagement sooner so that we might all back her up; and if it hadn't been for that cousin Louisa would never have invited her to the dinner for the Duke."

"Well—what harm was there in inviting her? She was the best-looking woman in the room; she made the dinner a little less funereal than the usual van der Luyden banquet."

"You know cousin Henry asked her to please you: he persuaded cousin Louisa. And now they're so upset that they're going back to Skuytercliff tomorrow. I think, Newland, you'd better come down. You don't seem to understand how mother feels."

In the drawing-room, Newland found his mother. She raised a troubled brow from her needlework to ask, "Has Janey told you?"

"Yes." He tried to keep his tone as measured as her own. "But I can't take it very seriously."

"Not the fact of having offended cousin Louisa and cousin Henry?"

"The fact that they can be offended by such a trifle as Countess Olenska's going to the house of a woman they consider common."

"Consider—!"

"Well, who is; but who has good music, and amuses people on Sunday evenings, when the whole of New York is dying of inanition."

"Good music? All I know is, there was a woman who got up on a table and sang the things they sing at the places you go to in Paris. There was smoking and champagne."

"Well—that kind of thing happens in other places, and the world still goes on."

"I don't suppose, dear, you're really defending the French Sunday?"

"I've heard you often enough, mother, grumble at the English Sunday when we've been in London."

"New York is neither Paris nor London."

"Oh, no, it's not!" her son groaned.

"You mean, I suppose, that society here is not as brilliant? You're right, I daresay; but we belong here, and people should respect our ways when they come among us. Ellen Olenska especially: she came back to get away from the kind of life people lead in brilliant societies."

Newland made no answer, and after a moment, his mother ventured: "I was going to put on my bonnet and ask you to take me to see cousin Louisa for a moment before dinner." He frowned, and she continued: "I thought you might explain to her what you've just said: that society abroad is different... that people are not as particular, and that Madame Olenska may not have realized how we feel about such things. It would be, you know, dear," she added with an innocent adroitness, "in Madame Olenska's interest if you did."

"Dearest mother, I really don't see how we're concerned in the matter. The Duke took Madame Olenska to Mrs. Struthers's—in fact, he brought Mrs. Struthers to call on her. I was there when they came. If the van der Luydens want to quarrel with anybody, the real culprit is under their own roof."

"Quarrel? Newland, did you ever know of cousin Henry's quarrelling? Besides, the Duke's his guest; and a stranger too. Strangers don't discriminate: how should they? Countess Olenska is a New Yorker, and should have respected the feelings of New York."

"Well, then, if they must have a victim, you have my leave to throw Madame Olenska to them," cried her son, exasperated. "I don't see myself—or you either—offering ourselves up to expiate her crimes."

Two weeks had slipped by, leaving Newland Archer in a state of abstracted idleness within the private confines of his office at Letterblair, Lamson, and Low. The silence of the room, usually a sanctuary for the meticulous lawyer, felt heavy with unspoken tension until the head of the firm summoned him.

Old Mr. Letterblair sat throned behind his mahogany desk, a figure of established authority and perplexity. As he stroked his close-clipped white whiskers and ran a hand through the rumpled grey locks atop his jutting brows, Archer observed him with a mixture of respect and amusement. The senior partner looked remarkably like the Family Physician, a caricature of paternal anxiety, annoyed by a patient whose symptoms refused to fit into any neat medical classification.

"My dear sir," Letterblair began, addressing Archer with the formal deference reserved for a junior, "I have called you to discuss a delicate matter. For the moment, I prefer not to mention it to Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood." He gestured vaguely toward the other senior partners, men whose names adorned the firm's letterhead but whose physical forms had long since departed the mortal coil. In the old New York legal world, where lineage often outweighed tenure, Letterblair was effectively his own grandfather.

Leaning back, his brow furrowed in deep concern, the elder partner continued, "For family reasons..."

Archer looked up, his curiosity piqued.

"The Mingott family," Letterblair said, offering an explanatory smile that didn't quite reach his eyes. "Mrs. Manson Mingott sent for me yesterday. Her granddaughter, the Countess Olenska, wishes to sue her husband for divorce. Certain papers have been placed in my hands." He paused, drumming his fingers rhythmically on the polished wood. "Given your prospective alliance with the family, I believe it is incumbent upon me to consult you—to consider the case with you—before taking any further steps."

A sudden heat rose in Archer's temples. He had seen the Countess only once since their brief encounter at the Opera, where she had appeared less vivid and importunate, receding from his mental foreground as May Welland had effortlessly reclaimed her rightful place in his life. He had not heard the word divorce spoken seriously since Janey's casual, random allusion, dismissing it as mere gossip. Theoretically, the concept of divorce held a distaste for him, second only to his mother's prejudices. He was annoyed that Letterblair, perhaps prompted by the formidable old matriarch, should so evidently be maneuvering to draw him into the fray. After all, there were plenty of Mingott men capable of handling such legal entanglements, and as yet, he was not even a Mingott by marriage.

He waited for the senior partner to elaborate. Letterblair unlocked a drawer and produced a packet of documents. "If you will run your eye over these papers..."

Archer frowned, his composure slipping. "I beg your pardon, sir; but precisely because of the prospective relationship, I should prefer your consulting Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood."

Letterblair looked surprised, perhaps slightly offended. It was highly unusual for a junior partner to reject such an opening.

"I respect your scruple, sir," Letterblair bowed smoothly. "But in this specific instance, I believe true delicacy requires you to do as I ask. Indeed, the suggestion is not mine, but that of Mrs. Manson Mingott and her son. I have seen Lovell Mingott, and also Mr. Welland. They all named you."

Archer felt his temper rising. For the past fortnight, he had drifted languidly through events, allowing May's radiant nature to obliterate the pressing claims of the Mingott clan. But this summons from old Mrs. Mingott roused him to a sharp awareness of what the family believed they had the right to exact from a prospective son-in-law. He chafed at the role.

"Her uncles ought to deal with this," he said firmly.

"They have," Letterblair replied. "The matter has been thoroughly discussed by the family. They are opposed to the Countess's idea; yet she is firm and insists on a legal opinion."

The young man fell silent, refusing to open the packet in his hand.

"Does she intend to marry again?"

"I believe it is suggested, but she denies it."

"Then—"

"Will you oblige me, Mr. Archer, by first looking through these papers? Afterward, when we have discussed the case, I will give you my opinion."

Reluctantly, Archer withdrew with the unwelcome documents. Since their last meeting, he had half-consciously collaborated with fate in shedding the burden of Madame Olenska. His solitary hour by the firelight had created a momentary intimacy, shattered providentially by the Duke of St. Austrey's intrusion with Mrs. Lemuel Struthers and the Countess's joyous greeting of them. Two days later, he had witnessed the comedy of her reinstatement in the van der Luydens' favor. He had told himself, with a touch of tartness, that a lady who knew how to thank all-powerful elderly gentlemen so effectively for a bunch of flowers did not require the private consolations or public championship of a young man of his limited stature. Viewing the matter in this light simplified his own position and surprisingly restored faith in his domestic virtues. He could not imagine May Welland, in any conceivable emergency, hawking her private difficulties or lavishing her confidences on strange men. She had never seemed finer or fairer than in the week that followed. He had even yielded to her wish for a long engagement, since she had offered the one disarming answer to his plea for haste.

"You know, when it comes to the point, your parents have always let you have your way ever since you were a little girl," he had argued.

And she had answered, with her clearest look: "Yes; and that's what makes it so hard to refuse the very last thing they'll ever ask of me as a little girl."

That was the old New York note; that was the kind of assurance he desired to hear from his wife. If one breathed the New York air habitually, there were times when anything less crystalline seemed stifling.

The papers he retreated to read told him little in terms of facts, yet they plunged him into an atmosphere where he choked and spluttered. They consisted mainly of an exchange of letters between Count Olenski's solicitors and a French legal firm to whom the Countess had applied for the settlement of her financial situation. There was also a short letter from the Count to his wife. After reading it, Newland Archer rose, jammed the papers back into their envelope, and reentered Mr. Letterblair's office.

"Here are the letters, sir. If you wish, I'll see Madame Olenska," he said in a constrained voice.

"Thank you—thank you, Mr. Archer. Come and dine with me tonight if you're free, and we'll go into the matter afterward: in case you wish to call on our client tomorrow."

Newland Archer walked straight home that afternoon. It was a winter evening of transparent clearness, with an innocent young moon hovering above the rooftops. He felt a desperate need to fill his soul's lungs with the pure radiance of the night, wishing to exchange no words with anyone until he and Mr. Letterblair were closeted together after dinner. It was impossible to decide otherwise; he must see Madame Olenska himself rather than allow her secrets to be bared to other eyes. A great wave of compassion had swept away his indifference and impatience. She stood before him as an exposed and pitiful figure, to be saved at all costs from further wounding herself in her mad plunges against fate.

He remembered what she had told him of Mrs. Welland's request to be spared whatever was "unpleasant" in her history, and he winced at the thought that it was perhaps this attitude of mind that kept the New York air so pure. "Are we only Pharisees after all?" he wondered, puzzled by the effort to reconcile his instinctive disgust at human vileness with his equally instinctive pity for human frailty.

For the first time, he perceived how elementary his own principles truly were. He passed for a young man unafraid of risks, knowing that his secret love affair with poor, silly Mrs. Thorley Rushworth had not been too secret to invest him with a becoming air of adventure. But Mrs. Rushworth was "that kind of woman"—foolish, vain, clandestine by nature, far more attracted by the secrecy and peril of the affair than by the charms and qualities he possessed. When the fact had dawned on him, it had nearly broken his heart; now, it seemed the redeeming feature of the case. The affair, in short, had been of the kind most young men of his age had experienced, emerging from it with calm consciences and an undisturbed belief in the abysmal distinction between the women one loved and respected and those one merely enjoyed—and pitied. In this view, they were sedulously abetted by their mothers, aunts, and other elderly female relatives, who all shared Mrs. Archer's belief that when "such things happened," it was undoubtedly foolish of the man, but somehow always criminal of the woman. All the elderly ladies Archer knew regarded any woman who loved imprudently as necessarily unscrupulous and designing, viewing the mere simple-minded man as powerless in her clutches. The only solution, they believed, was to persuade him, as early as possible, to marry a nice girl and then trust to her to look after him.

In the complicated old European communities, Archer began to guess, love-problems might be less simple and less easily classified. Rich, idle, and ornamental societies must produce many more such situations; and there might even be one in which a woman, naturally sensitive and aloof, would yet, from the force of circumstances, from sheer defenselessness and loneliness, be drawn into a tie inexcusable by conventional standards.

Upon reaching home, he wrote a line to the Countess Olenska, asking at what hour the next day she could receive him, and dispatched it by a messenger boy. The boy returned shortly with a message that she was going to Skuytercliff the next morning to stay over Sunday with the van der Luydens, but that he would find her alone that evening after dinner. The note was written on a rather untidy half-sheet, without date or address, but her hand was firm and free. He was amused at the idea of her week ending in the stately solitude of Skuytercliff, but immediately afterward felt that there, of all places, she would most keenly feel the chill of minds rigorously averted from the "unpleasant."

He arrived at Mr. Letterblair's punctually at seven, glad of the pretext to excuse himself soon after dinner. He had already formed his own opinion from the papers entrusted to him and did not particularly wish to delve deeper with his senior partner. Mr. Letterblair was a widower, and they dined alone, copiously and slowly, in a dark, shabby room hung with yellowing prints of "The Death of Chatham" and "The Coronation of Napoleon." On the sideboard, between fluted Sheraton knife-cases, stood a decanter of Haut Brion and another of the old Lanning port—a gift from a client that the wastrel Tom Lanning had sold off a year or two before his mysterious and discreditable death in San Francisco, an incident less publicly humiliating to the family than the sale of the cellar.

After a velvety oyster soup came shad and cucumbers, then a young broiled turkey with corn fritters, followed by a canvas-back with currant jelly and a celery mayonnaise. Mr. Letterblair, who lunched on a sandwich and tea, dined deliberately and deeply, insisting on his guest's doing the same. Finally, when the closing rites had been accomplished, the cloth was removed, cigars were lit, and Mr. Letterblair, leaning back in his chair and pushing the port westward, said, spreading his back agreeably to the coal fire behind him: "The whole family is against a divorce. And I think rightly."

Archer instantly felt himself on the other side of the argument. "But why, sir? If there ever was a case—"

"Well—what's the use? *She's* here—he's there; the Atlantic is between them. She'll never get back a dollar more of her money than what he's voluntarily returned to her: their damned heathen marriage settlements take precious good care of that. As things go over there, Olenski has acted generously; he might have turned her out without a penny."

The young man knew this and remained silent.

"I understand, though," Mr. Letterblair continued, "that she attaches no importance to the money. Therefore, as the family says, why not let well enough alone?"

Archer had gone to the house an hour earlier in full agreement with Mr. Letterblair's view; but put into words by this selfish, well-fed, and supremely indifferent old man, it suddenly became the Pharisaic voice of a society wholly absorbed in barricading itself against the unpleasant.

"I think that's for her to decide."

"H'm—have you considered the consequences if she decides for divorce?"

"You mean the threat in her husband's letter? What weight would that carry? It's no more than the vague charge of an angry blackguard."

"Yes; but it might make some unpleasant talk if he really defends the suit."

"Unpleasant—!" said Archer explosively.

Mr. Letterblair looked at him from under enquiring eyebrows, and the young man, aware of the uselessness of trying to explain what was in his mind, bowed acquiescently while his senior continued: "Divorce is always unpleasant."

"You agree with me?" Mr. Letterblair resumed, after a waiting silence.

"Naturally," said Archer.

"Well, then, I may count on you; the Mingotts may count on you; to use your influence against the idea?"

Archer hesitated. "I can't pledge myself till I've seen the Countess Olenska," he said at length.

"Mr. Archer, I don't understand you. Do you want to marry into a family with a scandalous divorce-s

In the old days of New York, dinner was served at seven, and the custom of post-prandial calls, though mocked by the younger set, remained the law of the land. As the young man strolled up Fifth Avenue from Waverley Place, the broad avenue was nearly empty save for a cluster of carriages parked before the Reggie Chiverses' residence—where a dinner was being held for the Duke—and the solitary figure of an elderly gentleman in a heavy overcoat and muffler ascending a brownstone stoop to vanish into a gas-lit hall. Thus, as Archer crossed Washington Square, he noted that Mr. du Lac was visiting his cousins, the Dagonets, and turning the corner of West Tenth Street, he spotted Mr. Skipworth of his own firm, clearly en route to the Miss Lannings. A short distance further, Beaufort emerged onto his doorstep, silhouetted against a brilliant flood of light, descended to his private brougham, and rolled away toward a destination shrouded in mystery and likely silence. It was not an Opera night, nor was anyone hosting a party; therefore, Beaufort's outing was undoubtedly clandestine. Archer mentally linked it to a small house beyond Lexington Avenue, recently adorned with beribboned window curtains and flower boxes, before whose freshly painted door the canary-colored brougham of Miss Fanny Ring was often seen waiting.

Beyond the small, slippery pyramid that constituted Mrs. Archer's world lay the almost unmapped quarter inhabited by artists, musicians, and "those who write." These scattered fragments of humanity had never sought to merge with the social structure. Despite their eccentricities, they were largely respectable, yet they preferred isolation. Medora Manson, in her prosperous days, had attempted to inaugurate a "literary salon," but it quickly faded due to the reluctance of the literary crowd to frequent it. Others had tried; there was the household of the Blenkers—an intense, voluble mother and three blowsy daughters who mimicked her—where one might meet Edwin Booth, Patti, William Winter, the new Shakespearian actor George Rignold, and various magazine editors and critics.

Mrs. Archer and her circle felt a distinct timidity regarding these individuals. They were odd, uncertain, and possessed backgrounds in their lives and minds that remained obscure. Literature and art were deeply respected in the Archer set, and Mrs. Archer was always eager to remind her children how much more agreeable and cultivated society had been when it included figures like Washington Irving, Fitz-Greene Halleck, and the poet of "The Culprit Fay." The most celebrated authors of that generation had been "gentlemen"; perhaps the unknown successors shared their sentiments, but their origins, appearances, hairstyles, and intimate connections with the stage and the Opera rendered any traditional New York criterion inapplicable to them.

"When I was a girl," Mrs. Archer used to say, "we knew everyone between the Battery and Canal Street; and only the people one knew owned carriages. It was perfectly easy to place anyone then; now one cannot tell, and I prefer not to try."

Only old Catherine Mingott, with her lack of moral prejudices and a parvenu indifference to subtle distinctions, might have bridged the divide; but she had never opened a book or examined a painting, caring for music only because it recalled gala nights at the Italiens during her triumphs at the Tuileries. Possibly Beaufort, who matched her in daring, would have succeeded in forging a union; but his grand house and silk-stockinged footmen were obstacles to informal sociability. Moreover, he was as illiterate as old Mrs. Mingott and regarded "fellows who write" as mere paid purveyors of rich men's pleasures, a view never questioned by anyone wealthy enough to influence his opinion.

Newland Archer had been aware of these divisions since childhood, accepting them as part of the fabric of his universe. He knew of societies where painters, poets, novelists, scientists, and great actors were as sought after as Dukes; he had often imagined living in the intimacy of drawing-rooms dominated by the conversation of Mérimée, Thackeray, Browning, or William Morris. Yet such things were inconceivable in New York and unsettling to contemplate. Archer knew most of the "fellows who write," the musicians, and the painters; he met them at the Century Club or at the emerging musical and theatrical societies. He enjoyed them there, though he grew bored at the Blenkers', where they were mingled with fervid, dowdy women who passed them around like captured curiosities. Even after his most stimulating conversations with Ned Winsett, he always departed with the feeling that if his world was small, so was theirs, and that the only way to enlarge either was to reach a stage of manners where they would naturally merge.

He was reminded of this while trying to picture the society in which the Countess Olenska had lived, suffered, and perhaps tasted mysterious joys. He recalled with amusement how she had told him that her grandmother Mingott and the Wellands objected to her living in a "Bohemian" quarter given over to "people who write." It was not the peril but the poverty her family disliked; however, that nuance escaped her, and she suspected they considered literature compromising.

She herself harbored no fears, and the books scattered about her drawing-room—a space where books were usually deemed "out of place"—though chiefly works of fiction, had whetted Archer's interest with new names like Paul Bourget, Huysmans, and the Goncourt brothers. Ruminating on these matters as he approached her door, he became once more conscious of the curious way in which she reversed his values, and of the necessity of imagining conditions incredibly different from any he knew if he were to be of use in her present difficulty.

Nastasia opened the door, smiling mysteriously. On the bench in the hall lay a sable-lined overcoat, a folded opera hat of dull silk with a gold "J. B." on the lining, and a white silk muffler; there was no mistaking that these costly articles belonged to Julius Beaufort.

Archer felt anger rising within him, so intense that he nearly scribbled a word on his card and departed. Then he remembered that in his letters to Madame Olenska, he had been restrained by excessive discretion from stating his desire to see her privately. He had therefore no one to blame but himself if she had opened her doors to other visitors, and he entered the drawing-room with a dogged determination to make Beaufort feel obstructed and to outstay him.

The banker stood leaning against the mantelpiece, draped with an old embroidery held in place by brass candelabra containing church candles of yellowish wax. He had thrust his chest out, supporting his shoulders against the mantel and resting his weight on one large patent-leather foot. As Archer entered, he was smiling and looking down upon his hostess, who sat on a sofa placed at right angles to the chimney. A table banked with flowers formed a screen behind her, and against the orchids and azaleas—which Archer recognized as tributes from the Beaufort hot-houses—Madame Olenska sat half-reclined, her head propped on a hand, her wide sleeve leaving her arm bare to the elbow.

It was customary for ladies receiving in the evenings to wear "simple dinner dresses": a close-fitting armor of whale-boned silk, slightly open at the neck with lace ruffles filling the gap, and tight sleeves with a flounce uncovering just enough wrist to display an Etruscan gold bracelet or a velvet band. But Madame Olenska, heedless of tradition, was attired in a long robe of red velvet bordered about the chin and down the front with glossy black fur. Archer remembered, on his last visit to Paris, seeing a portrait by the new painter Carolus Duran, whose works were the sensation of the Salon, depicting a lady in one of these bold, sheath-like robes with her chin nestling in fur. There was something perverse and provocative in the notion of fur worn in the evening within a heated drawing-room, and in the combination of a muffled throat and bare arms; yet the effect was undeniably pleasing.

"Lord love us—three whole days at Skuytercliff!" Beaufort exclaimed in his loud, sneering voice as Archer entered. "You'd better take all your furs, and a hot-water-bottle."

"Why? Is the house so cold?" she asked, holding out her left hand to Archer in a manner mysteriously suggesting she expected him to kiss it.

"No; but the missus is," Beaufort said, nodding carelessly to the young man.

"But I thought her so kind. She came herself to invite me. Granny says I must certainly go."

"Granny would, of course. And I say it's a shame you're going to miss the little oyster supper I planned for you at Delmonico's next Sunday, with Campanini and Scalchi and a lot of jolly people."

She looked doubtfully from the banker to Archer.

"Ah—that does tempt me! Except the other evening at Mrs. Struthers's, I haven't met a single artist since I've been here."

"Painters? I know one or two painters, very good fellows, that I could bring to see you if you'd allow me," Archer said boldly.

"Painters? Are there painters in New York?" Beaufort asked, in a tone implying that there could be none since he did not buy their pictures; and Madame Olenska said to Archer, with her grave smile, "That would be charming. But I was really thinking of dramatic artists, singers, actors, musicians. My husband's house was always full of them."

She said the words "my husband" as if no sinister associations were connected to them, and in a tone that seemed almost to sigh over the lost delights of her married life. Archer looked at her perplexedly, wondering if it were lightness or dissimulation that enabled her to touch so easily on the past at the very moment she was risking her reputation to break with it.

"I do think," she continued, addressing both men, "that the *imprévu* adds to one's enjoyment. It is perhaps a mistake to see the same people every day."

"It is confoundedly dull, anyhow; New York is dying of dullness," Beaufort grumbled. "And when I try to liven it up for you, you go back on me. Come—think better of it! Sunday is your last chance, for Campanini leaves next week for Baltimore and Philadelphia; and I have a private room, a Steinway, and they'll sing all night for me."

"How delicious! May I think it over, and write to you tomorrow morning?"

She spoke amiably, yet with the slightest hint of dismissal in her voice. Beaufort evidently felt it, and being unused to dismissals, stood staring at her with an obstinate line between his eyes.

"Why not now?"

"It is too serious a question to decide at this late hour."

"Do you call it late?"

She returned his glance coolly. "Yes; because I still have to discuss business with Mr. Archer for a little while."

"Ah," Beaufort snapped. There was no appeal in her tone, and with a slight shrug he recovered his composure, took her hand, which he kissed with practiced ease, and calling out from the threshold: "I say, Newland, if you can persuade the Countess to stay in town, of course you're included in the supper," left the room with his heavy, important step.

For a moment, Archer fancied that Mr. Letterblair must have informed her of his coming, but the irrelevance of her next remark made him change his mind.

"You know painters, then? You live in their milieu?" she asked, her eyes full of interest.

"Oh, not exactly. I don't know that the arts have a milieu here; they are more like a very thinly settled outskirt."

"But you care for such things?"

"Immensely. When I am in Paris or London, I never miss an exhibition. I try to keep up."

She looked down at the tip of the little satin boot that peeped from her long draperies.

"I used to care immensely too; my life was full of such things. But now I want to try not to."

"You want to try not to?"

"Yes: I want to cast off all my old life, to become just like everybody else here."

Archer reddened. "You'll never be like everybody else," he said.

She raised her straight eyebrows slightly. "Ah, don't say that. If you knew how I hate to be different!"

Her face had grown as sombre as a tragic mask. She leaned forward, clasping her knee in her thin hands, and gazing away from him into remote, dark distances.

"I want to get away from it all," she insisted.

He waited a moment and cleared his throat. "I know. Mr. Letterblair has told me."

"Ah?"

"That is the reason I have come. He asked me to—you see, I am in the firm."

She looked slightly surprised, and then her eyes brightened. "You mean you can manage it for me? I can talk to you instead of Mr. Letterblair? Oh, that will be so much easier!"

Her tone touched him, and his confidence grew with his self-satisfaction. He perceived that she had spoken of business to Beaufort simply to get rid of him, and to have routed Beaufort was something of a triumph.

"I am here to talk about it," he repeated.

She sat silent, her head still propped by the arm resting on the back of the sofa. Her face looked pale and extinguished, as if dimmed by the rich red of her dress. She struck Archer, suddenly, as a pathetic and even pitiful figure.

"Now we are coming to hard facts," he thought, conscious within himself of the same instinctive recoil that he had so often criticized in his mother and her contemporaries. How little practice he had had in dealing with unusual situations! Their very vocabulary was unfamiliar to him, seeming to belong to fiction and the stage. In the face of what was coming, he felt as awkward and embarrassed as a boy.

After a pause, Madame Olenska broke out with unexpected vehemence: "I want to be free

It was a crowded night at Wallack's theatre, the air thick with the scent of lilies and the rustle of silk. The stage was alive with "The Shaughraun," a production that had swept the city, featuring the formidable Dion Boucicault and the beloved English pair, Harry Montague and Ada Dyas. While the gallery audiences roared with unbridled enthusiasm for every hackneyed sentiment and clap-trap situation, those seated in the plush boxes offered only polite smiles, finding delight in the familiar rhythms of the play.

Yet, there was one moment that held the entire house in a suspended breath. It was the final scene: Harry Montague, having delivered a parting so sad and monosyllabic that it seemed to drain the color from the room, turned to leave. Miss Dyas stood near the mantelpiece, her tall figure draped in a simple gray cashmere dress devoid of fashionable trimmings, flowing elegantly to the floor. A narrow black velvet ribbon framed her neck, its ends trailing down her back. As her wooer stepped away, she leaned her arms against the shelf, burying her face in her hands. On the threshold, Montague paused, stealing a glance back. He reached out, lifted one end of the velvet ribbon, pressed a kiss to it, and vanished into the darkness without a sound. The curtain fell on this silent, aching goodbye.

For Newland Archer, this scene was the sole reason for his attendance. He found in Montague and Dyas's reticent sorrow a depth that surpassed even the finest performances he had witnessed in Paris or London. That evening, however, the scene carried a weight far heavier than usual. It triggered a memory he could not quite name: his own leave-taking from Madame Olenska, a conversation from a week prior that had left him unsettled.

The parallels between the two situations were as elusive as the resemblance between the actors and the people involved. Archer was no romantic hero like Montague, nor was Miss Dyas the vivid, striking Ellen Olenska. Furthermore, they were not lovers parting in heartbreak; they were client and lawyer separating after a discussion that had painted a grim picture of the client's precarious position. So, what stirred the young man's heart with such retrospective excitement? It was Madame Olenska's unique ability to suggest tragic possibilities that lay beyond the mundane flow of daily life. She possessed a mysterious quality, perhaps rooted in her exotic background, that made her seem inherently dramatic. Archer had always believed that fate played a smaller role than innate tendency in shaping lives, and he saw this clearly in her. She appeared to be a woman destined for drama, someone to whom things would inevitably happen regardless of her efforts to avoid them. The startling fact was her lack of surprise; she seemed plucked from a maelstrom, taking for granted the very storms she had once rebelled against.

Leaving her that afternoon, Archer had been convinced that Count Olenski's accusations were justified. The mysterious "secretary" from her past had likely been rewarded for his role in her escape. The conditions she had fled were intolerable; she was young, frightened, and desperate. Naturally, she would be grateful to her rescuer. The tragedy, as Archer had explained to her, was that in the eyes of the law and society, her gratitude placed her on equal footing with her abominable husband. He had made this clear, as he was bound to do, and had also shown her that simple-hearted New York was the least forgiving place for such a situation.

Witnessing her resigned acceptance had been excruciating. He felt drawn to her by a tangled mix of jealousy and pity, as if her confessed error had placed her at his mercy, simultaneously humbling and endearing her. He was relieved she had confided in him rather than facing the cold scrutiny of Mr. Letterblair or the awkward gazes of her family. Immediately, he took it upon himself to assure them that she had abandoned her pursuit of a divorce, basing her decision on the futility of the legal battle. With immense relief, the family turned their eyes away from the "unpleasantness" she had spared them.

"I was sure Newland would manage it," Mrs. Welland had said proudly of her future son-in-law. Old Mrs. Mingott, summoning him for a private interview, had congratulated him on his cleverness, adding impatiently, "Silly goose! I told her myself what nonsense it was. Wanting to pass herself off as Ellen Mingott and an old maid, when she has the luck to be a married woman and a Countess!"

These interactions had rendered the memory of his last talk with Madame Olenska so vivid that as the curtain fell, Archer's eyes filled with tears, and he rose to leave the theatre.

Turning toward the side of the house, he spotted the lady occupying his thoughts seated in a box with the Beauforts, Lawrence Lefferts, and a few others. He had avoided being alone with her since their evening together, yet now their eyes met. As Mrs. Beaufort recognized him and made her languid gesture of invitation, there was no choice but to enter the box.

Beaufort and Lefferts made way for him. After exchanging a few words with Mrs. Beaufort, who preferred to look beautiful rather than speak, Archer settled behind Madame Olenska. The only other occupant was Mr. Sillerton Jackson, whispering confidentially to Mrs. Beaufort about Mrs. Lemuel Struthers's recent reception. Under the cover of this narrative, Madame Olenska turned and spoke in a low voice.

"Do you think," she asked, glancing toward the stage, "he will send her a bunch of yellow roses tomorrow morning?"

Archer flushed, his heart leaping with surprise. He had visited her only twice, sending a box of yellow roses each time without a card. She had never acknowledged the sender, assuming perhaps she didn't know. Now, her sudden recognition of the gift, linking it to the tender farewell on stage, filled him with an agitated pleasure.

"I was thinking of that too," he admitted. "I was going to leave the theatre to take the picture away with me."

To his surprise, her cheeks flushed, reluctantly and dusky. She looked down at the mother-of-pearl opera-glass in her gloved hands and, after a pause, asked, "What do you do while May is away?"

"I stick to my work," he replied, faintly annoyed by the question.

Indeed, the Wellands had departed the previous week for St. Augustine, a tradition born of Mr. Welland's bronchial susceptibility. He was a mild, silent man with many habits and no opinions, requiring his wife and daughter to accompany him to preserve the domestic order essential to his peace of mind. His sons, bound by their winter practices, joined him for Easter, but Archer could not discuss the necessity of May's departure. The Mingott physician's reputation relied on the fact that Mr. Welland had never suffered pneumonia, making his insistence on the southern trip inflexible. Archer would have loved to join the travelers for sunshine and boating with his betrothed, but custom and convention bound him. To suggest a holiday in mid-winter would be deemed frivolous by the entire Mingott clan, so he accepted May's departure with the resignation he knew was necessary for married life.

He was aware that Madame Olenska watched him through lowered lids. "I have done what you wished—what you advised," she said abruptly.

"Ah—I'm glad," he returned, embarrassed by her bringing up the subject at such a moment.

"I understand—that you were right," she continued, breathless. "But sometimes life is difficult... perplexing..."

"I know."

"And I wanted to tell you that I *do* feel you were right; and that I'm grateful to you," she concluded, lifting her opera-glass quickly to her eyes just as the box door opened and Beaufort's resonant voice interrupted them.

Archer stood, left the box, and exited the theatre.

Only the day before, he had received a letter from May Welland, written with characteristic candor. She asked him to "be kind to Ellen" in their absence, noting that she liked and admired him, though she remained lonely and unhappy. May wrote that her grandmother and uncle Lovell Mingott misunderstood her, thinking her worldly and fond of society, whereas May saw that New York seemed dull to her, accustomed to wonders like great music, picture shows, and celebrities. "I think she's been used to lots of things we haven't got," May had written. "And I can quite see that New York must seem dull to her... but I can see that you're almost the only person in New York who can talk to her about what she really cares for."

His wise May—he had loved her for that letter! Yet, he had not meant to act on it. He was too busy, and as an engaged man, he did not wish to play too conspicuously the part of Madame Olenska's champion. He suspected she knew how to care for herself better than the ingenuous May imagined. She had Beaufort at her feet, Mr. van der Luyden hovering protectively, and candidates like Lawrence Lefferts waiting in the wings. Still, every time he saw her or spoke with her, he felt that May's innocence was almost a gift of divination. Ellen Olenska was lonely, and she was unhappy.

Stepping out into the cavernous lobby of the club, Archer collided with Ned Winsett, the sole member of Janey's circle of "clever people" with whom he felt compelled to dig beneath the surface of ordinary club chatter and chop-house gossip. Across the room, Archer had spotted Winsett's familiar, shabby, round-shouldered silhouette, his gaze fixed intently on the Beaufort box. The two men exchanged a handshake, and Winsett, ever the pragmatist, suggested a beer at a small German eatery around the corner. Archer, however, was not in the mood for the kind of cynical dissection they usually indulged in there; he feigned a pressing engagement at home. Winsett, unfazed, merely shrugged, declaring, "Oh, well, so have I, for that matter. I'll be the Industrious Apprentice today too."

They strolled side by side through the bustling hall until Winsett broke the silence. "Look here," he said, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "What I'm really after is the name of the dark lady in that swell box of yours—the one with the Beauforts, wasn't it? The one your friend Lefferts seems so hopelessly smitten by."

Archer felt a flicker of annoyance he couldn't quite name. What did Ned Winsett want with Ellen Olenska's name? And why, above all, did he link her so casually to Lefferts? It was unlike Winsett to display such specific curiosity, yet Archer recalled that he was, after all, a journalist.

"It's not for an interview, I hope?" Archer laughed, trying to deflect the tension.

"Well—not for the press; just for myself," Winsett replied smoothly. "The truth is, she's a neighbor of mine. A queer quarter for such a beauty to settle in. She's been awfully kind to my little boy, who fell down her steps chasing his kitten and gave himself a nasty cut. She rushed in bareheaded, carrying him in her arms, his knee beautifully bandaged, and was so sympathetic and radiant that my wife was completely dazzled and forgot to ask her name."

A warm glow spread through Archer's chest. The tale itself was unremarkable; any woman would have acted so for a neighbor's child. Yet, it felt distinctly like Ellen to rush in bareheaded, cradling the boy, and to possess such a luminous presence that it rendered Mrs. Winsett speechless.

"That is the Countess Olenska—a granddaughter of old Mrs. Mingott's," Archer supplied.

"Whew—a Countess!" Winsett whistled. "Well, I didn't know Countesses were so neighborly. The Mingotts certainly aren't."

"They would be, if you'd let them," Archer retorted.

"Ah, well—" It was their perennial, interminable debate regarding the obstinate unwillingness of the "clever people" to frequent the fashionable set. Both men knew there was no point in prolonging it.

"I wonder," Winsett broke off, "how a Countess happens to live in our slum?"

"Because she doesn't care a hang about where she lives—or about any of our little social sign-posts," Archer said, feeling a secret pride in his own idealized image of her.

"H'm—been in bigger places, I suppose," the other commented dryly. "Well, here's my corner."

He slouched off across Broadway, and Archer stood watching him go, musing on his final words. Ned Winsett possessed flashes of penetrating insight; they were the most fascinating aspect of him, and they always left Archer wondering why they had allowed him to accept failure so stolidly at an age when most men were still struggling.

Archer knew Winsett had a wife and child, though he had never seen them. Their meetings were invariably at the Century Club or in haunts of journalists and theatrical types, like the restaurant where Winsett had proposed a drink. Winsett had hinted that his wife was an invalid—a claim that might be true, or merely a euphemism for a lack of social grace, evening attire, or both. Winsett himself harbored a savage abhorrence of social observances. Archer, who dressed in the evening because he found it cleaner and more comfortable, viewed Winsett's attitude as part of the tedious "Bohemian" pose that made fashionable people, who changed clothes without comment and never harped on the number of servants, seem so simple and self-assured. Nevertheless, Winsett always stimulated him. Whenever Archer caught sight of the journalist's lean, bearded face and melancholy eyes, he would drag him out of his corner for a long, stimulating talk.

Winsett was not a journalist by choice. He was a pure man of letters, untimely born in a world that held no need for his talents. After publishing a single volume of brief, exquisite literary appreciations—one hundred and twenty copies sold, thirty given away, and the rest destroyed by the publishers to make room for marketable material—he had abandoned his true calling. He now took a sub-editorial job on a women's weekly, where fashion plates and paper patterns alternated with New England love stories and advertisements for temperance drinks.

On the subject of "Hearth-fires"—the paper's name—he was inexhaustibly entertaining. But beneath his humor lurked the sterile bitterness of a young man who had tried and given up. His conversation always forced Archer to measure his own life against Winsett's and feel how little it contained. Yet Winsett's life contained even less. While their shared fund of intellectual curiosities made their talks exhilarating, their exchange of views usually remained within the limits of a pensive dilettantism.

"The fact is, life isn't much a fit for either of us," Winsett had once said. "I'm down and out; nothing to be done about it. I've got only one ware to produce, and there's no market for it here, and won't be in my time. But you're free and you're well-off. Why don't *you* get into touch? There's only one way to do it: to go into politics."

Archer threw his head back and laughed. There, instantly, appeared the unbridgeable chasm between men like Winsett and Archer's own kind. Everyone in polite society knew that in America, "a gentleman couldn't go into politics." But since he could hardly say that to Winsett, he answered evasively: "Look at the career of the honest man in American politics! They don't want us."

"Who's 'they'? Why don't you all get together and be 'they' yourselves?"

Archer's laugh lingered on his lips as a slightly condescending smile. It was useless to prolong the discussion; everyone knew the melancholy fate of the few gentlemen who had risked their clean linen in municipal or state politics in New York. The day was past when such things were possible. The country was now in the possession of the bosses and the emigrants, and decent people were forced to retreat to sport or culture.

"Culture! Yes—if we had it! But there are just a few little local patches, dying out here and there for lack of... well, hoeing and cross-fertilizing: the last remnants of the old European tradition your forebears brought with them. But you're in a pitiful little minority. You've got no center, no competition, no audience. You're like the pictures on the walls of a deserted house: 'The Portrait of a Gentleman.' You'll never amount to anything, any of you, till you roll up your sleeves and get right down into the muck. That, or emigrate... God! If I could emigrate..."

Archer mentally shrugged his shoulders and turned the conversation back to books, where Winsett, if uncertain, was always interesting. Emigrate! As if a gentleman could abandon his own country! One could no more do that than roll up one's sleeves and go down into the muck. A gentleman simply stayed at home and abstained. But you couldn't make a man like Winsett see that; and that was why the New York of literary clubs and exotic restaurants, though a first glance made it seem like a kaleidoscope, ultimately revealed itself to be a smaller box with a more monotonous pattern than the assembled atoms of Fifth Avenue.

The next morning, Archer scouring the town in vain for more yellow roses, arrived late at the office. He perceived that his tardiness made no difference to anyone and was filled with sudden exasperation at the elaborate futility of his existence. Why should he not be, at that very moment, on the sands of St. Augustine with May Welland? No one was deceived by his pretense of professional activity. In old-fashioned legal firms like Mr. Letterblair's, which managed large estates and "conservative" investments, there were always two or three young men, fairly well-off and devoid of professional ambition, who sat at their desks accomplishing trivial tasks or simply reading newspapers for a certain number of hours each day. Though it was considered proper for them to have an occupation, the crude fact of money-making was still regarded as derogatory, and the law, being a profession, was accounted a more gentlemanly pursuit than business. But none of these young men had much hope of really advancing, nor any earnest desire to do so; and over many of them, the green mold of the perfunctory was already perceptibly spreading.

It made Archer shiver to think that it might be spreading over him too. He had, to be sure, other tastes and interests; he spent his vacations in European travel, cultivated the "clever people" May spoke of, and generally tried to "keep up," as he had somewhat wistfully told Madame Olenska. But once he was married, what would become of this narrow margin of life in which his real experiences were lived? He had seen enough of other young men who had dreamed his dream, perhaps less ardently, and who had gradually sunk into the placid and luxurious routine of their elders.

From the office, he sent a note by messenger to Madame Olenska, asking if he might call that afternoon and begging her to let him find a reply at his club. But at the club he found nothing, nor did he receive any letter the following day. This unexpected silence mortified him beyond reason. Though the next morning he saw a glorious cluster of yellow roses behind a florist's window-pane, he left them there. It was only on the third morning that he received a line by post from the Countess Olenska. To his surprise, it was dated from Skuytercliff, whither the van der Luydens had promptly retreated after putting the Duke on his steamer.

"I ran away," the note began abruptly, skipping all usual preliminaries, "the day after I saw you at the play, and these kind friends have taken me in. I wanted to be quiet, and think things over. You were right in telling me how kind they were; I feel myself so safe here. I wish that you were with us." She ended with a conventional "Yours sincerely," and made no allusion to the date of her return.

The tone of the note surprised the young man. What was Madame Olenska running away from, and why did she feel the need to be safe? His first thought was of some dark menace from abroad; then he reflected that he did not know her epistolary style, and that it might run to picturesque exaggeration. Women always exaggerated; and moreover, she was not wholly at ease in English, often speaking as if translating from French. "Je me suis évadée"—put in that way, the opening sentence immediately suggested she might merely have wanted to escape a boring round of engagements, which was very likely true, for he judged her to be capricious and easily wearied of the pleasure of the moment.

It amused him to think of the van der Luydens having carried her off to Skuytercliff on a second visit, this time for an indefinite period. The doors of Skuytercliff were rarely and grudgingly opened to visitors, and a chilly weekend was the most ever offered to the few thus privileged. But Archer had seen, on his last visit to Paris, the delicious play of Labiche, "Le Voyage de M. Perrichon," and he remembered M. Perrichon's dogged and undiscouraged attachment to the young man whom he had pulled out of the glacier. The van der Luydens had rescued Madame Olenska from a doom almost as icy; and though there were many other reasons for being attracted to her, Archer knew that beneath them all lay the gentle and obstinate determination to go on rescuing her.

He felt a distinct disappointment upon learning she was away, and almost immediately remembered that only the day before he had refused an invitation to spend the following Sunday with the Reggie Chiverses at their house on the Hudson, a few miles below Skuytercliff. He had had his fill long ago of the noisy, friendly parties at Highbank, with coasting, ice-boating, sleighing, long tramps in the snow, and a general flavor of mild flirting and milder practical jokes. He had just received a box of new books from his London bookseller and had preferred the prospect of a quiet Sunday at home with his spoils. But now, he went into the club writing-room, wrote a hurried telegram, and told the servant to send it immediately. He knew that Mrs. Reggie didn't object to her visitors suddenly changing their minds, and that there was always a room to spare in her elastic house.

Friday evening found Newland Archer arriving at the Chiverses' estate, and Saturday unfolded with the meticulous, dutiful rhythm of a weekend at Highbank. He began the day with a brisk ride in the ice-boat alongside his hostess and the season's most spirited guests. By afternoon, he accompanied Reggie on a tour of the farm, spending hours in the opulent stables listening to grandiloquent lectures on equine excellence. Following tea, he found himself cornered in the fire-lit hall by a young woman who had once claimed her heart was shattered by his engagement, only to pivot eagerly to her own romantic prospects. As the clock struck midnight, the festivities escalated into absurdity: he helped place a goldfish in a guest's bed, dressed a nervous aunt's brother as a burglar in the bathroom, and joined a chaotic pillow fight that swept from the nurseries down to the basement. Yet, by Sunday lunch, the spell of social obligation broke. Borrowing a cutter, Archer drove across the snow to Skuytercliff.

The house at Skuytercliff was a legend, often mistaken for an Italian villa. Built by Mr. van der Luyden in his youth after his grand tour, it was a massive wooden structure painted pale green and white, featuring a Corinthian portico and fluted pilasters. From its high perch, terraces lined with urns cascaded like a steel engraving toward a small, irregular lake, while weedless lawns dotted with specimen trees rolled down to the hollow where the original Patroon's stone house sat. Against the bleak winter sky, the villa loomed with a grim majesty; even in summer, nature seemed afraid to approach its formidable front.

As Archer rang the bell, the sound echoed through a mausoleum. The butler's response was one of startled disbelief, as if summoned from the grave. Fortunately, Archer was of the family, and the butler quickly informed him that the Countess Olenska had departed for afternoon service with Mrs. van der Luyden just forty-five minutes prior. The butler suggested Mr. van der Luyden might be napping or reading the *Evening Post*, offering to eavesdrop in the library to confirm. Archer politely declined, thanking him and stepping out to meet the ladies, leaving the butler to close the door with a sigh of relief.

Striking out through the park, Archer kept to the high road, knowing Mrs. van der Luyden never walked. Soon, however, he spotted a slender figure in a vibrant red cloak, a large dog bounding ahead. He hurried forward, and Madame Olenska stopped, her face lighting up.

"Ah, you've come!" she exclaimed, pulling her hand from her muff.
