Archer gazed down at the familiar spectacle with a sense of wonder, yet it struck him profoundly that life continued in the old ways while his own internal landscape had shifted entirely. Newport had been the catalyst that revealed the magnitude of this change. In New York, during the previous winter, after settling into their new greenish-yellow house with its bow-window and Pompeian vestibule, he had retreated with relief into the old routines of his office. The daily grind served as a tether to his former self, complemented by the pleasurable excitement of selecting a showy grey stepper for May's brougham and the abiding interest of arranging his new library—a project realized despite family doubts, featuring dark embossed paper, Eastlake bookcases, and "sincere" armchairs. At the *Century* and the *Knickerbocker*, he had rediscovered Winsett and the fashionable young men of his set. With hours dedicated to law, dining out, entertaining, and occasional evenings at the Opera, his life had seemed a fairly real, inevitable business.

But Newport represented an escape from duty into an atmosphere of unmitigated holiday-making. Archer had tried to persuade May to spend the summer on a remote Maine island called Mount Desert, where hardy Bostonians and Philadelphians camped in "native" cottages, enjoying enchanting scenery and a wild, trapper-like existence. Yet the Wellands always went to Newport, where they owned one of the square boxes on the cliffs. Archer could offer no good reason why he and May should not join them. As Mrs. Welland had rather tartly pointed out, it was hardly worth May wearing herself out trying on summer clothes in Paris if she were not allowed to wear them. This argument, logical and pleasant, left Archer with no answer.

May herself could not comprehend his obscure reluctance. She reminded him that he had always loved Newport in his bachelor days—an indisputable fact—and he could only profess that he was certain he would like it better now that they were together. But as he stood on the Beaufort verandah, watching the brightly peopled lawn, a shiver ran through him; he knew he would not like it at all.

It was not May's fault, poor dear. If, now and then, during their travels, they had fallen slightly out of step, harmony was restored by returning to the conditions she was used to. He had always foreseen that she would not disappoint him, and he had been right. He had married, as most young men did, because he had met a perfectly charming girl at the moment when a series of aimless sentimental adventures ended in premature disgust. She represented peace, stability, comradeship, and the steadying sense of an unescapable duty.

He could not claim to have been mistaken in his choice; she had fulfilled all his expectations. It was undoubtedly gratifying to be the husband of one of New York's handsomest and most popular young married women, especially when she was also one of the sweetest-tempered and most reasonable of wives. Archer had never been insensible to such advantages. As for the momentary madness that had fallen upon him on the eve of his marriage, he had trained himself to regard it as the last of his discarded experiments. The idea that he could ever, in his senses, have dreamed of marrying the Countess Olenska had become almost unthinkable; she remained in his memory simply as the most plaintive and poignant of a line of ghosts.

Yet, these abstractions and eliminations left his mind feeling empty and echoing, which explained why the busy, animated people on the Beaufort lawn shocked him as if they were children playing in a graveyard.

He heard a murmur of skirts beside him, and the Marchioness Manson fluttered out of the drawing-room window. As usual, she was extraordinarily festooned and bedizened, with a limp Leghorn hat anchored by windings of faded gauze and a little black velvet parasol absurdly balanced over her much larger hatbrim.

"My dear Newland, I had no idea that you and May had arrived! You yourself came only yesterday, you say? Ah, business—business—professional duties... I understand. Many husbands find it impossible to join their wives here except for the weekend." She cocked her head on one side and languished at him through screwed-up eyes. "But marriage is one long sacrifice, as I used often to remind my Ellen..."

Archer's heart stopped with a queer jerk, the same sensation he had felt once before, seeming suddenly to slam a door between himself and the outer world. But this break of continuity was brief, for he soon heard Medora answering a question he had apparently found voice to ask.

"No, I am not staying here, but with the Blenkers, in their delicious solitude at Portsmouth. Beaufort was kind enough to send his famous trotters for me this morning, so that I might have at least a glimpse of one of Regina's garden-parties; but this evening I go back to rural life. The Blenkers, dear original beings, have hired a primitive old farm-house at Portsmouth where they gather about them representative people..." She drooped slightly beneath her protecting brim and added with a faint blush, "This week Dr. Agathon Carver is holding a series of Inner Thought meetings there. A contrast indeed to this gay scene of worldly pleasure—but then I have always lived on contrasts! To me, the only death is monotony. I always say to Ellen: Beware of monotony; it's the mother of all the deadly sins. But my poor child is going through a phase of exaltation, of abhorrence of the world. You know, I suppose, that she has declined all invitations to stay at Newport, even with her grandmother Mingott? I could hardly persuade her to come with me to the Blenkers', if you will believe it! The life she leads is morbid, unnatural. Ah, if she had only listened to me when it was still possible... When the door was still open... But shall we go down and watch this absorbing match? I hear your May is one of the competitors."

Strolling toward them from the tent, Beaufort advanced over the lawn, tall, heavy, and too tightly buttoned into a London frock-coat, with one of his own orchids in his buttonhole. Archer, who had not seen him for two or three months, was struck by the change in his appearance. In the hot summer light, his floridness seemed heavy and bloated, and but for his erect, square-shouldered walk, he would have looked like an over-fed and over-dressed old man.

Rumors abounded about Beaufort. In the spring, he had gone off on a long cruise to the West Indies in his new steam-yacht, and it was reported that at various points where he had touched, a lady resembling Miss Fanny Ring had been seen in his company. The steam-yacht, built in the Clyde and fitted with tiled bathrooms and other unheard-of luxuries, was said to have cost him half a million; and the pearl necklace he had presented to his wife on his return was as magnificent as such expiatory offerings are apt to be. Beaufort's fortune was substantial enough to stand the strain, yet disquieting rumors persisted, not only in Fifth Avenue but in Wall Street. Some said he had speculated unfortunately in railways; others that he was being bled by one of the most insatiable members of her profession. To every report of threatened insolvency, Beaufort replied with fresh extravagance: building a new row of orchid-houses, purchasing a new string of race-horses, or adding a new Meissonnier or Cabanel to his picture gallery.

He advanced toward the Marchioness and Newland with his usual half-sneering smile. "Hullo, Medora! Did the trotters do their business? Forty minutes, eh? ... Well, that's not so bad, considering your nerves had to be spared." He shook hands with Archer, then, turning back with them, placed himself on Mrs. Manson's other side and said, in a low voice, a few words their companion did not catch.

The Marchioness replied with one of her queer foreign jerks and a "_Que voulez-vous?_" which deepened Beaufort's frown, but he produced a good semblance of a congratulatory smile as he glanced at Archer to say, "You know May's going to carry off the first prize."

"Ah, then it remains in the family," Medora rippled. At that moment, they reached the tent, and Mrs. Beaufort met them in a girlish cloud of mauve muslin and floating veils.

May Welland was just coming out of the tent. In her white dress, with a pale green ribbon about the waist and a wreath of ivy on her hat, she possessed the same Diana-like aloofness she had shown when entering the Beaufort ballroom on the night of her engagement. In the interval, not a thought seemed to have passed behind her eyes or a feeling through her heart. Though her husband knew she had the capacity for both, he marvelled anew at the way experience dropped away from her.

She held her bow and arrow, placing herself on the chalk-mark traced on the turf. She lifted the bow to her shoulder and took aim. The attitude was so full of classic grace that a murmur of appreciation followed her appearance, and Archer felt the glow of proprietorship that so often cheated him into momentary well-being. Her rivals—Mrs. Reggie Chivers, the Merry girls, and divers rosy Thorleys, Dagonets, and Mingotts—stood behind her in a lovely, anxious group, brown heads and golden bent above the scores, with pale muslins and flower-wreathed hats mingling in a tender rainbow. All were young and pretty, bathed in summer bloom, but none possessed the nymph-like ease of his wife, who, with tense muscles and a happy frown, bent her soul upon some feat of strength.

"Gad," Archer heard Lawrence Lefferts say, "not one of the lot holds the bow as she does"; and Beaufort retorted, "Yes; but that's the only kind of target she'll ever hit."

Archer felt irrationally angry. His host's contemptuous tribute to May's "niceness" was just what a husband should have wished to hear said of his wife. The fact that a coarse-minded man found her lacking in attraction was simply another proof of her quality; yet the words sent a faint shiver through his heart. What if "niceness" carried to that supreme degree were only a negation, the curtain dropped before an emptiness? As he looked at May, returning flushed and calm from her final bull's-eye, he had the feeling that he had never yet lifted that curtain.

She accepted the congratulations of her rivals and the rest of the company with the simplicity that was her crowning grace. No one could ever be jealous of her triumphs because she managed to give the feeling that she would have been just as serene if she had missed them. But when her eyes met her husband's, her face glowed with the pleasure she saw in his.

Mrs. Welland's basket-work pony-carriage was waiting for them, and they drove off among the dispersing carriages, May handling the reins and Archer sitting at her side.

The afternoon sunlight still lingered upon the bright lawns and shrubberies, and up and down Bellevue Avenue rolled a double line of victorias, dog-carts, landaus, and "vis-à-vis," carrying well-dressed ladies and gentlemen away from the Beaufort garden-party or homeward from their daily afternoon turn along the Ocean Drive.

"Shall we go to see Granny?" May suddenly proposed. "I should like to tell her myself that I've won the prize. There's lots of time before dinner."

Archer acquiesced, and she turned the ponies down Narragansett Avenue, crossed Spring Street, and drove out toward the rocky moorland beyond. In this unfashionable region, Catherine the Great, always indifferent to precedent and thrifty of purse, had built herself in her youth a many-peaked, cross-beamed *cottage-orné* on a bit of cheap land overlooking the bay. Here, in a thicket of stunted oaks, her verandahs spread themselves above the island-dotted waters. A winding drive led up between iron stags and blue glass balls embedded in mounds of geraniums to a front door of highly-varnished walnut under a striped verandah-roof. Behind it ran a narrow

"A party for the Blenkers—the Blenkers?"

Mr. Welland paused his knife and fork, his gaze fixed with a mixture of anxiety and disbelief upon his wife. She adjusted her gold-rimmed spectacles, her voice rising in a tone of theatrical delight as she read from the card:

"Professor and Mrs. Emerson Sillerton request the pleasure of Mr. and Mrs. Welland's company at the meeting of the Wednesday Afternoon Club on August 25th at 3 o'clock punctually. To meet Mrs. and the Misses Blenker. Red Gables, Catherine Street. R. S. V. P."

"Good gracious—" Mr. Welland gasped, as if a second reading were required to fully grasp the sheer absurdity of the situation.

"Poor Amy Sillerton," Mrs. Welland sighed, her expression one of practiced pity. "You never can tell what her husband will do next. I suppose he's finally discovered the Blenkers."

Professor Emerson Sillerton was a persistent irritant within Newport society, a thorn that could not be plucked because it grew from a venerable and venerated lineage. He was, as the town whispered, a man who had enjoyed every conceivable advantage. His father was Sillerton Jackson's uncle; his mother, a Pennilow of Boston. On both sides, wealth and status intertwined seamlessly. Yet, as Mrs. Welland often noted, nothing in the world compelled Emerson Sillerton to become an archaeologist, or a professor, or to reside in Newport during the winter, or to engage in any of the other radical pursuits he favored. Still, if he intended to shatter tradition and defy social norms, he need not have married poor Amy Dagonet, a woman entitled to expect "something different" and possessing enough fortune to maintain her own carriage.

No one in the Mingott circle could comprehend why Amy Sillerton had submitted so quietly to the eccentricities of a husband who populated his home with long-haired men and short-haired women, or who dragged her to explore tombs in Yucatán rather than visiting Paris or Italy. But there they were, entrenched in their habits, seemingly unaware that they differed from everyone else. Consequently, when they hosted one of their dreary annual garden parties, every family on the Cliffs, bound by the Sillerton-Pennilow-Dagonet connection, had to draw lots and send an unwilling representative.

"It's a wonder," Mrs. Welland remarked, "that they didn't choose Cup Race Day! Do you recall, two years ago, when they threw a party for a black man on the day of Julia Mingott's *thé dansant*? Fortunately, this time there is nothing else scheduled that I know of—for of course, some of us will have to attend."

Mr. Welland sighed nervously. "'Some of us,' my dear—more than one? Three o'clock is such an inconvenient hour. I must be here by half-past three to take my drops; it is truly futile to attempt Bencomb's new regimen without systematic adherence. If I join you later, I shall inevitably miss my drive." At the thought, he lowered his cutlery again, a flush of worry coloring his finely wrinkled cheek.

"There is no reason for you to go at all, my dear," his wife replied, her cheerfulness now entirely automatic. "I have cards to deliver at the other end of Bellevue Avenue, and I shall stop by around half-past three, staying long enough to assure poor Amy that she has not been slighted." She glanced hesitantly at her daughter. "And if Newland's afternoon is arranged, perhaps May can drive you out with the ponies to try their new russet harness."

It was a fundamental tenet of the Welland family that a person's days and hours should be meticulously "provided for." The melancholy prospect of having to "kill time"—particularly for those indifferent to whist or solitaire—haunted Mrs. Welland with the same terror that the specter of unemployment strikes a philanthropist. Another of her principles dictated that parents should never, at least visibly, interfere with the plans of their married children. The challenge of reconciling this respect for May's independence with the demands of Mr. Welland's schedule was met only through an ingenuity that ensured not a single second of Mrs. Welland's own time remained unaccounted for.

"Of course I'll drive with Papa—I'm certain Newland will find something to do," May said, her tone gently reminding her husband of his lack of initiative. It was a constant source of distress to Mrs. Welland that her son-in-law displayed such little foresight in planning his days. Often, during the fortnight he had spent under her roof, when asked how he intended to spend his afternoon, he had answered paradoxically, "Oh, I think for a change I'll just save it instead of spending it." Once, when she and May had been forced to endure a long-postponed round of afternoon calls, he had confessed to lying all afternoon beneath a rock on the beach below the house.

"Newland never seems to look ahead," Mrs. Welland had once ventured to complain to her daughter. May had answered serenely, "No; but you see it doesn't matter, because when there is nothing particular to do, he reads a book."

"Ah, yes—like his father!" Mrs. Welland had agreed, treating it as an inherited oddity, after which the subject of Newland's idleness was tacitly dropped.

Nevertheless, as the day for the Sillerton reception approached, May began to show a natural concern for his welfare, suggesting a tennis match at the Chiverses' or a sail on Julius Beaufort's cutter as a means of atoning for her temporary absence. "I shall be back by six, you know, dear; Papa never drives later than that." She was not reassured until Archer declared his intention of hiring a runabout and driving up the island to a stud-farm to inspect a second horse for her brougham. They had been searching for this horse for some time, and the suggestion was so agreeable that May glanced at her mother as if to say, "You see, he knows how to plan his time as well as any of us."

The notion of the stud-farm and the brougham horse had taken root in Archer's mind on the very day the Emerson Sillerton invitation was first mentioned, yet he had kept it secret, as if the plan itself were clandestine and discovery might thwart its execution. He had, however, taken the precaution of securing a runabout with a pair of old livery-stable trotters capable of covering eighteen miles on level roads. At two o'clock, hastily leaving the luncheon table, he sprang into the light carriage and drove off.

The day was perfect. A breeze from the north drove small puffs of white cloud across an ultramarine sky, beneath which the sea ran bright and clear. Bellevue Avenue was empty at that hour. After dropping the stable-lad at the corner of Mill Street, Archer turned down Old Beach Road and drove across Eastman's Beach.

He possessed a feeling of unexplained excitement, reminiscent of starting off into the unknown on half-holidays at school. Taking his pair at an easy gait, he calculated that he would reach the stud-farm, located not far beyond Paradise Rocks, before three o'clock. Thus, after examining the horse—and testing him if he seemed promising—he would still have four golden hours to dispose of.

As soon as he heard of the Sillerton party, he had told himself that the Marchioness Manson would certainly arrive in Newport with the Blenkers, offering Madame Olenska another opportunity to spend the day with her grandmother. At any rate, the Blenker habitation would likely be deserted, allowing him to satisfy a vague curiosity without indiscretion. He was not certain that he wished to see the Countess Olenska again; yet, ever since he had looked at her from the path above the bay, he had felt an irrational, indescribable desire to see the place where she lived, to follow the movements of her imagined figure as he had once watched the real one in the summer-house. This longing persisted day and night, an incessant, undefinable craving, akin to the sudden whim of a sick man for food or drink once tasted and long since forgotten. He could not envision beyond the craving, nor picture where it might lead, for he was not conscious of any wish to speak to Madame Olenska or hear her voice. He simply felt that if he could carry away the vision of the spot of earth she walked upon, and the way the sky and sea enclosed it, the rest of the world might seem less empty.

Upon reaching the stud-farm, a quick glance revealed that the horse was not what he desired. Nevertheless, he took a turn behind it to prove to himself that he was not in a hurry. But at three o'clock, he shook out the reins over the trotters and turned into the by-roads leading to Portsmouth. The wind had died down, and a faint haze on the horizon indicated that a fog was waiting to steal up the Saconnet on the turn of the tide; yet all around him, fields and woods were steeped in golden light.

He drove past grey-shingled farmhouses nestled in orchards, past hayfields and groves of oak, past villages with white steeples rising sharply into the fading sky. Finally, after stopping to ask the way from some men working in a field, he turned down a lane bordered by high banks of goldenrod and brambles. At the end of the lane lay the blue glimmer of the river. To the left, standing before a clump of oaks and maples, he saw a long, dilapidated house with white paint peeling from its clapboards.

On the roadside facing the gateway stood one of the open sheds where New Englanders shelter their farming implements and where visitors "hitch" their "teams." Archer jumped down, led his pair into the shed, and after tying them to a post, turned toward the house. The patch of lawn before it had reverted to a hayfield, but to the left, an overgrown box-garden filled with dahlias and rusty rose-bushes encircled a ghostly summer-house of trellis-work that had once been white, surmounted by a wooden Cupid who had lost his bow and arrow but continued to take ineffectual aim.

Archer leaned for a while against the gate. No one was in sight, and not a sound emanated from the open windows of the house; a grizzled Newfoundland dozing before the door seemed as ineffectual a guardian as the arrowless Cupid. It was strange to think that this place of silence and decay was the home of the turbulent Blenkers, yet Archer was sure he was not mistaken.

For a long time he stood there, content to absorb the scene, gradually falling under its drowsy spell. But eventually, he roused himself to the awareness of passing time. Should he look his fill and then drive away? He stood irresolute, wishing suddenly to see inside the house, to picture the room where Madame Olenska sat. There was nothing preventing him from walking up to the door and ringing the bell; if, as he supposed, she was away with the rest of the party, he could easily give his name and ask permission to enter the sitting-room to write a message.

Instead, he crossed the lawn and turned toward the box-garden. As he entered it, he caught sight of something brightly colored in the summer-house, and presently made it out to be a pink parasol. The parasol drew him like a magnet; he was certain it was hers. He went into the summer-house and, sitting on the rickety seat, picked up the silken object and examined its carved handle, crafted from some rare wood that emitted an aromatic scent. Archer lifted the handle to his lips.

He heard a rustle of skirts against the box and sat motionless, leaning on the parasol handle with clasped hands, letting the rustle approach without lifting his eyes. He had always known this must happen...

"Oh, Mr. Archer!" exclaimed a loud, young voice. Looking up, he saw before him the youngest and largest of the Blenker girls, blonde and blowsy, dressed in bedraggled muslin. A red blotch on one of her cheeks suggested it had recently been pressed against a pillow, and her half-awakened eyes stared at him with a hospitable but confused warmth.

"Gracious—where did you drop from? I must have been sound asleep in the hammock. Everybody else has gone to Newport. Did you ring?" she inquired incoherently.

Archer's confusion was greater than hers. "I—no—that is, I was just going to. I had to come up the island to see about a horse, and I drove over hoping to find Mrs. Blenker and your visitors. But the house seemed empty, so I sat down to wait."

Miss Blenker, shaking off the fumes of sleep, looked at him with increasing interest. "The house *is* empty. Mother isn't here, nor the Marchioness—or anybody but me." Her glance became faintly reproachful. "Didn't you know that Professor and Mrs. Sillerton are giving a garden-party for Mother and all of us this afternoon? It was too unlucky that I couldn't go; but I've had a sore throat, and Mother was afraid of the drive home this evening. Did you ever know anything so disappointing? Of course," she added gaily, "I shouldn't have minded half as much if I'd known you were coming."

Signs of a lumbering coquetry became visible in her, and Archer found the strength to interrupt: "But Madame Olenska—has she gone to Newport too?"

Miss Blenker looked at him with surprise. "Madame Olenska? Didn't you know she'd been called away?"

"Called away?"

"Oh, my best parasol! I lent it to that goose of a Katie because it matched her ribbons, and the careless thing must have dropped it here. We Blenkers are all like that... real Bohemians!" Recovering the sunshade with a powerful hand, she unfurled it and suspended its rosy dome above her head. "Yes, Ellen was called away yesterday; she lets us call her Ellen, you know. A telegram came from Boston;

The morning sun broke over Boston, transforming the city into a steaming, hazy dream of midsummer. As Archer stepped off the Fall River train, the air hit him thick with the scent of brewing coffee, stale beer, and rotting fruit. The streets were a chaotic river of shirt-sleeved figures moving with the frantic, intimate abandon of boarders rushing to the bathroom. It was a scene of untidy domesticity that felt alien to the refined order of Europe, where heat rarely degraded the grandeur of the streets.

Archer hailed a cab and drove to the Somerset Club for breakfast. Even here, the fashionable quarters seemed to succumb to the oppressive warmth, losing their polish for a moment. Caretakers lounged in calico on doorsteps, and the Common looked less like a public park and more like the aftermath of a massive picnic. It was a landscape where he could scarcely imagine fitting Ellen Olenska; the heat seemed to prostrate the very spirit of the city, leaving it deserted and weary.

Yet, Archer ate with renewed appetite and method. He sliced melon and studied the morning paper while waiting for his toast, driven by a sudden surge of energy. Since the night before, when he had told May he was heading to Boston and then on to New York, he had felt a strange liberation. His original plan to return early in the week had been effortlessly altered by a single letter from the office, a piece of fate conveniently placed on the hall table. He felt a flicker of shame at how easily he had manipulated the situation, reminiscent of Lawrence Lefferts' masterful schemes, but the feeling passed quickly. He was not in an analytical mood today; he was simply alive.

After breakfast, smoking a cigarette and glancing at the *Commercial Advertiser*, he watched as acquaintances drifted in, exchanging the polite greetings of a world that felt strangely unchanged despite his sense of having slipped through the meshes of time and space. At half-past nine, he retreated to the writing room. He penned a few lines and dispatched a messenger to the Parker House, instructing him to wait for a reply. Sitting behind another newspaper, Archer calculated the travel time to the hotel, his mind racing with anticipation.

"The lady was out, sir," a waiter's voice cut through his thoughts.

"Out?" Archer stammered, the word sounding foreign on his tongue.

A flush of anger rose in his cheeks. How stupid he had been not to send the note immediately upon arrival. He grabbed his hat and stick, stepping out into a city that suddenly felt vast, empty, and strange, as if he were a traveler from a distant land. Hesitating on the doorstep, he resolved to go to the Parker House himself. Perhaps the messenger had been mistaken.

Crossing the Common, he spotted her instantly on the first bench beneath a tree. She held a grey silk sunshade—how could he have ever imagined her with a pink one? Her posture was listless, as if she had nothing else to do. He noted the drooping profile, the knot of hair fastened low under her dark hat, and the long, wrinkled glove holding the shade. As he approached, she turned, her face registering a startled surprise before melting into a slow, wondering smile.

"Oh," she murmured, making space for him on the bench without rising.

"I'm here on business—just got here," Archer explained, feigning astonishment at the sight of her. "But what on earth are you doing in this wilderness?" He felt as though he were shouting across endless distances, fearing she might vanish before he could catch her.

"I? Oh, I'm here on business too," she replied, turning to face him. Her voice was low, with a faint roughness on the consonants that he had somehow forgotten until this moment.

"You do your hair differently," he said, his heart hammering as if he had spoken an irrevocable truth.

"Differently? No—it's only that I do it as best I can when I'm without Nastasia."

"Nastasia? But isn't she with you?"

"No; I'm alone. For two days it was not worth while to bring her."

"You're alone... at the Parker House?"

She flashed him a look of old malice. "Does it strike you as dangerous?"

"No; not dangerous—"

"But unconventional? I see; I suppose it is." She considered him for a moment. "I hadn't thought of it, because I've just done something so much more unconventional." A faint irony lingered in her eyes. "I've just refused to take back a sum of money—that belonged to me."

Archer sprang up, stepping back slightly. She had furled her parasol and sat absently drawing patterns in the gravel. He returned to stand before her.

"Some one... has come here to meet you?"

"Yes."

"With this offer?"

She nodded.

"And you refused—because of the conditions?"

"I refused," she said after a pause.

He sat down beside her. "What were the conditions?"

"Oh, they were not onerous: just to sit at the head of his table now and then."

Silence stretched between them. Archer's heart felt strangely shut, and he groped vainly for words.

"He wants you back—at any price?"

"Well—a considerable price. At least the sum is considerable for me."

He paused, beating around the question he felt compelled to ask. "It was to meet him here that you came?"

She stared, then burst into laughter. "Meet him—my husband? *Here*? At this season he's always at Cowes or Baden."

"He sent someone?"

"Yes."

"With a letter?"

She shook her head. "No; just a message. He never writes. I don't think I've had more than one letter from him." The mention brought color to her cheek, reflecting in Archer's own vivid blush.

"Why does he never write?"

"Why should he? What does one have secretaries for?"

The young man's blush deepened. She had spoken the word as if it held no special weight in her vocabulary. For a moment, the question "Did he send his secretary?" hovered on his lips, but the memory of Count Olenski's solitary letter to his wife silenced him. He plunged forward again.

"And the person?"

"The emissary? The emissary," Madame Olenska rejoined, still smiling, "might, for all I care, have left already; but he has insisted on waiting till this evening... in case... on the chance..."

"And you came out here to think the chance over?"

"I came out to get a breath of air. The hotel's too stifling. I'm taking the afternoon train back to Portsmouth."

They sat in silence, staring ahead at the passing pedestrians. Finally, she turned her eyes to his face. "You're not changed."

He wanted to answer, "I was, till I saw you again," but instead, he stood abruptly and glanced at the sweltering, untidy park. "This is horrible. Why shouldn't we go out a little on the bay? There's a breeze, and it will be cooler. We might take the steamboat down to Point Arley."

She glanced up hesitantly. He pressed on. "On a Monday morning there won't be anybody on the boat. My train doesn't leave till evening: I'm going back to New York. Why shouldn't we?" He looked down at her, his voice breaking. "Haven't we done all we could?"

"Oh," she murmured. She stood up, reopening her sunshade and scanning the scene as if seeking counsel from the surroundings, assuring herself of the impossibility of staying. Then her eyes returned to his. "You mustn't say things like that to me," she said softly.

"I'll say anything you like; or nothing. I won't open my mouth unless you tell me to. What harm can it do to anybody? All I want is to listen to you," he stammered.

She produced a small gold watch on an enamelled chain. "Oh, don't calculate," he broke out, seizing the moment. "Give me the day! I want to get you away from that man. At what time was he coming?"

Her color rose again. "At eleven."

"Then you must come at once."

"You needn't be afraid—if I don't come."

"Nor you either—if you do. I swear I only want to hear about you, to know what you've been doing. It's a hundred years since we've met—it may be another hundred before we meet again."

She wavered, her anxious eyes fixed on his face. "Why didn't you come down to the beach to fetch me, the day I was at Granny's?" she asked.

"Because you didn't look round—because you didn't know I was there. I swore I wouldn't unless you looked round." He laughed as the childishness of the confession struck him.

"But I didn't look round on purpose."

"On purpose?"

"I knew you were there; when you drove in I recognised the ponies. So I went down to the beach."

"To get away from me as far as you could?"

She repeated in a low voice: "To get away from you as far as I could."

He laughed again, this time with boyish satisfaction. "Well, you see it's no use. I may as well tell you," he added, "that the business I came here for was just to find you. But, look here, we must start or we shall miss our boat."

"Our boat?" She frowned perplexedly, then smiled. "Oh, but I must go back to the hotel first: I must leave a note—"

"As many notes as you please. You can write here." He produced a note-case and one of the new stylographic pens. "I've even got an envelope—you see how everything's predestined! There—steady the thing on your knee, and I'll get the pen going in a second. They have to be humoured; wait—" He banged the hand holding the pen against the back of the bench. "It's like jerking down the mercury in a thermometer: just a trick. Now try—"

She laughed, bending over the paper he had laid on his case, and began to write. Archer walked a few steps away, staring with radiant, unseeing eyes at the passersby, who in turn paused to stare at the unusual sight of a fashionably dressed lady writing a note on her knee on a park bench.

Madame Olenska slipped the sheet into the envelope, wrote a name, and tucked it into her pocket. She stood up, and they walked back toward Beacon Street. Near the club, Archer caught sight of the plush-lined carriage that had delivered his note; its driver was resting, wiping his brow at a hydrant.

"I told you everything was predestined! Here's a cab for us. You see!" They laughed, astonished at the miracle of catching a public conveyance at that hour in such an unlikely spot, in a city where cab-stands were still a foreign novelty.

Archer checked his watch; there was time to drive to the Parker House before heading to the steamboat landing. They rattled through the hot streets and pulled up at the hotel door. Archer held out his hand for the letter, asking if he should take it in, but Madame Olenska shook her head, springing out and disappearing through the glazed doors. It was barely half-past ten, but what if the emissary, impatient and idle, was already seated among the travelers with cooling drinks?

Archer paced up and down before the carriage. A Sicilian youth with eyes like Nastasia's offered to shine his boots, and an Irish matron sold him peaches. Every few moments, the doors opened to release hot men in straw hats who glanced at him as they passed. He marveled at the uniformity of their faces, the endless stream of identical travelers passing in and out of hotel doors across the land.

Suddenly, a face appeared that did not belong to the crowd. He caught only a flash of it as he turned back toward the hotel, amidst the lank, weary, round, and surprised countenances. It was a young man, pale and half-extinguished by heat or worry, yet somehow quicker, vivid, more conscious. Archer hung onto a thin thread of memory, but it snapped and floated away with the disappearing figure—apparently some foreign businessman looking doubly foreign in such a setting. He vanished into the stream of passersby, and Archer resumed his patrol.

He did not wish to be seen watching his watch near the hotel. His unaided calculation suggested that if Madame Olenska was delayed, it was only because she had met the emissary and been waylaid. At the thought, his apprehension rose to anguish.

"If she doesn't come soon I'll go in and find her," he muttered.

The doors swung open, and she was at his side. They hurried into the carriage, and as it drove off, he checked his watch: she had been gone just three minutes. Through the clatter of loose windows that made conversation impossible, they bumped over disjointed cobblestones to the wharf.

Seated side by side on the bench of the half-empty boat, they found they had hardly anything to say, or rather, that what they needed to communicate passed best in the blessed silence of their release and isolation. As the paddle-wheels began to turn and the wharves receded through the veil of heat, Archer felt that everything in the old, familiar world of habit was fading away. He longed to ask Madame Olenska if she shared the feeling that they were embarking on a long voyage from which they might never return. But he was afraid to speak, fearing to disturb the delicate balance of her trust. In reality, he had no wish to betray that trust. There had been days and nights when the memory of their kiss burned on his lips; the day before, on the drive to Portsmouth, the thought of her had run through him like fire. But now, with her beside him, drifting into this unknown world, they seemed to have reached a deeper nearness that a mere touch could sunder.

As the boat turned seaward, a breeze stirred, breaking the bay into long, oily undulations, then into ripples tipped with spray. The sultry fog still hung over the city, but ahead lay a fresh world of

They lunched slowly, the silence between their words heavy and deliberate, as if the air itself were holding its breath. The conversation ebbed and flowed, not in a steady stream, but in sudden rushes of speech followed by long, meditative pauses where speaking felt merely like an accompaniment to the deeper dialogue of their shared presence. Archer guarded his own life from her, not out of malice, but driven by a desperate hunger to hear every detail of hers; he leaned forward, his eyes fixed on her, while she rested her chin on her clasped hands, weaving a tapestry of the year and a half since they had crossed paths.

She spoke of her weariness with the polished veneer of New York society. The city had been kind, almost suffocatingly so, welcoming her back with open arms, yet the initial thrill of novelty had faded. She had found herself, in her own words, "too different" to truly care for the things that mattered to them. That realization had propelled her toward Washington, a place rumored to offer a kaleidoscope of opinions and people. She spoke of settling there, building a home for Medora, whose patience had finally worn thin under the weight of familial neglect and the looming specter of unwanted matrimony.

"But Dr. Carver," Archer interjected, his voice cutting through the quiet. "Aren't you afraid of him? I heard he's been staying at the Blenkers'."

A soft smile touched her lips. "The danger has passed. Dr. Carver is a brilliant man, but he seeks a wealthy wife to fund his grand designs. Medora is merely a convenient advertisement for his conversion."

"To what?" Archer pressed.

"To radical social experiments," she replied, her gaze drifting across the table. "But tell me, do these new ideas interest you more than the blind adherence to tradition that plagues our circle? It seems absurd to discover America only to replicate Europe. Do you think Christopher Columbus sailed the ocean blue just to attend the opera with the Selfridge Merrys?"

Archer's face flushed. "And Beaufort? Do you speak such truths to him?"

"I haven't seen him in ages," she admitted. "But I used to. And he understands."

"Exactly as I've always suspected," Archer said, his tone sharp with a mixture of jealousy and self-loathing. "You dislike us. You favor Beaufort precisely because he is so unlike us." He gestured to the sparse room, the stark white houses lining the shore, the empty beach. "We are dreadfully dull. Colorless. Lifeless. I wonder," he broke out, his voice rising, "why you do not return?"

Her eyes darkened, and he braced himself for an angry retort. Instead, she sat in profound silence, contemplating his question, and a cold fear seized him that she might answer that she wondered, too.

Finally, she spoke. "I believe it is because of you."

The confession hung in the air, delivered with a chilling dispassion that somehow stoked his vanity rather than cooled it. Archer turned crimson to the roots of his hair, paralyzed, terrified that any movement would shatter the fragile moment like a rare butterfly taking flight.

"At least," she continued, "it was you who taught me that beneath the dullness lie things so fine, so sensitive, so delicate that everything I once cherished in my other life now appears cheap by comparison. I struggle to explain," she furrowed her brow, "but it feels as though I never understood until now how much hardness, shabbiness, and baseness one must endure to pay for the most exquisite pleasures."

"Exquisite pleasures..." he felt the urge to retort, to point out the cost, but the pleading in her eyes silenced him.

"I wish," she went on, her voice dropping to a whisper, "to be perfectly honest with you, and with myself. For a long time, I have waited for this moment: to tell you how you have shaped me, what you have made of me."

Archer stared beneath his frowning brows, then laughed, a nervous, brittle sound. "And what do you think you have made of me?"

She paled. "Of you?"

"Yes," he insisted, his voice trembling slightly. "For I am the product of your making far more than you were ever of mine. I am the man who married one woman solely because another commanded him to."

Her pallor gave way to a fleeting flush. "I thought... you promised... you would not say such things today."

"Ah, how typical of a woman!" he exclaimed, masking his pain with sarcasm. "None of you can ever see a bad business through!"

She lowered her voice, her eyes searching his. "Is it a bad business—for May?"

He moved to the window, drumming his fingers against the glass, acutely aware of the wistful tenderness with which she had invoked her cousin's name.

"That is the burden we must always carry," she insisted, turning back to him. "By your own admission."

"My own admission?" he echoed, his eyes fixed blankly on the sea.

"Or if not," she pursued her thought with painful intensity, "if it is not worth sacrificing everything, missing out on everything, so that others might be spared disillusionment and misery—then everything I returned home for, everything that made my previous life seem so barren and poor because no one there acknowledged its value... all of it was a sham, a dream."

He turned from the window, his body still but his spirit shifting. "In that case," he concluded for her, "there is no earthly reason why you should not go back."

Her eyes clung to him with desperate intensity. "Oh, is there no reason?"

"Not if you staked your entire existence on the success of my marriage. My marriage," he said, his voice savage with frustration, "will not be a spectacle worthy of keeping you here." She remained silent, and he pressed on. "What is the point? You gave me my first glimpse of a real life, and at the very same moment, you asked me to continue a sham one. It is beyond human endurance—that is all."

"Oh, do not say that, when I am enduring it!" she burst out, tears welling in her eyes.

Her arms dropped to the table, and she sat with her face exposed to his gaze, vulnerable as if stripped bare, revealing the soul behind the eyes. Archer stood dumbfounded, overwhelmed by the sudden revelation.

"You too... oh, all this time, you too?"

In answer, she let the tears spill over her lashes, tracing slow, silent paths down her cheeks.

Half the room separated them, yet neither moved. Archer felt a strange indifference to her physical presence; he would not have noticed her unless one of the hands she had thrown onto the table had caught his eye, just as it had in the small house on Twenty-third Street, where he had watched it to avoid looking at her face. Now, his imagination spun around that hand as if it were the edge of a vortex, yet he made no effort to cross the distance. He knew the love that feeds on caresses and demands them, but this passion, burning closer than bone, refused to be satisfied superficially. His greatest terror was doing anything that might erase the sound and impact of her words; his only thought was that he should never again feel quite alone.

But soon, a crushing sense of waste and ruin overtook him. There they were, close together, safe and enclosed, yet chained to separate destinies that might as well have placed them worlds apart.

"What is the use," he exploded, a great, hopeless cry rising in his throat, "when you will go back? How on earth can I keep you?"

She sat motionless, her lids lowered. "Oh—I shall not go yet!"

"Not yet? Then some time? A time you already foresee?"

At that, she raised her clearest eyes. "I promise you: not as long as you hold out. Not as long as we can look straight at each other like this."

He sank into his chair. What her answer truly conveyed was: *If you lift a finger, you will drive me back—to all the abominations you know, and all the temptations you half-guess.* He understood it with crystal clarity, and the thought anchored him to his side of the table in a state of moved, sacred submission.

"What a life for you—" he groaned.

"Oh—as long as it is a part of yours."

"And mine a part of yours?"

She nodded.

"And that is to be all—for either of us?"

"Well; it *is* all, isn't it?"

At that, he sprang up, forgetting everything save the sweetness of her face. She rose as well, not to meet him or flee, but quietly, as though the hardest part of the task was done and she merely waited. So quietly that, as he approached, her outstretched hands acted not as a barrier but as a guide. They fell into his, while her arms, extended but not rigid, kept him at a distance that allowed her surrendered face to speak the rest.

They may have stood thus for a long time, or only a few moments; but it was sufficient for her silence to convey everything she had to say, and for him to realize that only one thing mattered. He must do nothing to make this meeting their last; he must leave their future in her care, asking only that she hold fast to it.

"Don't—do not be unhappy," she said, her voice breaking as she withdrew her hands.

He answered, "You won't go back—you won't go back?" as if it were the single possibility he could not bear.

"I won't go back," she said. Turning away, she opened the door and led the way into the public dining room.

Outside, strident schoolteachers gathered their belongings for a straggling flight to the wharf. Across the beach lay the white steamboat at the pier, and over the sunlit waters, Boston loomed in a line of hazy promise.

Once more aboard the boat, surrounded by the indifferent chatter of strangers, Newland Archer felt a profound tranquility settle over his spirit. It was a peace that surprised him as much as it sustained him. By any conventional measure, the day had been a ridiculous failure; he had not so much as kissed Madame Olenska's hand, nor drawn from her a single word promising future encounters. Yet, for a man consumed by unsatisfied love and facing an indefinite separation from his passion, he felt almost humiliated in his calmness.

It was the perfect equilibrium she had maintained between her loyalty to others and her honesty to herself that had stirred and then soothed him. It was not a balance artfully calculated, as her tears and faltering voice had revealed, but one born naturally from her unashamed sincerity. Now that the danger had passed, it filled him with a tender awe, making him thank the fates that no personal vanity, no desire to perform before sophisticated witnesses, had tempted him to lead her astray. Even after they had clasped hands in farewell at the Fall River station and he had turned away alone, the conviction remained that he had saved far more from their meeting than he had sacrificed.

He wandered back to the club and sat alone in the deserted library, turning over every second of their time together in his mind. It became increasingly clear to him that if she ultimately decided to return to Europe—to return to her husband—it would not be because her old life tempted her, even on the new terms offered. No, she would go only if she felt herself becoming a temptation to Archer, a lure to fall away from the standard they had both set up. Her choice would be to remain near him as long as he did not ask her to come nearer; and it depended entirely on himself to keep her just there, safe but secluded.

These thoughts accompanied him into the train, enclosing him in a kind of golden haze through which the faces of his fellow travelers looked remote and indistinct. He felt that if he spoke, they would not understand. In this state of abstraction, he woke the following morning to the stifling reality of a September day in New York. The heat-withered faces streaming past in the long train remained blurred, until suddenly, as he left the station, one face detached itself, forcing its way into his consciousness. It was the face of the young man he had seen the day before leaving the Parker House, a man who did not possess the typical American hotel look.

The same realization struck him anew, stirring a dim recollection of former associations. The young man stood looking about with the dazed air of a foreigner cast upon the harsh mercies of American travel. Then he advanced toward Archer, lifted his hat, and said in English, "Surely, Monsieur, we met in London?"

"Ah, to be sure: in London!" Archer grasped his hand with curiosity and sympathy. "So you *did* get here, after all?" he exclaimed, casting a wondering eye on the astute and haggard little countenance of young Carfry's French tutor.

"Oh, I got here—yes," M. Rivière smiled with drawn lips. "But not for long; I return the day after tomorrow." He stood grasping his light valise in one neatly gloved hand, gazing anxiously, perplexedly, almost appealingly, into Archer's face.

"I wonder, Monsieur, since I've had the good luck to run across you, if I might—"

"I was just going to suggest it: come to luncheon, won't you? Downtown, I mean. If you'll look me up in my office, I'll take you to a very decent restaurant in that quarter."

M. Rivière was visibly touched and surprised. "You're too kind. But I was only going to ask if you would tell me how to reach some sort of conveyance. There are no porters, and no one here seems to listen—"

"I know: our American stations must surprise you. When you ask for a porter, they give you chewing-gum. But if you'll come along, I'll extricate you; and you must really lunch with me, you know."

After a just perceptible hesitation, the young man replied, with profuse thanks and a tone that did not carry complete conviction, that he was already engaged. But once they had reached the comparative reassurance of the street, he asked if he might call that afternoon.

Archer, at ease in the midsummer leisure of the office, fixed an hour and scribbled his address, which the Frenchman pocketed with reiterated thanks and a wide flourish of his hat. A horse-car received him, and Archer walked away.

Punctually at the hour, M. Rivière appeared, shaved and smoothed-out, but still unmistakably drawn and serious. Archer was alone in his office, and the young man, before accepting the seat proffered, began abruptly, "I believe I saw you, sir, yesterday in Boston."

The statement was insignificant enough, and Archer was about to frame an assent when his words were checked by something mysterious yet illuminating in his visitor's insistent gaze.

"It is extraordinary, very extraordinary," M. Rivière continued, "that we should have met in the circumstances in which I find myself."

"What circumstances?" Archer asked, wondering a little crudely if he needed money.

M. Rivière continued to study him with tentative eyes. "I have come, not to look for employment, as I spoke of doing when we last met, but on a special mission—"

"Ah—" Archer exclaimed. In a flash, the two meetings connected themselves in his mind. He paused to take in the situation thus suddenly lighted up for him, and M. Rivière also remained silent, as if aware that what he had said was enough.

"A special mission," Archer at length repeated.

The young Frenchman opened his palms, raised them slightly, and the two men continued to look at each other across the office-desk until Archer roused himself to say, "Do sit down." Whereupon M. Rivière bowed, took a distant chair, and again waited.

"It was about this mission that you wanted to consult me?" Archer finally asked.

M. Rivière bent his head. "Not in my own behalf: on that score I—I have fully dealt with myself. I should like—if I may—to speak to you about the Countess Olenska."

Archer had known for the last few minutes that the words were coming; but when they came, they sent the blood rushing to his temples as if he had been caught by a bent-back branch in a thicket.

"And on whose behalf," he said, "do you wish to do this?"

M. Rivière met the question sturdily. "Well—I might say *hers*, if it did not sound like a liberty. Shall I say instead: on behalf of abstract justice?"

Archer considered him ironically. "In other words: you are Count Olenski's messenger?"

He saw his blush more darkly reflected in M. Rivière's sallow countenance. "Not to *you*, Monsieur. If I come to you, it is on quite other grounds."

"What right have you, in the circumstances, to *be* on any other ground?" Archer retorted. "If you're an emissary, you're an emissary."

The young man considered. "My mission is over: as far as the Countess Olenska goes, it has failed."

"I can't help that," Archer rejoined on the same note of irony.

"No: but you can help—" M. Rivière paused, turned his hat about in his still carefully gloved hands, looked into its lining, and then back at Archer's face. "You can help, Monsieur, I am convinced, to make it equally a failure with her family."

Archer pushed back his chair and stood up. "Well—and by God I will!" he exclaimed. He stood with his hands in his pockets, staring down wrathfully at the little Frenchman, whose face, though he too had risen, was still an inch or two below the line of Archer's eyes.

M. Rivière paled to his normal hue; paler than that, his complexion could hardly turn.

"Why the devil," Archer explosively continued, "should you have thought—since I suppose you're appealing to me on the ground of my relationship to Madame Olenska—that I should take a view contrary to the rest of her family?"

The change of expression in M. Rivière's face was for a time his only answer. His look passed from timidity to absolute distress; for a young man of his usually resourceful mien, it would have been difficult to appear more disarmed and defenseless. "Oh, Monsieur—"

"I can't imagine," Archer continued, "why you should have come to me when there are others so much nearer to the Countess; still less why you thought I should be more accessible to the arguments I suppose you were sent over with."

M. Rivière took this onslaught with a disconcerting humility. "The arguments I want to present to you, Monsieur, are my own and not those I was sent over with."

"Then I see still less reason for listening to them."

M. Rivière again looked into his hat, as if considering whether these last words were not a sufficiently broad hint to put it on and be gone. Then he spoke with sudden decision. "Monsieur—will you tell me one thing? Is it my right to be here that you question? Or do you perhaps believe the whole matter to be already closed?"

His quiet insistence made Archer feel the clumsiness of his own bluster. M. Rivière had succeeded in imposing himself; Archer, reddening slightly, dropped into his chair again and signaled to the young man to be seated.

"I beg your pardon: but why isn't the matter closed?"

M. Rivière gazed back at him with anguish. "You do, then, agree with the rest of the family that, in face of the new proposals I have brought, it is hardly possible for Madame Olenska not to return to her husband?"

"Good God!" Archer exclaimed; and his visitor gave out a low murmur of confirmation.

"Before seeing her, I saw—at Count Olenski's request—Mr. Lovell Mingott, with whom I had several talks before going to Boston. I understand that he represents his mother's view; and that Mrs. Manson Mingott's influence is great throughout her family."

Archer sat silent, with the sense of clinging to the edge of a sliding precipice. The discovery that he had been excluded from a share in these negotiations, and even from the knowledge that they were on foot, caused him a surprise hardly dulled by the acuter wonder of what he was learning. He saw in a flash that if the family had ceased to consult him, it was because some deep tribal instinct warned them that he was no longer on their side. He recalled, with a start of comprehension, a remark of May's during their drive home from Mrs. Manson Mingott's on the day of the Archery Meeting: "Perhaps, after all, Ellen would be happier with her husband."

Even in the tumult of new discoveries, Archer remembered his indignant exclamation, and the fact that since then his wife had never named Madame Olenska to him. Her careless allusion had no doubt been the straw held up to see which way the wind blew; the result had been reported to the family, and thereafter Archer had been tacitly omitted from their counsels. He admired the tribal discipline which made May bow to this decision. She would not have done so, he knew, had her conscience protested; but she probably shared the family view that Madame Olenska would be better off as an unhappy wife than as a separated one, and that there was no use in discussing the case with Newland, who had an awkward way of suddenly not seeming to take the most fundamental things for granted.

Archer looked up and met his visitor's anxious gaze. "Don't you know, Monsieur—is it possible you don't know—that the family begin to doubt if they have the right to advise the Countess to refuse her husband's last proposals?"

"The proposals you brought?"

"The proposals I brought."

It was on Archer's lips to exclaim that whatever he knew or did not know was no concern of M. Rivière's; but something in the humble and yet courageous tenacity of M. Rivière's gaze made him reject this conclusion, and he met the young man's question with another. "What is your object in speaking to me of this?"

He had not to wait a moment for the answer. "To beg you, Monsieur—to beg you with all the force I'm capable of—not to let her go back.—Oh, don't let her!" M. Rivière exclaimed.

Archer looked at him with increasing astonishment. There was no mistaking the sincerity of his distress or the strength of his determination: he had evidently resolved to let everything go by the board but the supreme need of thus putting himself on record. Archer considered.

"May I ask," he said at length, "if this is the line you took with the Countess Olenska?"

M. Rivière reddened, but his eyes did not falter. "No, Monsieur: I accepted my mission in good faith. I really believed—for reasons I need not trouble you with—that it would be better for Madame Olenska to recover her situation, her fortune, the social consideration that her husband's standing gives her."

"So I supposed: you could hardly have accepted such a mission otherwise."

"I should not have accepted it."

"Well, then—?" Archer paused again, and their eyes met in another protracted scrutiny.

"Ah, Monsieur, after I had seen her, after I had listened to her, I knew she was better off here."

"You knew—?"

"Monsieur, I discharged my mission faithfully: I put the Count's arguments, I stated his offers, without adding any comment of my own. The Countess was good enough to listen patiently; she carried her goodness so far as to see me twice; she considered impartially all I had come to say. And it was in the course of these two talks that I changed my mind, that I came to see things differently."

"May I ask what led to this change?"

"Simply seeing the change in *her*," M. Rivière replied.

"The change in her? Then you knew her before?"

The young man's color again rose. "

Every year on the fifteenth of October, Fifth Avenue performed its ritual of opening shutters, unrolling carpets, and hanging up its triple layers of window curtains. By the first of November, this domestic ceremony concluded, and society began to survey itself. By the fifteenth, the season was in full bloom; opera houses unveiled new attractions, dinner engagements multiplied, and dance dates were set. And punctually around this time, Mrs. Archer invariably declared that New York had changed profoundly.

Viewing the city from the lofty, detached standpoint of a non-participant, she was able, aided by Mr. Sillerton Jackson and Miss Sophy, to trace every new fissure in its surface and every strange weed pushing up between the orderly rows of social vegetation. It had been one of Archer's youthful amusements to await this annual pronouncement from his mother, listening as she enumerated the minute signs of disintegration that his careless gaze had missed. For New York, to Mrs. Archer's mind, never changed without deteriorating; and in this view, Miss Sophy Jackson heartily concurred.

Mr. Sillerton Jackson, acting as a man of the world, suspended his judgment and listened with amused impartiality to the ladies' lamentations. Yet even he never denied that New York had changed; and Newland Archer, in the winter of the second year of his marriage, was himself compelled to admit that if the city had not actually altered, it was certainly in the process of doing so.

These points were raised, as usual, at Mrs. Archer's Thanksgiving dinner. At the time she was officially enjoined to give thanks for the year's blessings, it was her habit to take a mournful, though not embittered, inventory of her world, wondering what there truly was to be thankful for. Certainly, not the state of society; society, if it could be said to exist, was more of a spectacle upon which to invoke Biblical curses—and indeed, everyone knew what the Reverend Dr. Ashmore meant when he selected a text from Jeremiah (chapter ii, verse 25) for his Thanksgiving sermon. Dr. Ashmore, the new Rector of St. Matthew's, had been chosen precisely because he was very "advanced"; his sermons were considered bold in thought and novel in language. When he fulminated against fashionable society, he always spoke of its "trend"; and to Mrs. Archer, it was both terrifying and fascinating to feel herself part of a community that was trending.

"There's no doubt that Dr. Ashmore is right: there *is* a marked trend," she said, as if it were something visible and measurable, like a crack in a house.

"It was odd, though, to preach about it on Thanksgiving," Miss Jackson remarked. Her hostess replied dryly, "Oh, he means us to give thanks for what's left."

Archer had been wont to smile at these annual prophecies of his mother's; but this year, even he was obliged to acknowledge, as he listened to an enumeration of the changes, that the "trend" was undeniable.

"The extravagance in dress—" Miss Jackson began. "Sillerton took me to the first night of the Opera, and I can only tell you that Jane Merry's dress was the only one I recognized from last year; and even that had had the front panel changed. Yet I know she got it out from Worth only two years ago, because my seamstress always goes in to make over her Paris dresses before she wears them."

"Ah, Jane Merry is one of *us*," said Mrs. Archer, sighing as if it were not such an enviable thing to be in an age when ladies began to flaunt their Paris dresses abroad as soon as they cleared Customs, rather than letting them mellow under lock and key, as was the custom among Mrs. Archer's contemporaries.

"Yes; she's one of the few. In my youth," Miss Jackson rejoined, "it was considered vulgar to dress in the newest fashions; and Amy Sillerton has always told me that in Boston the rule was to put away one's Paris dresses for two years. Old Mrs. Baxter Pennilow, who did everything handsomely, used to import twelve a year—two velvet, two satin, two silk, and the other six of poplin and the finest cashmere. It was a standing order, and as she was ill for two years before she died, they found forty-eight Worth dresses that had never been taken out of tissue paper; and when the girls left off their mourning, they were able to wear the first lot at the Symphony concerts without looking ahead of the fashion."

"Ah, well, Boston is more conservative than New York; but I always think it's a safe rule for a lady to lay aside her French dresses for one season," Mrs. Archer conceded.

"It was Beaufort who started the new fashion by making his wife clap her new clothes on her back as soon as they arrived: I must say at times it takes all Regina's distinction not to look like... like..." Miss Jackson glanced around the table, caught Janey's bulging gaze, and took refuge in an unintelligible murmur.

"Like her rivals," said Mr. Sillerton Jackson, with the air of producing an epigram.

"Oh—" the ladies murmured; and Mrs. Archer added, partly to distract her daughter's attention from forbidden topics, "Poor Regina! Her Thanksgiving hasn't been a very cheerful one, I'm afraid. Have you heard the rumors about Beaufort's speculations, Sillerton?"

Mr. Jackson nodded carelessly. Everyone had heard the rumors in question, and he scorned to confirm a tale that was already common property.

A gloomy silence fell upon the party. No one really liked Beaufort, and it was not wholly unpleasant to think the worst of his private life; but the idea of his having brought financial dishonor on his wife's family was too shocking to be enjoyed even by his enemies. Archer's New York tolerated hypocrisy in private relations; but in business matters, it exacted a limpid and impeccable honesty. It was a long time since any well-known banker had failed discreditably; but everyone remembered the social extinction visited upon the heads of a firm when the last event of the kind had happened. It would be the same with the Beauforts, in spite of his power and her popularity; not all the leagued strength of the Dallas connection would save poor Regina if there were any truth in the reports of her husband's unlawful speculations.

The conversation took refuge in less ominous topics; but everything they touched on seemed to confirm Mrs. Archer's sense of an accelerated trend.

"Of course, Newland, I know you let dear May go to Mrs. Struthers's Sunday evenings—" she began; and May interposed gaily, "Oh, you know, everybody goes to Mrs. Struthers's now; and she was invited to Granny's last reception."

It was thus, Archer reflected, that New York managed its transitions: conspiring to ignore them until they were well over, and then, in all good faith, imagining that they had taken place in a preceding age. There was always a traitor in the citadel; and after he (or generally she) had surrendered the keys, what was the use of pretending that it was impregnable? Once people had tasted of Mrs. Struthers's easy Sunday hospitality, they were not likely to sit at home remembering that her champagne was transmuted shoe polish.

"I know, dear, I know," Mrs. Archer sighed. "Such things have to be, I suppose, as long as *amusement* is what people go out for; but I've never quite forgiven your cousin Madame Olenska for being the first person to countenance Mrs. Struthers."

A sudden blush rose to young Mrs. Archer's face; it surprised her husband as much as the other guests around the table. "Oh, *Ellen*—" she murmured, much in the same accusing and yet deprecating tone in which her parents might have said, "Oh, *the Blenkers*—"

It was the note which the family had taken to sounding on the mention of the Countess Olenska's name, since she had surprised and inconvenienced them by remaining obdurate to her husband's advances; but on May's lips, it gave food for thought, and Archer looked at her with the sense of strangeness that sometimes came over him when she was most in the tone of her environment.

His mother, with less than her usual sensitiveness to atmosphere, still insisted: "I've always thought that people like the Countess Olenska, who have lived in aristocratic societies, ought to help us to keep up our social distinctions, instead of ignoring them."

May's blush remained permanently vivid; it seemed to have a significance beyond that implied by the recognition of Madame Olenska's social bad faith.

"I've no doubt we all seem alike to foreigners," said Miss Jackson tartly.

"I don't think Ellen cares for society; but nobody knows exactly what she does care for," May continued, as if she had been groping for something noncommittal.

"Ah, well—" Mrs. Archer sighed again.

Everybody knew that the Countess Olenska was no longer in the good graces of her family. Even her devoted champion, old Mrs. Manson Mingott, had been unable to defend her refusal to return to her husband. The Mingotts had not proclaimed their disapproval aloud; their sense of solidarity was too strong. They had simply, as Mrs. Welland said, "let poor Ellen find her own level"—and that, mortifyingly and incomprehensibly, was in the dim depths where the Blenkers prevailed, and "people who wrote" celebrated their untidy rites. It was incredible, but it was a fact, that Ellen, in spite of all her opportunities and her privileges, had become simply "Bohemian." The fact enforced the contention that she had made a fatal mistake in not returning to Count Olenski. After all, a young woman's place was under her husband's roof, especially when she had left it in circumstances that... well... if one had cared to look into them...

"Madame Olenska is a great favorite with the gentlemen," said Miss Sophy, with her air of wishing to put forth something conciliatory when she knew she was planting a dart.

"Ah, that's the danger that a young woman like Madame Olenska is always exposed to," Mrs. Archer mournfully agreed; and the ladies, on this conclusion, gathered up their trains to seek the candle globes of the drawing-room, while Archer and Mr. Sillerton Jackson withdrew to the Gothic library.

Once established before the grate, and consoling himself for the inadequacy of the dinner by the perfection of his cigar, Mr. Jackson became portentous and communicative.

"If the Beaufort smash comes," he announced, "there are going to be disclosures."

Archer raised his head quickly; he could never hear the name without the sharp vision of Beaufort's heavy figure, opulently furred and shod, advancing through the snow at Skuytercliff.

"There's bound to be," Mr. Jackson continued, "the nastiest kind of a cleaning up. He hasn't spent all his money on Regina."

"Oh, well—that's discounted, isn't it? My belief is he'll pull out yet," said the young man, wanting to change the subject.

"Perhaps—perhaps. I know he was to see some of the influential people today. Of course," Mr. Jackson reluctantly conceded, "it's to be hoped they can tide him over—this time anyhow. I shouldn't like to think of poor Regina's spending the rest of her life in some shabby foreign watering-place for bankrupts."

Archer said nothing. It seemed to him so natural—however tragic—that money ill-gotten should be cruelly expiated, that his mind, hardly lingering over Mrs. Beaufort's doom, wandered back to closer questions. What was the meaning of May's blush when the Countess Olenska had been mentioned?

Four months had passed since the midsummer day that he and Madame Olenska had spent together; and since then he had not seen her. He knew that she had returned to Washington, to the little house which she and Medora Manson had taken there; he had written to her once—a few words, asking when they were to meet again—and she had even more briefly replied: "Not yet."

Since then there had been no further communication between them, and he had built up within himself a kind of sanctuary in which she throned among his secret thoughts and longings. Little by little, it became the scene of his real life, of his only rational activities; thither he brought the books he read, the ideas and feelings which nourished him, his judgments and his visions. Outside it, in the scene of his actual life, he moved with a growing sense of unreality and insufficiency, blundering against familiar prejudices and traditional points of view as an absent-minded man goes on bumping into the furniture of his own room. Absent—that was what he was: so absent from everything most densely real and near to those about him that it sometimes startled him to find they still imagined he was there.

He became aware that Mr. Jackson was clearing his throat preparatory to further revelations.

"I don't know, of course, how far your wife's family are aware of what people say about—well, about Madame Olenska's refusal to accept her husband's latest offer."

Archer was silent, and Mr. Jackson obliquely continued: "It's a pity—it's certainly a pity—that she refused it."

"A pity? In God's name, why?"

Mr. Jackson looked down his leg to the unwrinkled sock that joined it to a glossy pump.

"Well—to put it on the lowest ground—what's she going to live on now?"

"Now—?"

"If Beaufort—"

Archer sprang up, his fist banging down on the black walnut edge of the writing-table. The wells of the brass double-inkstand danced in their sockets.

"What the devil do you mean, sir?"

Mr. Jackson, shifting himself slightly in his chair, turned a tranquil gaze on the young man's burning face.

"Well—I have it on pretty good authority—in fact, on old Catherine's herself—that the family reduced Countess Olenska's allowance considerably when she definitely refused to go back to her husband; and as, by this refusal, she also forfeits the money

Wall Street the following day offered a glimmer of reassurance regarding Beaufort's precarious position. While the reports remained vague, they were undeniably hopeful. It was widely whispered that he had successfully leveraged powerful connections during an emergency, and indeed, that evening, when Mrs. Beaufort arrived at the Opera adorned with her familiar, radiant smile and a striking new emerald necklace, society collectively exhaled in relief.

Yet, New York remained a city of ironclad judgment regarding financial impropriety. Its unwritten law dictated that those who violated the sanctity of probity must pay the ultimate price, and everyone knew that neither Beaufort nor his wife would be spared this sacrifice. However, the prospect of offering them up was not merely painful; it was deeply inconvenient. Their sudden disappearance would leave a gaping void in their tightly knit social circle. Those too oblivious or indifferent to feel genuine horror at the impending moral catastrophe lamented in advance the loss of what was considered the finest ballroom in New York.

Archer had resolved to travel to Washington. He awaited only the filing date of the lawsuit he had discussed with May, hoping to align his visit with the legal proceedings. Yet, on the following Tuesday, Mr. Letterblair informed him that the case might be delayed for several weeks. Undeterred, Archer returned home that afternoon with the firm intention of departing the very next evening. He reasoned that May, unaware of his professional life and never having shown interest in it, would likely remain ignorant of any postponement, nor recall the names of the litigants if mentioned. More importantly, he could no longer delay seeing Madame Olenska; there were too many words he needed to speak to her.

On Wednesday morning, however, Mr. Letterblair met Archer at his office with a face etched in distress. Beaufort, it turned out, had failed to "tide over" the crisis. By circulating rumors of his recovery, he had temporarily soothed his depositors, causing a flood of payments until the previous evening, when alarming reports resurfaced. Consequently, a bank run had commenced, threatening to close the doors before sundown. The most damning accusations were already swirling about Beaufort's treacherous maneuvering, promising a failure that would rank among the most discreditable in Wall Street history.

The magnitude of the disaster left Mr. Letterblair pallid and incapacitated. "I have witnessed worse in my time," he confessed, "but nothing as catastrophic as this. Everyone we know will be affected, directly or indirectly. And what of Mrs. Beaufort? What *can* be done for her? I pity Mrs. Manson Mingott as much as anyone; at her age, the repercussions of this affair are unpredictable. She always believed in Beaufort—she made a friend of him! Then there is the entire Dallas connection; poor Mrs. Beaufort is related to every single one of us. Her only recourse would be to abandon her husband, yet how can one possibly tell her so? Her duty lies at his side, and fortunately, she has always been blind to his private failings."

A knock interrupted his lament. Turning sharply, he demanded, "What is it? I cannot be disturbed."

A clerk entered bearing a letter for Archer and withdrew. Recognizing his wife's handwriting, the young man opened the envelope and read: *"Won't you please come up town as early as you can? Granny suffered a slight stroke last night. In some inexplicable manner, she discovered this awful news about the bank before anyone else. Uncle Lovell is away hunting, and the weight of this disgrace has left poor Papa so nervous he has a fever and cannot leave his room. Mamma needs you desperately, and I sincerely hope you can depart immediately and go straight to Granny's."*

Archer passed the note to his senior partner. Moments later, he was squeezed into a crowded horse-car, transferring at Fourteenth Street to one of the swaying omnibuses of the Fifth Avenue line. It was past noon when this arduous vehicle deposited him at old Catherine's residence. The sitting-room window on the ground floor, usually occupied by her commanding presence, was instead filled by the frail figure of her daughter, Mrs. Welland, who offered a haggard welcome upon catching sight of Archer. At the door, May awaited him. The hall bore the unnatural stillness of a pristine home suddenly invaded by illness: wraps and furs lay in disarray on the chairs, a doctor's bag and coat rested on the table, surrounded by a growing pile of unheeded letters and cards.

May appeared pale yet managed a smile.
