Dr. Bencomb, who had visited for the second time, held a more optimistic view, and Mrs. Mingott's indomitable will to survive was already beginning to influence her family. May guided Archer into the old lady's sitting-room, where the sliding doors to the bedroom were drawn shut, draped behind heavy yellow damask portières. Here, Mrs. Welland whispered the horrific details of the catastrophe in terrified tones. It appeared that the evening before, something dreadful and mysterious had transpired. Around eight o'clock, shortly after Mrs. Mingott concluded her customary game of solitaire, the doorbell rang. A lady, so heavily veiled that the servants failed to recognize her immediately, requested an audience.

The butler, hearing a familiar voice, threw open the sitting-room door, announcing, "Mrs. Julius Beaufort," before closing it again on the two women. They must have remained together for an hour. When Mrs. Mingott finally rang her bell, Mrs. Beaufort had already slipped away unseen. The old lady, white, vast, and terrifying, sat alone in her great chair, signaling the butler to assist her to her room. At that moment, though visibly distressed, she seemed in complete command of her faculties. The mulatto maid settled her in bed, served her tea as usual, arranged the room, and departed. But at three in the morning, the bell rang again. Hastening in at this unusual summons—for old Catherine usually slept like a child—the two servants found their mistress propped against her pillows, wearing a crooked smile, with one small hand hanging limp from its massive arm.

The stroke proved to be relatively minor, as she was able to articulate her thoughts and convey her wishes. Soon after the doctor's initial visit, she began to regain control of her facial muscles. Yet the alarm had been profound, and equally intense was the outrage when it became clear from Mrs. Mingott's fragmented phrases that Regina Beaufort had come to implore her—incredible audacity—to support her husband, to see them through, not to "desert" them, as she termed it, but to compel the entire family to shield and condone their monstrous dishonor.

"I told her: 'Honor has always been honor, and honesty honesty, in the Manson Mingott house, and will be until I am carried out of it feet first,'" the old woman had stammered into her daughter's ear, her voice thick with partial paralysis. "And when she said, 'But my name, Auntie—my name is Regina Dallas,' I replied, 'It was Beaufort when he covered you with jewels, and it must remain Beaufort now that he has covered you with shame.'"

With tears and gasps of horror, Mrs. Welland recounted this, blanched and shattered by the unprecedented necessity of confronting the unpleasant and the discreditable. "If only I could keep it from your father-in-law," she wailed. "He always says, 'Augusta, for pity's sake, do not destroy my last illusions.' How am I to prevent him from knowing these horrors?"

"After all, Mamma, he hasn't *seen* them," her daughter suggested gently. Mrs. Welland sighed. "Ah, no; thank heaven he is safe in bed. Dr. Bencomb has promised to keep him there until poor Mamma recovers, and Regina has been spirited away somewhere."

Archer sat near the window, gazing blankly at the deserted street. It was evident he had been summoned less for practical aid than for the moral support of the stricken ladies. Mr. Lovell Mingott had been telegraphed for, and handwritten messages were being dispatched to family members across New York. Meanwhile, the only activity was the hushed discussion of Beaufort's disgrace and his wife's indefensible actions.

Mrs. Lovell Mingott, who had been writing notes in another room, reappeared and joined the conversation. In their era, the elder ladies agreed, the wife of a man who committed a business disgrace had only one option: to efface herself and disappear with him. "There was the case of poor Grandmamma Spicer," Mrs. Welland noted, referring to May's great-grandmother. "Of course," she hastened to add, "your great-grandfather's financial difficulties were private—gambling losses or signing notes for others—I never fully knew, because Mamma would never speak of it. But she was raised in the country because her mother had to flee New York after the disgrace, whatever it was. They lived up the Hudson alone, winter and summer, until Mamma was sixteen. It would never have occurred to Grandmamma Spicer to ask the family to 'countenance' her, as I understand Regina calls it; though a private disgrace pales in comparison to the scandal of ruining hundreds of innocent people."

"Yes, it would be more becoming for Regina to hide her own countenance than to speak of others'," Mrs. Lovell Mingott concurred. "I understand that the emerald necklace she wore at the Opera last Friday had been sent on approval from Ball and Black's in the afternoon. I wonder if they will ever retrieve it?"

Archer listened impassively to the relentless chorus. The concept of absolute financial probity as the cornerstone of a gentleman's code was too deeply ingrained in him for sentimental considerations to weaken it. An adventurer like Lemuel Struthers might accumulate millions through shady dealings, but unblemished honesty was the *noblesse oblige* of old financial New York. Nor did Mrs. Beaufort's fate greatly move Archer. He felt, perhaps, more sorrow for her than her indignant relatives; yet it seemed to him that the bond between husband and wife, even if breakable in prosperity, should be indissoluble in misfortune. As Mr. Letterblair had noted, a wife's place was at her husband's side in trouble, but society's place was not. Mrs. Beaufort's cool assumption that it was seemed almost to make her his accomplice. The mere notion of a woman appealing to her family to screen her husband's business dishonor was inadmissible, for it was the one thing the Family, as an institution, could not do.

The mulatto maid summoned Mrs. Lovell Mingott to the hall, and she returned moments later with a frowning brow. "She wants me to telegraph for Ellen Olenska. I had written to Ellen, of course, and to Medora; but now it seems that is insufficient. I am to telegraph to her immediately, instructing her to come alone."

The announcement hung in the air, met with silence. Mrs. Welland sighed resignedly, and May rose to gather scattered newspapers.

"I suppose it must be done," Mrs. Lovell Mingott continued, as if hoping to be contradicted. May turned back toward the center of the room.

"Of course it must be done," she said. "Granny knows what she wants, and we must fulfill all her wishes. Shall I write the telegram for you, Auntie? If it goes immediately, Ellen can probably catch tomorrow morning's train." She pronounced the syllables of the name with a peculiar clarity, as if tapping two silver bells.

"Well, it cannot go at once. Jasper and the pantry-boy are both out with notes and telegrams."

May turned to her husband with a smile. "But here is Newland, ready to do anything. Will you take the telegram, Newland? There will be just time before luncheon."

Archer rose with a murmured agreement. May seated herself at old Catherine's rosewood "Bonheur du Jour" and wrote the message in her large, immature hand. Once finished, she blotted it neatly and handed it to Archer.

"What a pity," she remarked, "that you and Ellen will cross paths on the way!" Turning to her mother and aunt, she added, "Newland is obliged to go to Washington regarding a patent lawsuit coming before the Supreme Court. I suppose Uncle Lovell will return by tomorrow night, and with Granny improving so much, it does not seem right to ask Newland to forego an important engagement for the firm—does it?"

She paused, awaiting an answer. Mrs. Welland hastily declared, "Oh, of course not, darling. Your Granny would be the last person to wish it." As Archer left the room with the telegram, he heard his mother-in-law mutter, presumably to Mrs. Lovell Mingott, "But why on earth she should make you telegraph for Ellen Olenska..." before May's clear voice rejoined, "Perhaps it is to urge her again that after all, her duty lies with her husband."

The outer door closed behind Archer, and he walked hurriedly away toward the telegraph office.

"Ol... ol... how do you spell it, anyhow?" asked the sharp-tongued young lady to whom Archer had just slid his wife's telegram across the brass ledge of the Western Union office.

"Olenska—O-len-ska," he repeated, pulling the message back to print the foreign syllables above May's sprawling script.

"It's a peculiar name for a New York telegraph office; at least, in this part of town," an unexpected voice remarked. Turning, Archer found Lawrence Lefferts standing at his elbow, tugging at an impassive mustache and pretending not to look at the paper.

"Hallo, Newland. Thought I'd catch you here. I just heard about old Mrs. Mingott's stroke, and since I was heading to the house, I saw you turning down this street and followed. Did you come from there?"

Archer nodded and shoved the telegram under the lattice.

"Very bad, eh?" Lefferts continued. "Wiring the family, I suppose. I gather it *is* bad, if you're including the Countess Olenska."

Archer's lips tightened. A savage urge surged within him to drive his fist into the long, vain, handsome face beside him.

"Why?" he asked.

Lefferts, a man known for avoiding discussion, raised his eyebrows in an ironic grimace that warned Archer of the watching girl behind the lattice. Nothing could be worse "form" than displaying temper in public. Archer had never been more indifferent to social propriety, yet his impulse to harm Lawrence Lefferts was fleeting. The thought of bandying Ellen Olenska's name with him at such a moment, under any pretext, was unthinkable. He paid for the telegram, and the two young men stepped out into the street together. Once his composure returned, Archer said, "Mrs. Mingott is much better; the doctor feels no anxiety whatever." Lefferts, expressing profuse relief, then asked if Archer had heard the beastly rumors swirling again about Beaufort.

That afternoon, the announcement of Beaufort's failure dominated every newspaper, eclipsing the report of Mrs. Manson Mingott's stroke. Only those aware of the mysterious link between the two events considered attributing old Catherine's illness to anything other than the accumulation of flesh and years. The entire city was darkened by the tale of Beaufort's dishonor. As Mr. Letterblair noted, there had never been a worse case in his memory, nor in the memory of the distant Letterblair who gave the firm its name. The bank had continued accepting deposits for a full day after its collapse was inevitable. Since many clients belonged to the ruling clans, Beaufort's duplicity seemed doubly cynical. Had Mrs. Beaufort not adopted the tone that such misfortunes were "the test of friendship," compassion for her might have softened the general indignation against her husband. As it was—and especially after the object of her nocturnal visit to Mrs. Manson Mingott became known—her cynicism was deemed to exceed his. She lacked the excuse, nor did her detractors find satisfaction in claiming she was "a foreigner." It offered some comfort (to those whose securities were safe) to remind themselves that Beaufort *was*; yet, if a Dallas of South Carolina took his view and glibly spoke of soon being "on his feet again," the argument lost its bite. There was nothing left but to accept this awful proof of the indissolubility of marriage. Society must manage without the Beauforts, and that was that—except for hapless victims like Medora Manson, the poor old Miss Lannings, and certain misguided ladies of good family who, if only they had listened to Mr. Henry van der Luyden...

"The best thing the Beauforts can do," declared Mrs. Archer, summing it up as if diagnosing a patient and prescribing a cure, "is to move to Regina's little place in North Carolina. Beaufort has always kept a racing stable; he should breed trotting horses. I'd say he possesses all the qualities of a successful horsedealer." Everyone agreed, though no one condescended to inquire what the Beauforts truly intended.

The next day, Mrs. Manson Mingott was significantly better. She recovered enough voice to order that no one mention the Beauforts to her again and, when Dr. Bencomb appeared, asked what in the world her family meant by making such a fuss about her health.

"If people of my age *will* eat chicken salad in the evening, what are they to expect?" she inquired. With the doctor opportunistically modifying her diet, the stroke transformed into an attack of indigestion. Yet, despite her firm tone, old Catherine did not fully regain her former attitude toward life. The growing remoteness of age, though it hadn't dimmed her curiosity about her neighbors, had dulled her never-very-lively compassion for their troubles. She seemed to have no trouble putting the Beaufort disaster from her mind. For the first time, she became absorbed in her own symptoms, developing a sentimental interest in certain family members to whom she had previously been contemptuously indifferent.

Mr. Welland, in particular, attracted her notice. Of her sons-in-law, he was the one she had most consistently ignored; all his wife's efforts to portray him as a man of forceful character and marked intellect (if he had only "chosen") had been met with a derisive chuckle. But his eminence as a hypochondriac now made him an object of engrossing interest. Mrs. Mingott issued an imperial summons for him to come and compare diets as soon as his temperature permitted, for old Catherine was now the first to recognize that one could not be too careful about temperatures.

Twenty-four hours after Madame Olenska's summons, a telegram announced she would arrive from Washington on the evening of the following day. At the Wellands', where the Newland Archers happened to be lunching, the question of who should meet her at Jersey City arose immediately. The material difficulties with which the Welland household struggled, as if it were a frontier outpost, lent animation to the debate. It was agreed that Mrs. Welland could not possibly go to Jersey City, as she was to accompany her husband to old Catherine's that afternoon, and the brougham could not be spared, since if Mr. Welland were "upset" by seeing his mother-in-law for the first time after her attack, he might need to be taken home at a moment's notice. The Welland sons would naturally be "down town," Mr. Lovell Mingott would be just returning from his shooting with his carriage engaged, and one could not ask May, at the close of a winter afternoon, to go alone across the ferry to Jersey City, even in her own carriage. Nevertheless, it might seem inhospitable—and contrary to old Catherine's express wishes—if Madame Olenska were allowed to arrive without any family member at the station. It was just like Ellen, Mrs. Welland's tired voice implied, to place the family in such a dilemma. "It's always one thing after another," the poor lady grieved, in one of her rare revolts against fate; "the only thing that makes me think Mamma must be less well than Dr. Bencomb admits is this morbid desire to have Ellen come at once, however inconvenient it is to meet her."

The words were thoughtless, as utterances of impatience often are, and Mr. Welland was upon them with a pounce.

"Augusta," he said, turning pale and laying down his fork, "do you have any other reason for thinking Bencomb is less reliable than he was? Have you noticed he has been less conscientious than usual in following up my case or your mother's?"

It was Mrs. Welland's turn to grow pale as the endless consequences of her blunder unrolled before her, but she managed to laugh and take a second helping of scalloped oysters before saying, struggling back into her old armor of cheerfulness: "My dear, how could you imagine such a thing? I only meant that, after the decided stand Mamma took about it being Ellen's duty to return to her husband, it seems strange she should be seized with this sudden whim to see her, when there are half a dozen other grandchildren she might have asked for. But we must never forget that Mamma, in spite of her wonderful vitality, is a very old woman."

Mr. Welland's brow remained clouded, and it was evident his perturbed imagination had fastened immediately on this last remark. "Yes: your mother is a very old woman; and for all we know, Bencomb may not be as successful with very old people. As you say, my dear, it's always one thing after another; and in another ten or fifteen years, I suppose, I shall have the pleasing duty of looking about for a new doctor. It's always better to make such a change before it's absolutely necessary." Having arrived at this Spartan decision, Mr. Welland firmly took up his fork.

"But all the while," Mrs. Welland began again as she rose from the luncheon table and led the way into the wilderness of purple satin and malachite known as the back drawing-room, "I don't see how Ellen is to be got here tomorrow evening; and I do like to have things settled for at least twenty-four hours ahead."

Archer turned from the fascinated contemplation of a small painting depicting two Cardinals carousing, framed in octagonal ebony set with onyx medallions.

"Shall I fetch her?" he proposed. "I can easily get away from the office in time to meet the brougham at the ferry, if May will send it there." His heart beat excitedly as he spoke.

Mrs. Welland heaved a sigh of gratitude, and May, who had moved to the window, turned to shower him with a beam of approval. "So you see, Mamma, everything *will* be settled twenty-four hours in advance," she said, stooping to kiss her mother's troubled forehead.

May's brougham awaited her at the door, and she was to drive Archer to Union Square, where he could catch a Broadway car to the office. As she settled into her corner, she said, "I didn't want to worry Mamma by raising fresh obstacles; but how can you meet Ellen tomorrow and bring her back to New York when you're going to Washington?"

"Oh, I'm not going," Archer answered.

"Not going? Why, what's happened?" Her voice was clear as a bell, full of wifely solicitude.

"The case is off—postponed."

"Postponed? How odd! I saw a note this morning from Mr. Letterblair to Mamma saying he was going to Washington tomorrow for the big patent case he was to argue before the Supreme Court. You said it was a patent case, didn't you?"

"Well—that's it: the whole office can't go. Letterblair decided to go this morning."

"Then it's *not* postponed?" she continued, with an insistence so unlike her that he felt blood rising to his face, as if he were blushing for her unwonted lapse from all traditional delicacies.

"No: but my going is," he answered, cursing the unnecessary explanations he had given when announcing his intention to go to Washington, and wondering where he had read that clever liars give details, but the cleverest do not. It did not hurt him half as much to tell May an untruth as to see her trying to pretend she had not detected him.

"I'm not going till later on: luckily for the convenience of your family," he continued, taking base refuge in sarcasm. As he spoke, he felt her looking at him and turned his eyes to hers to avoid appearing to evade them. Their glances met for a second, perhaps letting them into each other's meanings more deeply than either cared to admit.

"Yes; it *is* awfully convenient," May brightly agreed, "that you should be able to meet Ellen after all; you saw how much Mamma appreciated your offering to do it."

"Oh, I'm delighted to do it." The carriage stopped, and as he jumped out, she leaned toward him and laid her hand on his. "Good-bye, dearest," she said, her eyes so blue that he wondered afterward if they had shone on him through tears.

He turned away and hurried across Union Square, repeating to himself in a sort of inward chant: "It's all of two hours from Jersey City to old Catherine's. It's all of two hours—and it may be more."

The dark blue brougham, still gleaming with the fresh varnish of their wedding day, waited for Archer at the ferry. It carried him in luxurious comfort to the Pennsylvania terminus in Jersey City. Outside, the afternoon was heavy with snow, the air sombre and grey, while inside the vast, echoing station, gas lamps cast a warm, artificial glow.

As Archer paced the platform, waiting for the Washington Express, he found himself thinking of the dreamers who spoke of a tunnel boring beneath the Hudson, allowing trains to rush straight into New York. They were part of a brotherhood of visionaries who also predicted ships crossing the Atlantic in five days, flying machines, electric light, and wireless telephony—marvels straight out of an Arabian Nights tale.

"I don't care which of these fantasies comes true," Archer mused, "provided the tunnel remains unbuilt." In a state of boyish, irrational happiness, he conjured a scene: Madame Olenska stepping from the train, his discovery of her amidst a sea of indifferent faces, her taking his arm as he led her to the carriage. He imagined their slow journey to the wharf, navigating slipping horses and shouting cart drivers, before settling into the quiet of the ferry boat. There, side by side under the falling snow, they would watch the earth glide away beneath them, rolling toward the other side of the sun. It was impossible to count the things he needed to say to her, the words forming in his mouth with such eloquence.

The train's clanging and groaning grew louder, staggering slowly into the station like a beast returning to its lair. Archer pushed through the crowd, staring blindly into the windows of the high carriages. Then, suddenly, he saw her. Madame Olenska's pale, surprised face appeared close at hand, and he felt the familiar, mortifying sensation of forgetting exactly what she looked like.

They met, their hands joined, and he drew her arm through his. "This way—I have the carriage," he said.

Everything unfolded exactly as he had dreamed. He helped her into the brougham with her bags, and later recalled vaguely reassuring her about her grandmother and giving her a summary of the situation at Beaufort. He noted the softness in her voice when she whispered, "Poor Regina!" Meanwhile, the carriage wound its way out of the station's chaotic coils, crawling down the slippery incline toward the wharf. They were surrounded by swaying coal carts, bewildered horses, and disheveled wagons. Then, an empty hearse passed by.

Archer watched her shut her eyes and clutch his hand tightly.

"If only it doesn't mean... poor Granny!"

"Oh, no, no—she is much better. She is quite all right. There—we have passed it!" he exclaimed, as if mentioning it aloud could banish the fear. Her hand remained in his. As the carriage lurched across the gangplank onto the ferry, he bent down, unbuttoned her tight brown glove, and kissed her palm with the reverence of someone touching a relic. She pulled away with a faint smile.

"You didn't expect me today?"

"Oh, no."

"I intended to go to Washington to see you. I had made all my arrangements; I very nearly missed you on the train."

"Oh—" she exclaimed, as though terrified by how close they had come to missing one another.

"Do you know... I hardly remembered you?"

"Hardly remembered me?"

"I mean, how shall I explain? I—it happens every time. Each time you happen to me all over again."

"Oh, yes. I know. I know!"

"Does it—to you?" he insisted.

She nodded, gazing out the window.

"Ellen—Ellen—Ellen!"

She offered no answer. He sat in silence, watching her profile blur against the snow-streaked dusk beyond the glass. What had she done in those four long months? How little they truly knew of each other! The precious moments slipped away, leaving him helpless to brood over the mystery of their simultaneous closeness and distance, symbolized by their physical proximity and their inability to see each other's faces clearly.

"What a pretty carriage! Is it May's?" she asked suddenly, turning from the window.

"Yes."

"It was May who sent you to fetch me, then? How kind of her!"

For a moment, he said nothing. Then, explosively: "Your husband's secretary came to see me the day after we met in Boston."

In his brief letter, he had made no mention of M. Rivière's visit, intending to bury the incident within himself. But her reminder that they were sitting in his wife's carriage provoked a sudden impulse of retaliation. He would see if she preferred his reference to Rivière to her reference to May! As on other occasions when he expected to shake her out of her usual composure, she showed no sign of surprise. Immediately, he concluded: "He writes to her, then."

"M. Rivière went to see you?"

"Yes: didn't you know?"

"No," she answered simply.

"And you're not surprised?"

She hesitated. "Why should I be? He told me in Boston that he knew you; that he'd met you in England, I think."

"Ellen—I must ask you one thing."

"Yes."

"I wanted to ask it after I saw him, but I couldn't put it in a letter. It was Rivière who helped you to get away when you left your husband?"

His heart hammered against his ribs. Would she meet this question with the same calm composure?

"Yes: I owe him a great debt," she answered, her quiet voice devoid of any tremor.

Her tone was so natural, so almost indifferent, that Archer's inner turmoil subsided. Once again, through her sheer simplicity, she had made him feel stupidly conventional just when he thought he was casting convention aside.

"I think you're the most honest woman I have ever met!" he exclaimed.

"Oh, no—but probably one of the least fussy," she replied, a smile in her voice.

"Call it what you like: you look at things as they are."

"Ah—I've had to. I've had to look at the Gorgon."

"Well—it hasn't blinded you! You've seen that she's just an old bogey like all the others."

"She doesn't blind one; but she dries up one's tears."

The answer halted the pleading rising in Archer's throat; it seemed to come from depths of experience beyond his reach. The ferry boat's slow advance had ceased, and its bow bumped violently against the piles of the slip, causing the brougham to stagger and throw Archer and Madame Olenska against one another. The young man, trembling, felt the pressure of her shoulder and passed his arm around her.

"If you're not blind, then you must see that this cannot last."

"What cannot?"

"Our being together—and not together."

"No. You ought not to have come today," she said, her voice altered. Suddenly, she turned, threw her arms around him, and pressed her lips to his. At that exact moment, the carriage began to move, and a gas lamp at the head of the slip flashed its light into the window. She drew away, and they sat silent and motionless while the brougham struggled through the congestion of carriages at the ferry landing.

As they gained the street, Archer began to speak hurriedly.

"Don't be afraid of me: you needn't squeeze yourself back into your corner like that. A stolen kiss isn't what I want. Look: I'm not even trying to touch the sleeve of your jacket. Don't suppose that I don't understand your reasons for not wanting this feeling between us to dwindle into an ordinary, secret affair. I couldn't have spoken like this yesterday, because when we are apart and I am looking forward to seeing you, every thought is consumed by a great flame. But then you come; and you are so much more than I remembered, and what I want of you is so much more than an hour or two every now and then, with wastes of thirsty waiting in between, that I can sit perfectly still beside you, like this, with that other vision in my mind, quietly trusting it to come true."

For a moment, she made no reply. Then, barely above a whisper, she asked: "What do you mean by trusting to it to come true?"

"Why—you know it will, don't you?"

"Your vision of you and me together?" She burst into a sudden, hard laugh. "You choose your place well to put it to me!"

"Do you mean because we are in my wife's brougham? Shall we get out and walk, then? I don't suppose you mind a little snow?"

She laughed again, more gently. "No; I shall not get out and walk, because my business is to get to Granny's as quickly as I can. And you will sit beside me, and we will look, not at visions, but at realities."

"I don't know what you mean by realities. The only reality to me is this."

She met his words with a long silence. During this pause, the carriage rolled down an obscure side street and then turned into the searching illumination of Fifth Avenue.

"Is it your idea, then, that I should live with you as your mistress—since I cannot be your wife?" she asked.

The crudeness of the question startled him; the word was one women of his class fought shy of, even when their conversation flirted closest with the topic. He noticed that Madame Olenska pronounced it as if it held a recognized place in her vocabulary, and he wondered if it had been used familiarly in her presence in the terrible life she had fled. Her question jerked him to a halt, and he floundered.

"I want—I want somehow to get away with you into a world where words like that, categories like that, won't exist. Where we shall be simply two human beings who love each other, who are the whole of life to each other; and nothing else on earth will matter."

She drew a deep sigh that ended in another laugh. "Oh, my dear—where is that country? Have you ever been there?" she asked. As he remained sullenly dumb, she continued: "I know so many who have tried to find it; and, believe me, they all got out by mistake at wayside stations: at places like Boulogne, or Pisa, or Monte Carlo—and it wasn't at all different from the old world they'd left, but only rather smaller, dingier, and more promiscuous."

He had never heard her speak in such a tone, and he remembered the phrase she had used a short while before.

"Yes, the Gorgon *has* dried your tears," he said.

"Well, she opened my eyes too; it is a delusion to say that she blinds people. What she does is just the contrary—she fastens their eyelids open, so that they are never again in the blessed darkness. Isn't there a Chinese torture like that? There ought to be. Ah, believe me, it is a miserable little country!"

The carriage had crossed Forty-second Street; May's sturdy horse was carrying them northward as if he were a Kentucky trotter. Archer choked with the sense of wasted minutes and vain words.

"Then what, exactly, is your plan for us?" he asked.

"For *us*? But there is no *us* in that sense! We are near each other only if we stay far from each other. Then we can be ourselves. Otherwise, we are only Newland Archer, the husband of Ellen Olenska's cousin, and Ellen Olenska, the cousin of Newland Archer's wife, trying to be happy behind the backs of the people who trust them."

"Ah, I am beyond that," he groaned.

"No, you are not! You have never been beyond. And I have," she said, in a strange voice, "and I know what it looks like there."

He sat silent, dazed with inarticulate pain. Then, he groped in the darkness of the carriage for the little bell that signaled orders to the coachman. He remembered that May rang twice when she wished to stop. He pressed the bell, and the carriage drew up beside the curbstone.

"Why are we stopping? This is not Granny's," Madame Olenska exclaimed.

"No: I shall get out here," he stammered, opening the door and jumping to the pavement. By the light of a street lamp, he saw her startled face and the instinctive motion she made to detain him. He closed the door and leaned for a moment in the window.

"You are right: I ought not to have come today," he said, lowering his voice so the coachman would not hear. She bent forward, seeming about to speak; but he had already called out the order to drive on, and the carriage rolled away while he stood on the corner.

The snow had stopped, and a tingling wind had sprung up, lashing his face as he stood gazing. Suddenly, he felt something stiff and cold on his lashes and realized he had been crying, and that the wind had frozen his tears.

He thrust his hands into his pockets and walked at a sharp pace down Fifth Avenue to his own house.

That evening, when Archer descended the stairs before dinner, the drawing-room stood empty. The household had retreated into silence; every social engagement had been postponed in the wake of Mrs. Manson Mingott's illness, leaving the couple to dine alone. May, usually the more punctual of the two, had not yet joined him. Archer knew she was home—he had heard the soft rustle of her movements in her room while he dressed—but he was struck by a sudden, uncharacteristic delay.

In recent days, he had cultivated a habit of dwelling on such conjectures, using them to anchor his drifting thoughts to the solid ground of reality. Sometimes, he mused, he had glimpsed the key to his father-in-law's obsession with trivialities. Perhaps Mr. Welland, long ago, had experienced his own escapes and visions, conjuring up entire hostilities of domesticity merely to defend himself against them.

When May finally appeared, she looked exhausted. She wore the low-necked, tightly laced dinner dress required by Mingott ceremony even on the most informal nights, her fair hair coiled into its usual intricate accumulation. Yet her face was wan, almost faded, though her eyes retained the brilliant blue dazzle of the previous day. She greeted him with her customary tenderness.

"What became of you, dear?" she asked gently. "I was waiting at Granny's, and Ellen came alone to say she'd dropped you on the way because you had to rush off on business. Is everything alright?"

"Only some letters I forgot," he replied, surprised by her insistence. "I wanted to get them off before dinner."

"Ah—" she murmured, then paused. "I'm sorry you didn't come to Granny's, unless those letters were truly urgent."

"They were," he said, feeling a flicker of irritation at her scrutiny. "Besides, I didn't know you were there."

She turned toward the mirror above the mantelpiece, lifting a long arm to adjust a puff that had slipped from her elaborate hairstyle. Watching her, Archer felt a strange heaviness settle in his chest. There was something languid and inelastic in her posture, as if the deadly monotony of their lives had already begun to weigh upon her. He remembered that this morning, as he left the house, she had called down the stairs that they would meet at her grandmother's to drive home together. He had called back a cheerful "Yes!" and, lost in his own visions, had forgotten his promise. Now, guilt smote him, mingled with annoyance that such a trifling omission should be stored away against him after nearly two years of marriage.

He was weary of living in a perpetual, tepid honeymoon—devoid of the temperature of passion yet burdened by all its demands. If May had voiced her grievances, he suspected she harbored many, he might have laughed them away. But she was trained to conceal imaginary wounds beneath a Spartan smile.

To mask his own unease, he asked how her grandmother was. May answered that Mrs. Mingott was improving but had been disturbed by the latest news regarding the Beauforts.

"What news?" Archer asked.

"It seems they are staying in New York," she said. "I believe he is entering an insurance business or something similar. They are looking for a small house."

The absurdity of the situation was undeniable, and they proceeded to dinner. Their conversation remained within its usual narrow circle, yet Archer noted that his wife made no mention of Madame Olenska, nor of Old Catherine's reception of her. He felt a vague sense of foreboding, though he was secretly grateful for the silence.

Later, in the library, Archer lit a cigar and opened a volume of Michelet. He had recently taken to history in the evenings since May had begun asking him to read aloud whenever she saw him with poetry. It was not that he disliked the sound of his own voice; rather, he could always foresee her comments. In the days of their engagement, she had simply echoed his words, but since he had stopped providing opinions, she had begun to hazard her own, with results destructive to his enjoyment.

Seeing that he had chosen history, she fetched her workbasket, drew up an armchair to the green-shaded student lamp, and uncovered a cushion she was embroidering for his sofa. She was not a skilled needlewoman; her large, capable hands were made for riding, rowing, and open-air activities. Yet, since other wives embroidered cushions for their husbands, she did not wish to omit this final link in her devotion.

She sat so that Archer, by merely raising his eyes, could see her bent over her workframe, her ruffled elbow-sleeves slipping back from her firm arms, the betrothal sapphire shining on her left hand above her broad gold wedding ring, and her right hand slowly, laboriously stabbing the canvas. Bathed in the lamplight, with her clear brow illuminated, he felt a secret dismay rise within him. He realized that he would always know the thoughts behind her eyes; that never, in all the years to come, would she surprise him with an unexpected mood, a new idea, a weakness, a cruelty, or an emotion. She had spent her poetry and romance on their short courtship; the function was exhausted because the need was past. Now, she was simply ripening into a copy of her mother, and mysteriously, through that very process, trying to turn him into a Mr. Welland.

He laid down his book and stood up impatiently. At once, she raised her head.

"What's the matter?"

"The room is stifling," he said. "I want a little air."

He had insisted that the library curtains slide backward and forward on a rod, allowing them to be closed in the evening, rather than remaining nailed to a gilt cornice and immovably looped up over layers of lace as in the drawing-room. He pulled them back and pushed up the sash, leaning out into the icy night. The mere act of not looking at May, seated beside his table under his lamp, of seeing other houses, roofs, and chimneys, of grasping the sense of other lives outside his own and other cities beyond New York, cleared his brain and made it easier to breathe.

After leaning out into the darkness for a few minutes, he heard her say, "Newland! Do shut the window. You'll catch your death."

He pulled the sash down and turned back. "Catch my death!" he echoed, and felt the urge to add, "But I've caught it already. I *am* dead—I've been dead for months and months."

Suddenly, the play of the word sparked a wild suggestion. What if it were *she* who was dead? If she were going to die—to die soon—and leave him free! The sensation of standing there in that warm, familiar room, looking at her, and wishing her dead, was so strange, so fascinating, and overmastering that its enormity did not immediately strike him. He simply felt that chance had offered him a new possibility to which his sick soul might cling. Yes, May might die; people did: young, healthy people like herself. She might die, and set him suddenly free.

She glanced up, and he saw by her widening eyes that there must be something strange in his own.

"Newland! Are you ill?"

He shook his head and turned toward his armchair. She bent over her workframe, and as he passed, he laid his hand on her hair. "Poor May!" he said.

"Poor? Why poor?" she echoed with a strained laugh.

"Because I shall never be able to open a window without worrying you," he rejoined, laughing also.

For a moment she was silent; then, her head bowed over her work, she said very softly, "I shall never worry if you're happy."

"Ah, my dear; and I shall never be happy unless I can open the windows!"

"In *this* weather?" she remonstrated. With a sigh, he buried his head in his book.

Six or seven days passed. Archer heard nothing from Madame Olenska, and he became aware that her name would not be mentioned in his presence by any member of the family. He did not try to see her; to do so while she was at Old Catherine's guarded bedside would have been almost impossible. In the uncertainty of the situation, he let himself drift, conscious, somewhere below the surface of his thoughts, of a resolve that had come to him when he leaned out from his library window into the icy night. The strength of that resolve made it easy to wait and make no sign.

Then one day, May told him that Mrs. Manson Mingott had asked to see him. There was nothing surprising in the request, for the old lady was steadily recovering, and she had always openly declared that she preferred Archer to any of her other grandsons-in-law. May delivered the message with evident pleasure; she was proud of Old Catherine's appreciation of her husband.

There was a moment's pause, and then Archer felt it incumbent on him to say, "All right. Shall we go together this afternoon?"

His wife's face brightened, but she instantly answered, "Oh, you'd much better go alone. It bores Granny to see the same people too often."

Archer's heart beat violently as he rang Old Mrs. Mingott's bell. He had wanted above all things to go alone, for he felt sure the visit would give him the chance to say a word in private to the Countess Olenska. He had determined to wait until the chance presented itself naturally; and here it was, and here he was on the doorstep. Behind the door, behind the curtains of the yellow damask room next to the hall, she was surely awaiting him; in another moment he should see her, and be able to speak to her before she led him to the sick-room.

He wanted only to put one question; after that, his course would be clear. What he wished to ask was simply the date of her return to Washington, and that question she could hardly refuse to answer.

But in the yellow sitting-room, it was the mulatto maid who waited. Her white teeth shining like a keyboard, she pushed back the sliding doors and ushered him into Old Catherine's presence.

The old woman sat in a vast, throne-like armchair near her bed. Beside her was a mahogany stand bearing a cast-bronze lamp with an engraved globe, over which a green paper shade had been balanced. There was not a book or a newspaper in reach, nor any evidence of feminine employment: conversation had always been Mrs. Mingott's sole pursuit, and she would have scorned to feign an interest in fancywork.

Archer saw no trace of the slight distortion left by her stroke. She merely looked paler, with darker shadows in the folds and recesses of her obesity. In the fluted mob-cap tied by a starched bow between her first two chins, and the muslin kerchief crossed over her billowing purple dressing gown, she seemed like some shrewd and kindly ancestress of her own who might have yielded too freely to the pleasures of the table.

She held out one of the little hands that nestled in a hollow of her huge lap like pet animals and called to the maid, "Don't let in anyone else. If my daughters call, say I'm asleep."

The maid disappeared, and the old lady turned to her grandson.

"My dear, am I perfectly hideous?" she asked gaily, launching out one hand in search of the folds of muslin on her inaccessible bosom. "My daughters tell me it doesn't matter at my age—as if hideousness didn't matter all the more the harder it gets to conceal!"

"My dear, you're handsomer than ever!" Archer rejoined in the same tone, and she threw back her head and laughed.

"Ah, but not as handsome as Ellen!" she jerked out, twinkling at him maliciously. Before he could answer, she added, "Was she so awfully handsome the day you drove her up from the ferry?"

He laughed, and she continued, "Was it because you told her so that she had to put you out on the way? In my youth, young men didn't desert pretty women unless they were made to!" She gave another chuckle and interrupted it to say almost querulously, "It's a pity she didn't marry you; I always told her so. It would have spared me all this worry. But who ever thought of sparing their grandmother worry?"

Archer wondered if her illness had blurred her faculties, but suddenly she broke out: "Well, it's settled, anyhow: she's going to stay with me, whatever the rest of the family says! She hadn't been here five minutes before I'd have gone down on my knees to keep her—if only, for the last twenty years, I'd been able to see where the floor was!"

Archer listened in silence, and she went on: "They'd talked me over, as no doubt you know: persuaded me, Lovell, and Letterblair, and Augusta Welland, and all the rest of them, that I must hold out and cut off her allowance, till she was made to see that it was her duty to go back to Olenski. They thought they'd convinced me when the secretary, or whatever he was, came out with the last proposals: handsome proposals I confess they were. After all, marriage is marriage, and money's money—both useful things in their way... and I didn't know what to answer—" She broke off and drew a long breath, as if speaking had become an effort. "But the minute I laid eyes on her, I said: 'You sweet bird, you! Shut you up in that cage again? Never!' And now it's settled that she's to stay here and nurse her Granny as long as there's a Granny to nurse. It's not a gay prospect, but she doesn't mind; and of course I've told Letterblair that she's to be given her proper allowance."

The young man heard her with veins aglow, but in his confusion of mind, he hardly knew whether her news brought joy or pain. He had so definitely decided on the course he meant to pursue that for the moment he could not readjust his thoughts. But gradually there stole over him the delicious sense of difficulties deferred and opportunities miraculously provided. If Ellen had consented to come and live with her grandmother, it must surely be because she had recognized the impossibility of giving him up. This was her answer to his final

The news from Old Catherine had left Archer stunned, a sensation entirely predictable given the gravity of the revelation. It was natural enough that Madame Olenska would hasten back from Washington to her grandmother's summons; indeed, it was the duty of a granddaughter. But the decision to remain under the roof of the Mingotts, especially now that Mrs. Mingott's health had nearly recovered, defied easy explanation.

Archer was certain that Madame Olenska's choice was not driven by financial necessity. He knew the precise, meager sum her husband had allotted her since their separation. Without the generous allowance of her grandmother, it was scarcely enough to sustain a life according to the Mingott standard of living; and with Medora Manson, her constant companion, now ruined, that pittance would barely cover the basic needs of two women. Yet, Archer was convinced that Madame Olenska had not accepted her grandmother's offer out of desperation or calculation.

She possessed a heedless generosity and a spasmodic extravagance characteristic of those accustomed to vast fortunes, rendering them indifferent to monetary constraints. However, she could dispense with many things her relatives deemed essential, and Mrs. Lovell Mingott and Mrs. Welland had often lamented that anyone who had enjoyed the cosmopolitan luxuries of Count Olenski's establishments cared so little for "how things were done." Furthermore, Archer knew that several months had passed since her allowance was cut off, yet during that interval, she had made no effort to regain her grandmother's favor. If she had altered her course, it must be for a reason entirely different from money.

He did not need to search far for that reason. On the journey from the ferry, she had told him that they must remain apart, but she had spoken with her head resting on his breast. Archer knew there was no calculated coquetry in her words; she was fighting her fate as he had fought his, clinging desperately to her resolve that they should not break faith with the people who trusted them. Yet, during the ten days since her return to New York, she might have guessed from his silence and his failure to visit that he was contemplating a decisive step, one from which there was no turning back. At the thought, a sudden fear of her own weakness might have seized her, leading her to feel that it was better to accept the compromise usual in such cases and follow the line of least resistance.

Just an hour earlier, when he had rung Mrs. Mingott's bell, Archer had fancied his path was clear. He had intended to speak alone with Madame Olenska, and failing that, to learn from her grandmother the day and train of her return to Washington. In that train, he intended to join her, traveling with her to Washington or as far as she was willing to go. His fancy inclined toward Japan; at any rate, she would understand immediately that wherever she went, he was going. He meant to leave a note for May that would cut off any other alternative.

He had fancied himself not only nerved for this plunge but eager to take it. Yet, his first feeling upon hearing that the course of events had changed had been one of relief. Now, however, as he walked home from Mrs. Mingott's, he was conscious of a growing distaste for what lay before him. There was nothing unknown or unfamiliar in the path he was presumably to tread; but when he had trodden it before, it was as a free man, accountable to no one for his actions, able to lend himself with an amused detachment to the game of precautions, prevarications, concealments, and compliances required by the situation. This procedure was called "protecting a woman's honor," and the best fiction, combined with the after-dinner talk of his elders, had long since initiated him into every detail of its code.

Now he saw the matter in a new light, and his part in it seemed singularly diminished. It was, in fact, that which, with a secret fatuity, he had watched Mrs. Thorley Rushworth play toward a fond and unperceiving husband: a smiling, bantering, humor-ing, watchful, and incessant lie. A lie by day, a lie by night, a lie in every touch and every look; a lie in every caress and every quarrel; a lie in every word and in every silence.

It was easier, and less dastardly on the whole, for a wife to play such a part toward her husband. A woman's standard of truthfulness was tacitly held to be lower; she was the subject creature, versed in the arts of the enslaved. Then she could always plead moods and nerves, and the right not to be held too strictly to account; and even in the most strait-laced societies, the laugh was always against the husband.

But in Archer's little world, no one laughed at a wife deceived, and a certain measure of contempt was attached to men who continued their philandering after marriage. In the rotation of crops, there was a recognized season for wild oats, but they were not to be sown more than once.

Archer had always shared this view: in his heart, he thought Lefferts despicable. But to love Ellen Olenska was not to become a man like Lefferts; for the first time, Archer found himself face to face with the dread argument of the individual case. Ellen Olenska was like no other woman; he was like no other man; therefore, their situation resembled no one else's, and they were answerable to no tribunal but that of their own judgment.

Yes, but in ten minutes more, he would be mounting his own doorstep. There were May, and habit, and honor, and all the old decencies that he and his people had always believed in...

At his corner, he hesitated, and then walked on down Fifth Avenue.

Ahead of him, in the winter night, loomed a big unlit house. As he drew near, he thought how often he had seen it blazing with lights, its steps awninged and carpeted, and carriages waiting in double line to draw up at the curbstone. It was in the conservatory that stretched its dead-black bulk down the side street that he had taken his first kiss from May; it was under the myriad candles of the ballroom that he had seen her appear, tall and silver-shining as a young Diana.

Now the house was as dark as the grave, except for a faint flare of gas in the basement and a light in one upstairs room where the blind had not been lowered. As Archer reached the corner, he saw that the carriage standing at the door was Mrs. Manson Mingott's. What an opportunity for Sillerton Jackson, if he should chance to pass! Archer had been greatly moved by Old Catherine's account of Madame Olenska's attitude toward Mrs. Beaufort; it made the righteous reprobation of New York seem like a passing by on the other side. But he knew well enough what construction the clubs and drawing-rooms would put on Ellen Olenska's visits to her cousin.

He paused and looked up at the lighted window. No doubt the two women were sitting together in that room; Beaufort had probably sought consolation elsewhere. There were even rumors that he had left New York with Fanny Ring, but Mrs. Beaufort's attitude made the report seem improbable.

Archer had the nocturnal perspective of Fifth Avenue almost to himself. At that hour, most people were indoors, dressing for dinner; and he was secretly glad that Ellen's exit was likely to be unobserved. As the thought passed through his mind, the door opened, and she came out. Behind her was a faint light, such as might have been carried down the stairs to show her the way. She turned to say a word to someone; then the door closed, and she came down the steps.

"Ellen," he said in a low voice as she reached the pavement.

She stopped with a slight start, and just then he saw two young men of fashionable cut approaching. There was a familiar air about their overcoats and the way their smart silk mufflers were folded over their white ties, and he wondered how youths of their quality happened to be dining out so early. Then he remembered that the Reggie Chiverses, whose house was a few doors above, were taking a large party that evening to see Adelaide Neilson in *Romeo and Juliet*, and guessed that the two were of the number. They passed under a lamp, and he recognized Lawrence Lefferts and a young Chivers.

A mean desire not to have Madame Olenska seen at the Beauforts' door vanished as he felt the penetrating warmth of her hand.

"I shall see you now—we shall be together," he broke out, hardly knowing what he said.

"Ah," she answered, "Granny has told you?"

While he watched her, he was aware that Lefferts and Chivers, on reaching the farther side of the street corner, had discreetly struck away across Fifth Avenue. It was the kind of masculine solidarity that he himself often practiced; now he sickened at their connivance. Did she really imagine that he and she could live like this? And if not, what else did she imagine?

"Tomorrow I must see you—somewhere where we can be alone," he said, in a voice that sounded almost angry to his own ears.

She wavered and moved toward the carriage.

"But I shall be at Granny's—for the present that is," she added, as if conscious that her change of plans required some explanation.

"Somewhere where we can be alone," he insisted.

She gave a faint laugh that grated on him.

"In New York? But there are no churches... no monuments."

"There's the Art Museum—in the Park," he explained as she looked puzzled. "At half-past two. I shall be at the door..."

She turned away without answering and got quickly into the carriage. As it drove off, she leaned forward, and he thought she waved her hand in the obscurity. He stared after her in a turmoil of contradictory feelings. It seemed to him that he had been speaking not to the woman he loved but to another, a woman he was indebted to for pleasures already wearied of; it was hateful to find himself the prisoner of this hackneyed vocabulary.

"She'll come!" he said to himself, almost contemptuously.

Avoiding the popular "Wolfe collection," whose anecdotic canvases filled one of the main galleries of the queer wilderness of cast-iron and encaustic tiles known as the Metropolitan Museum, they had wandered down a passage to the room where the "Cesnola antiquities" mouldered in unvisited loneliness.

They had this melancholy retreat to themselves, and seated on the divan enclosing the central steam-radiator, they were staring silently at the glass cabinets mounted in ebonized wood which contained the recovered fragments of Ilium.

"It's odd," Madame Olenska said, "I never came here before."

"Ah, well—. Some day, I suppose, it will be a great Museum."

"Yes," she assented absently.

She stood up and wandered across the room. Archer, remaining seated, watched the light movements of her figure, so girlish even under its heavy furs, the cleverly planted heron wing in her fur cap, and the way a dark curl lay like a flattened vine spiral on each cheek above the ear. His mind, as always when they first met, was wholly absorbed in the delicious details that made her herself and no other. Presently he rose and approached the case before which she stood. Its glass shelves were crowded with small broken objects—hardly recognizable domestic utensils, ornaments, and personal trifles—made of glass, of clay, of discolored bronze, and other time-blurred substances.

"It seems cruel," she said, "that after a while nothing matters... any more than these little things, that used to be necessary and important to forgotten people, and now have to be guessed at under a magnifying glass and labeled: 'Use unknown.'"

"Yes; but meanwhile—"

"Ah, meanwhile—"

As she stood there, in her long sealskin coat, her hands thrust in a small round muff, her veil drawn down like a transparent mask to the tip of her nose, and the bunch of violets he had brought her stirring with her quickly-taken breath, it seemed incredible that this pure harmony of line and color should ever suffer the stupid law of change.

"Meanwhile everything matters—that concerns you," he said.

She looked at him thoughtfully and turned back to the divan. He sat down beside her and waited; but suddenly he heard a step echoing far off down the empty rooms and felt the pressure of the minutes.

"What is it you wanted to tell me?" she asked, as if she had received the same warning.

"What I wanted to tell you?" he rejoined. "Why, that I believe you came to New York because you were afraid."

"Afraid?"

"Of my coming to Washington."

She looked down at her muff, and he saw her hands stir in it uneasily.

"Well—?"

"Well—yes," she said.

"You _were_ afraid? You knew—?"

"Yes: I knew..."

"Well, then?" he insisted.

"Well, then: this is better, isn't it?" she returned with a long questioning sigh.

"Better—?"

"We shall hurt others less. Isn't it, after all, what you always wanted?"

"To have you here, you mean—in reach and yet out of reach? To meet you in this way, on the sly? It's the very reverse of what I want. I told you the other day what I wanted."

She hesitated. "And you still think this—worse?"

"A thousand times!" He paused. "It would be easy to lie to you; but the truth is I think it detestable."

"Oh, so do I!" she cried with a deep breath of relief.

He sprang up impatiently. "Well, then—it's my turn to ask: what is it, in God's name, that you think better?"

She hung her head and continued to clasp and unclasp her hands in her muff. The step drew nearer, and a guardian in a braided cap walked listlessly through the room like a ghost stalking through a necropolis. They fixed their eyes simultaneously on the case opposite them, and when the official figure had vanished down a vista of mummies and sarcophagi, Archer spoke again.

"What

"At the court of the Tuileries," Mr. Sillerton Jackson remarked, his smile curling with the nostalgia of a man recounting a distant, golden age, "such indiscretions were treated with a charming openness."

They sat in the van der Luydens' imposing black walnut dining room on Madison Avenue, the evening following Newland Archer's visit to the Museum. The hosts had arrived in town from Skuytercliff with hurried urgency, fleeing the scandal of Beaufort's financial collapse. Society, it was whispered, required their presence to stabilize; as Mrs. Archer had noted, they "owed it to society" to appear at the Opera and keep their own doors open.

"It would never do, my dear Louisa," Mrs. van der Luyden admonished, her voice sharp with the anxiety of a gatekeeper, "to allow women like Mrs. Lemuel Struthers to believe they can step into Regina's shoes. It is precisely during times of turmoil that newcomers push in and take root. Remember the chicken-pox epidemic last winter? That is how Mrs. Struthers first gained a foothold, allowing married men to slip away to her house while their wives tended the nursery. You and dear Henry, Louisa, must stand firm in the breach as you always have."

Bound by duty, the couple had reluctantly descended from their country estate, unveiling their home and issuing invitations for two dinners and a grand reception. On this specific night, they had enlisted Mr. Jackson, Mrs. Archer, and Newland with his wife to attend the premiere of *Faust*. Under the van der Luyden roof, nothing occurred without ceremony. Though only four guests graced the table, dinner commenced punctually at seven, ensuring the proper sequence of courses before the gentlemen retired to their cigars.

Newland had not seen his wife since the previous evening. He had buried himself in a mountain of trivial office work, interrupted by an unexpected call from a senior partner, arriving home so late that May had driven ahead to the van der Luydens' and sent the carriage back. Now, across the Skuytercliff carnations and heavy plates, she appeared pale and languid, yet her eyes burned with an exaggerated animation.

The topic that had sparked Mr. Jackson's favorite anecdote had been introduced, perhaps intentionally, by the hostess. The aftermath of the Beaufort failure remained a fertile ground for moralizing. After thoroughly dissecting and condemning the affair, Mrs. van der Luyden turned her scrutinizing gaze upon May Archer.

"Is it true, dear? I was told your grandmother Mingott's carriage was seen standing at Mrs. Beaufort's door." Notably, she no longer used the offending lady's first name.

May's cheeks flushed, and Mrs. Archer interjected hastily, "If it was, I am convinced it was there without Mrs. Mingott's knowledge."

"Ah, you think—?" Mrs. van der Luyden paused, sighing, and glanced at her husband.

"I fear," Mr. van der Luyden said, "that Madame Olenska's kind heart led her into the imprudence of calling on Mrs. Beaufort."

"Or her taste for peculiar individuals," Mrs. Archer added dryly, her eyes fixed innocently on her son.

"I am sorry to think it of Madame Olenska," Mrs. van der Luyden murmured. Mrs. Archer replied softly, "Ah, my dear—and after you had hosted her twice at Skuytercliff!"

It was then that Mr. Jackson seized the opportunity to deliver his allusion.

"At the Tuileries," he repeated, sensing the expectant gaze of the company, "the standards were excessively lax in certain respects. If you asked where Morny's fortune originated—or who settled the debts of certain Court beauties..."

"I hope, dear Sillerton," Mrs. Archer said, "you are not suggesting we should adopt such standards?"

"I never suggest," Mr. Jackson returned, imperturbable. "But Madame Olenska's foreign upbringing may render her less particular—"

"Ah," the two elder ladies sighed in unison.

"Still, to keep her grandmother's carriage at a defaulter's door!" Mr. van der Luyden protested, likely recalling and resenting the hampers of carnations he had sent to the small house on Twenty-third Street.

"Of course, I have always said she views things differently," Mrs. Archer summarized.

A flush rose to May's forehead. She looked across the table at her husband and said precipitously, "I am sure Ellen meant it kindly."

"Imprudent people are often kind," Mrs. Archer observed, as if the observation offered little comfort. Mrs. van der Luyden murmured, "If only she had consulted someone—"

"Ah, that she never did!" Mrs. Archer rejoined.

At this juncture, Mr. van der Luyden glanced at his wife, who bowed her head slightly toward Mrs. Archer. The shimmering trains of the three ladies swept out of the room as the gentlemen settled into their cigars. Mr. van der Luyden provided short ones for opera nights, yet they were so exquisite that his guests lamented his inexorable punctuality.

After the first act, Newland detached himself from the party and moved to the rear of the club box. From there, peering over the shoulders of various Chivers, Mingott, and Rushworth, he watched the same scene he had observed two years prior on the night of his first meeting with Ellen Olenska. He had half-expected her to appear in old Mrs. Mingott's box, but it remained empty. He sat motionless, his eyes fixed upon it, until suddenly Madame Nilsson's pure soprano broke out into "_M'ama, non m'ama..._"

Newland turned to the stage, where, amidst the familiar backdrop of giant roses and pen-wiper pansies, the same large blonde victim succumbed to the same small brown seducer. His gaze drifted to the point of the horseshoe where May sat between two older ladies, just as she had on that former evening, positioned between Mrs. Lovell Mingott and her newly arrived "foreign" cousin. As on that night, she was clad entirely in white. Newland, who had not noticed her attire earlier, recognized the blue-white satin and old lace of her wedding dress.

In old New York, it was customary for brides to wear this costly garment during the first year or two of marriage. His mother, he knew, kept hers in tissue paper, hoping Janey might one day wear it, though poor Janey was reaching an age when pearl grey poplin and no bridesmaids would be deemed more appropriate.

It struck Newland that May, since their return from Europe, had seldom worn her bridal satin. The sight of her in it prompted him to compare her appearance with the young girl he had watched with such blissful anticipation two years earlier. Though May's outline was slightly heavier, as her goddesslike build had predicted, her athletic erectness of carriage and the girlish transparency of her expression remained unchanged. Save for the slight languor he had recently noticed, she was the exact image of the girl playing with the bouquet of lilies-of-the-valley on her betrothal evening. This fact seemed an additional appeal to his pity; such innocence was as moving as the trustful clasp of a child. Then he remembered the passionate generosity latent beneath her incurious calm. He recalled her look of understanding when he had urged that their engagement be announced at the Beaufort ball; he heard the voice in which she had said in the Mission garden, "I couldn't have my happiness made out of a wrong—a wrong to some one else." An uncontrollable longing seized him to tell her the truth, to throw himself upon her generosity, and ask for the freedom he had once refused.

Newland Archer was a quiet, self-controlled young man. Conformity to the discipline of a small society had become almost his second nature. It was deeply distasteful to him to do anything melodramatic or conspicuous, anything Mr. van der Luyden would have deprecated and the club box condemned as bad form. Yet, he had suddenly become unconscious of the box, of Mr. van der Luyden, of everything that had so long enclosed him in the warm shelter of habit. He walked along the semi-circular passage at the back of the house and opened the door of Mrs. van der Luyden's box as if it were a gate into the unknown.

"_M'ama!_" thrilled out the triumphant Marguerite, and the occupants of the box looked up in surprise at Archer's entrance. He had already broken one of the rules of his world, which forbade entering a box during a solo.

Slipping between Mr. van der Luyden and Sillerton Jackson, he leaned over his wife.

"I've got a beastly headache; don't tell anyone, but come home, won't you?" he whispered.

May gave him a glance of comprehension. He saw her whisper to his mother, who nodded sympathetically; then she murmured an excuse to Mrs. van der Luyden and rose from her seat just as Marguerite fell into Faust's arms. Archer, while helping her on with her opera cloak, noticed the exchange of a significant smile between the older ladies.

As they drove away, May laid her hand shyly on his. "I'm so sorry you don't feel well. I'm afraid they've been overworking you again at the office."

"No—it's not that: do you mind if I open the window?" he returned confusedly, lowering the pane on his side. He sat staring out into the street, feeling his wife beside him as a silent, watchful interrogation, keeping his eyes steadily fixed on the passing houses. At their door, she caught her skirt in the step of the carriage and fell against him.

"Did you hurt yourself?" he asked, steadying her with his arm.

"No; but my poor dress—see how I've torn it!" she exclaimed. She bent to gather up a mud-stained breadth and followed him up the steps into the hall. The servants had not expected them so early, and there was only a glimmer of gas on the upper landing.

Archer mounted the stairs, turned up the light, and lit the brackets on each side of the library mantelpiece. The curtains were drawn, and the warm, friendly aspect of the room smote him like that of a familiar face met during an unavowable errand. He noticed that his wife was very pale and asked if he should get her some brandy.

"Oh, no," she exclaimed with a momentary flush as she removed her cloak. "But hadn't you better go to bed at once?" she added as he opened a silver box on the table and took out a cigarette.

Archer threw down the cigarette and walked to his usual place by the fire.

"No; my head is not as bad as that." He paused. "And there's something I want to say; something important—that I must tell you at once."

She had dropped into an armchair and raised her head as he spoke. "Yes, dear?" she rejoined, so gently that he wondered at the lack of wonder with which she received this preamble.

"May—" he began, standing a few feet from her chair and looking over at her as if the slight distance between them were an unbridgeable abyss. The sound of his voice echoed uncannily through the homelike hush, and he repeated, "There is something I've got to tell you... about myself..."

She sat silent, without a movement or a tremor of her lashes. She was still extremely pale, but her face possessed a curious tranquility of expression that seemed drawn from some secret inner source.

Archer checked the conventional phrases of self-accusal crowding to his lips. He was determined to state the case baldly, without vain recrimination or excuse.

"Madame Olenska—" he said; but at the name, his wife raised her hand as if to silence him. As she did so, the gaslight struck the gold of her wedding ring.

"Oh, why should we talk about Ellen tonight?" she asked, with a slight pout of impatience.

"Because I ought to have spoken before."

Her face remained calm. "Is it really worth while, dear? I know I've been unfair to her at times—perhaps we all have. You've understood her, no doubt, better than we did; you've always been kind to her. But what does it matter, now it's all over?"

Archer looked at her blankly. Could it be possible that the sense of unreality in which he felt imprisoned had communicated itself to his wife?

"All over—what do you mean?" he asked in an indistinct stammer.

May still looked at him with transparent eyes. "Why—since she's going back to Europe so soon; since Granny approves and understands, and has arranged to make her independent of her husband—"

She broke off, and Archer, grasping the corner of the mantelpiece in one convulsed hand and steadying himself against it, made a vain effort to extend the same control to his reeling thoughts.

"I supposed," he heard his wife's even voice continue, "that you had been kept at the office this evening about the business arrangements. It was settled this morning, I believe." She lowered her eyes under his unseeing stare, and another fugitive flush passed over her face.

He understood that his own eyes must be unbearable. Turning away, he rested his elbows on the mantel-shelf and covered his face. Something drummed and clanged furiously in his ears; he could not tell if it were the blood in his veins or the tick of the clock on the mantel.

May sat without moving or speaking while the clock slowly measured out five minutes. A lump of coal fell forward in the grate, and hearing her rise to push it back, Archer at length turned and faced her.

"It's impossible," he exclaimed.

"Impossible—?"

"How do you know—what you've just told me?"

"I saw Ellen yesterday—I told you I'd seen her at Granny's."

"It wasn't then that she told you?"

"No; I had a note from her this afternoon.—Do you want to see it?"

He could not find his voice, and she went out of the room and came back almost immediately.

"I thought

It was, as Mrs. Archer smiled to Mrs. Welland, a grand occasion for a young couple to host their inaugural formal dinner.

Since establishing their household, the Newland Archers had entertained frequently in an informal capacity. Archer enjoyed dining with three or four friends, and May welcomed them with the radiant enthusiasm her mother had taught her for marital duties. Her husband often wondered if, left entirely to her own inclinations, she would ever have invited anyone to their home; yet he had long ceased attempting to separate her true self from the persona shaped by tradition and upbringing. In New York, it was expected that affluent young couples engage in significant informal entertaining, and a Welland married to an Archer carried a double pledge to uphold this custom.

However, a grand dinner, complete with a hired chef, borrowed footmen, Roman punch, roses from Henderson's, and menus printed on gilt-edged cards, was a distinct undertaking, not to be approached lightly. As Mrs. Archer noted, the Roman punch made all the difference—not merely in taste, but in its manifold implications: it signaled canvas-back ducks or terrapin, two soups, both hot and cold sweets, low-cut dresses with short sleeves, and guests of corresponding stature.

Such occasions were always intriguing when a young pair issued their first invitations in the third person, and their summons was rarely declined, even by the most sought-after. Still, it was acknowledged as a triumph that the van der Luydens, at May's request, had stayed over specifically to attend her farewell dinner for the Countess Olenska.

On the afternoon of the great day, the two mothers-in-law occupied May's drawing-room. Mrs. Archer meticulously wrote out the menus on Tiffany's thickest gilt-edged Bristol board, while Mrs. Welland supervised the placement of palms and standard lamps.

Archer arrived late from his office to find them still there. Mrs. Archer had turned her attention to the name-cards for the table, and Mrs. Welland was deliberating the effect of moving the large gilt sofa forward to create another "corner" between the piano and the window.

May, they informed him, was in the dining-room inspecting the mound of Jacqueminot roses and maidenhair fern in the center of the long table, arranging the Maillard bonbons in openwork silver baskets between the candelabra. On the piano rested a large basket of orchids sent from Skuytercliff by Mr. van der Luyden. In short, everything was precisely as it should be for such a considerable event.

Mrs. Archer ran her sharp gold pen thoughtfully over the list, checking off each name.

"Henry van der Luyden—Louisa—the Lovell Mingotts—the Reggie Chiverses—Lawrence Lefferts and Gertrude—(yes, I suppose May was right to include them)—the Selfridge Merrys, Sillerton Jackson, Van Newland and his wife. (How time flies! It seems only yesterday he was your best man, Newland)—and Countess Olenska—yes, I believe that's all...."

Mrs. Welland surveyed her son-in-law with affection. "No one can say, Newland, that you and May are not giving Ellen a handsome send-off."

"Ah, well," said Mrs. Archer, "I understand May's wanting her cousin to assure people abroad that we're not quite barbarians."

"I'm sure Ellen will appreciate it. She was due to arrive this morning, I believe. It will make a most charming final impression. The evening before sailing is usually so dreary," Mrs. Welland continued cheerfully.

Archer turned toward the door, and his mother-in-law called out: "Do go in and have a look at the table. And don't let May tire herself too much." But he feigned not to hear and sprang up the stairs to his library. The room stared back at him like an alien countenance composed into a polite grimace; he perceived it had been ruthlessly "tidied" and prepared, through a judicious distribution of ash-trays and cedar-wood boxes, for the gentlemen to smoke in.

"Ah, well," he thought, "it's not for long"—and he proceeded to his dressing-room.

Ten days had passed since Madame Olenska's departure from New York. During those ten days, Archer had received no sign from her save for the return of a key wrapped in tissue paper, sent to his office in a sealed envelope addressed in her hand. This retort to his last appeal might have been interpreted as a classic move in a familiar game; but the young man chose to assign it a different meaning. She was still fighting her fate; she was going to Europe, and she was not returning to her husband. Therefore, nothing prevented him from following her. Once he took the irrevocable step and proved to her that it was irrevocable, he believed she would not send him away.

This confidence in the future steadied him to play his part in the present. It kept him from writing to her or betraying, by any sign or act, his misery and mortification. It seemed to him that in the deadly silent game between them, the trumps remained in his hands; and he waited.

Nevertheless, there had been moments sufficiently difficult to endure. When Mr. Letterblair summoned him the day after Madame Olenska's departure to review the details of the trust Mrs. Manson Mingott wished to create for her granddaughter, Archer spent two hours examining the terms with his senior. Throughout, he felt obscurely that he was being consulted for reasons other than mere cousinship, and that the conclusion of the conference would reveal it.

"Well, the lady can't deny that it's a handsome arrangement," Mr. Letterblair summarized after mumbling over the settlement. "In fact, I'm bound to say she's been treated pretty handsomely all round."

"All round?" Archer echoed with a touch of derision. "Do you refer to her husband's proposal to give her back her own money?"

Mr. Letterblair's bushy eyebrows rose a fraction of an inch. "My dear sir, the law's the law; and your wife's cousin was married under the French law. It's to be presumed she knew what that meant."

"Even if she did, what happened subsequently..." Archer paused. Mr. Letterblair had laid his pen-handle against his big corrugated nose and was looking down it with the expression assumed by virtuous elderly gentlemen when they wish their juniors to understand that virtue is not synonymous with ignorance.

"My dear sir, I've no wish to extenuate the Count's transgressions; but—but on the other side... I wouldn't put my hand in the fire... well, that there hadn't been tit for tat... with the young champion...." Mr. Letterblair unlocked a drawer and pushed a folded paper toward Archer. "This report, the result of discreet enquiries..." Then, as Archer made no effort to glance at the paper or repudiate the suggestion, the lawyer continued somewhat flatly: "I don't say it's conclusive, you observe; far from it. But straws show... and on the whole, it's eminently satisfactory for all parties that this dignified solution has been reached."
